Exam tip: If a question mentions pods failing to start due to "insufficient capabilities" or "PSA Restricted," the answer is likely istio-cni. It's the standard fix for clusters that prohibit NET_ADMIN in pod security policies.

Install/Config Q16 of 200
How do you exclude a specific pod from sidecar injection even when its namespace is labeled istio-injection=enabled?
A) Add the annotation sidecar.istio.io/inject: "false" to the pod template. This overrides the namespace label and prevents injection for that specific pod only
B) Remove the istio-injection=enabled label from the namespace — this is the only way to prevent injection
C) Set automountServiceAccountToken: false on the pod — this prevents sidecar injection
D) Exclude the pod by adding it to an exclusion list in the istiod ConfigMap — pod-level annotations cannot override namespace labels
✅ Correct Answer: A

The injection webhook checks annotations in this order: pod annotation (highest priority) → namespace label → global policy. Setting sidecar.istio.io/inject: "false" on a pod template overrides everything and prevents injection. This is commonly used for Jobs, init-only pods, or pods that need to start before the mesh is ready.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Sidecar Injection Precedence — Pod Overrides Namespace
  INJECTION DECISION FLOW (by the MutatingWebhook):

  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ① Pod annotation: sidecar.istio.io/inject = "false"?           │
  │     ├─ YES → NO injection (pod-level override wins!)            │
  │     └─ NO or "true" → continue to ②                             │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ② Namespace label: istio-injection = enabled?                  │
  │     ├─ YES → INJECT sidecar                                     │
  │     └─ NO → continue to ③                                      │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ③ Global mesh policy (meshConfig)                               │
  │     └─ Check default injection policy                            │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  EXAMPLE — Excluding anihpj-db from mesh:
  apiVersion: apps/v1
  kind: StatefulSet
  metadata:
    name: anihpj-db
    namespace: anihpj  ← labeled istio-injection=enabled
  spec:
    template:
      metadata:
        annotations:
          sidecar.istio.io/inject: "false"  ← This pod WON'T get sidecar
      spec:
        containers:
        - name: postgres
          image: postgres:15

For anihpj/jobpost, you might exclude the PostgreSQL pod from the mesh if you want database traffic to bypass Envoy (lower latency, less resource overhead for DB connections). But remember: without the sidecar, mTLS and metrics won't apply to DB traffic.

Install/Config Q17 of 200
What happens when you run istioctl uninstall --purge?
A) It only removes the istiod deployment — CRDs, gateways, and sidecars remain intact
B) It removes ALL Istio components but preserves your application pods and their injected sidecars — pods continue running with Envoy
C) It removes ALL Istio components (istiod, gateways, CRDs) AND the --purge flag also removes ALL Istio CRDs. However, existing pods with injected sidecars continue running until restarted — the Envoy sidecar container is already part of the pod spec. To fully remove the mesh, you must also restart all application pods
D) It deletes the entire Kubernetes cluster — istioctl uninstall is irreversible and destroys everything
✅ Correct Answer: C

istioctl uninstall removes Istio control plane components but existing pods retain their injected sidecars (they were mutated during pod creation and the sidecar is baked into their spec). --purge also removes CRDs. To fully de-mesh, you must restart all application pods after uninstalling.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Uninstalling Istio — What Gets Removed vs What Survives
  istioctl uninstall --purge

  ✅ REMOVED IMMEDIATELY:
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  • istiod Deployment + Service                                  │
  │  • Ingress/Egress Gateway Deployments + Services                │
  │  • All Istio CRDs (VirtualService, DestinationRule, etc.)       │
  │  • MutatingWebhookConfiguration (no more injection!)            │
  │  • istio-system namespace resources                              │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  ⚠️ SURVIVES (until pod restart):
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  • EXISTING pods still have istio-proxy container               │
  │  • Envoy sidecars still TRY to connect to istiod (will fail)    │
  │  • Pod shows 2/2 but istio-proxy logs errors                    │
  │  • Traffic may be disrupted (Envoy has stale config)            │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  FULL CLEANUP (production):
  $ istioctl uninstall --purge
  $ kubectl label namespace anihpj istio-injection-
  $ kubectl rollout restart deploy -n anihpj   ← Restart all apps!
  $ kubectl delete ns istio-system

For anihpj/jobpost, uninstalling Istio requires restarting all application pods afterward. The injected istio-proxy container is baked into each pod spec and survives istiod removal. Run istioctl uninstall --purge, then kubectl rollout restart deploy -n anihpj to fully de-mesh the job board.

Exam tip: The most commonly missed step is restarting application pods after uninstalling. The question may show "pods still have Envoy sidecars after uninstall" — the answer is to restart them, not to reinstall Istio.

Install/Config Q18 of 200
What happens during an in-place upgrade of Istio using istioctl upgrade?
A) In-place upgrade replaces the existing control plane with the new version immediately. All Envoy sidecars continue running with their current config until pods are restarted. This is faster than canary but riskier — there's no parallel old control plane to roll back to
B) In-place upgrade and canary upgrade are identical — they both run two control planes simultaneously
C) In-place upgrade is the ONLY supported upgrade method — canary upgrades were deprecated in Istio 1.20
D) In-place upgrade requires deleting the entire mesh and reinstalling — there is no way to upgrade without downtime
✅ Correct Answer: A

In-place upgrade (istioctl upgrade) is simple but risky — it replaces the control plane immediately. Existing sidecars keep working on old config. For production, canary upgrade (two revisions side-by-side) is recommended because you can instantly roll back by switching namespace labels.

📝 Detailed Explanation
In-Place vs Canary Upgrade — Risk Comparison
  IN-PLACE: istioctl upgrade              CANARY: Two revisions
  ┌────────────────────────┐             ┌────────────────────────┐
  │ istiod v1.22            │             │ istiod-stable (v1.22)  │
  │     ↓ replace            │             │ istiod-canary (v1.23)  │
  │ istiod v1.23            │             │                         │
  │                          │             │ kubectl label ns test  │
  │ ⚠️  No rollback          │             │   istio.io/rev=canary  │
  │ ⚠️  All or nothing       │             │ ✅ Instant rollback    │
  └────────────────────────┘             │ ✅ Gradual migration   │
                                          └────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, prefer canary upgrades over in-place upgrades. Deploy the new istiod revision alongside the existing one, label the anihpj-test namespace with the new revision, verify, then migrate anihpj production. If issues arise, switch the label back -- instant rollback to the old control plane with zero downtime for the job board.

Install/Config Q19 of 200
How do you verify that all Envoy sidecars have been updated to the new Istio version after an upgrade?
A) Check kubectl get pods --all-namespaces — if all pods are Running, the upgrade is complete
B) Run istioctl version — it always shows the version of all sidecars in the mesh
C) There is no way to check sidecar versions — you must trust that the upgrade completed successfully
D) Use istioctl proxy-status to see all connected Envoys and their sync status. Check the proxy version with istioctl proxy-config bootstrap <pod> -n <ns> | grep ISTIO_VERSION. Sidecars only update when pods are restarted — running kubectl rollout restart deploy is required to pick up the new sidecar image
✅ Correct Answer: D

Upgrading the control plane does NOT upgrade existing sidecars — they keep running the old Envoy version until their pods restart. istioctl proxy-status shows which version each Envoy is running. A gradual kubectl rollout restart is needed to complete the upgrade.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Sidecar Version vs Control Plane Version After Upgrade
  AFTER istioctl upgrade (control plane only):
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  istiod: v1.23 ✅ (upgraded)                                    │
  │                                                                  │
  │  anihpj-web sidecar: v1.22 ⚠️ (OLD — pod not restarted yet)    │
  │  anihpj-api sidecar: v1.22 ⚠️ (OLD)                            │
  │  anihpj-db sidecar:  v1.22 ⚠️ (OLD)                            │
  │                                                                  │
  │  FIX: kubectl rollout restart deploy -n anihpj                  │
  │  → New pods start with v1.23 sidecar ✅                         │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, after upgrading the control plane, run istioctl proxy-status to verify all anihpj-web, anihpj-api, and anihpj-db Envoys show SYNCED. Then kubectl rollout restart deploy -n anihpj to update sidecars. Check proxy-status again -- all proxies should show the new ISTIO_VERSION.

Install/Config Q20 of 200
After upgrading the Istio control plane, some namespaces still have the OLD revision label (istio.io/rev=stable) while you want them on the new revision. What is the safest way to migrate?
A) Immediately change ALL namespace labels to the new revision — all pods will auto-restart with new sidecars
B) Migrate namespaces ONE AT A TIME: label a test namespace with the new revision, restart its workloads, verify health, then proceed to production namespaces. This limits the blast radius if the new version has issues
C) Delete the old control plane first — then all namespaces will automatically pick up the new revision
D) Namespace labels are irrelevant — sidecars update automatically when the control plane is upgraded
✅ Correct Answer: B

Canary revision migration should be gradual: test namespace first → staging → production. Each migration requires labeling the namespace AND restarting pods. If the test namespace shows issues, you haven't affected production. This is the whole point of canary upgrades.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Canary Migration — Gradual Namespace-by-Namespace Rollout
  STEP 1: Test namespace first           STEP 2: Staging next
  ┌────────────────────────┐            ┌────────────────────────┐
  │ kubectl label ns test  │            │ kubectl label ns stage │
  │   istio.io/rev=canary  │            │   istio.io/rev=canary  │
  │ kubectl rollout restart│            │ kubectl rollout restart│
  │   deploy -n test       │            │   deploy -n stage      │
  │                         │            │                         │
  │ ✅ Test OK? → Continue  │            │ ✅ Stage OK? → Continue │
  │ ❌ Issues? → Rollback   │            │ ❌ Issues? → Rollback   │
  └────────────────────────┘            └────────────────────────┘

  STEP 3: Production                     ROLLBACK (anytime):
  ┌────────────────────────┐            ┌────────────────────────┐
  │ kubectl label ns anihpj│            │ kubectl label ns anihpj│
  │   istio.io/rev=canary  │            │   istio.io/rev=stable  │
  │ kubectl rollout restart│            │   --overwrite          │
  │   deploy -n anihpj     │            │ kubectl rollout restart│
  │                         │            │   deploy -n anihpj     │
  │ ✅ Production migrated! │            │ ✅ Instant rollback!   │
  └────────────────────────┘            └────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, migrate namespaces to the new Istio revision one at a time: first anihpj-test, then anihpj-staging, finally anihpj production. Each migration requires labeling the namespace AND restarting pods. If the test namespace shows issues, the production namespace is unaffected -- this is the safety of canary revision upgrades.

Install/Config Q21 of 200
What does istioctl analyze do and when should you run it?
A) It deploys Istio to your cluster — it's an alias for istioctl install
B) It analyzes cluster performance and generates optimization recommendations
C) It performs static analysis of your Istio configuration (VirtualServices, DestinationRules, Gateways, etc.) and reports misconfigurations, conflicts, and deprecated API usage BEFORE you apply them. Run it before kubectl apply to catch errors early
D) It monitors runtime traffic and alerts on anomalies — it's a monitoring tool
✅ Correct Answer: C

istioctl analyze is your pre-flight checker. It validates Istio CRDs against known best practices and catches issues like VirtualService host mismatches, missing DestinationRule subsets, and deprecated field usage — before they cause production outages.

📝 Detailed Explanation
istioctl analyze — Catch Errors Before They Reach Production
  $ istioctl analyze -n anihpj

  ✅ Info [IST0102] The namespace is not enabled for injection.
  ⚠️  Warning [IST0108] VirtualService anihpj-api-vs: destination host
      "anihpj-api-wrong" not found (did you mean "anihpj-api"?)
  ❌ Error [IST0113] DestinationRule anihpj-api-dr: subset "v2"
      has no matching pods (label "version: v2" not found on any pod)

  Run BEFORE kubectl apply — saves hours of debugging!

For anihpj/jobpost, always run istioctl analyze -n anihpj before applying any mesh configuration changes. It catches VirtualService host mismatches, missing DestinationRule subsets, and deprecated API usage before they cause production outages. Make it part of your CI/CD pipeline for mesh configuration changes.

1.3 Upgrading Istio — Canary, In-Place & RollbackQ22-Q40

Install/Config Q22 of 200
What is the purpose of istioctl proxy-status and what does each sync status (SYNCED, STALE, NOT SENT) mean?
A) It shows the sync state between istiod and EVERY Envoy sidecar in the mesh. SYNCED = Envoy has the latest config from istiod. STALE = Envoy hasn't acknowledged config updates (possible network issue). NOT SENT = istiod hasn't pushed config to this Envoy yet (proxy just started or istiod is overloaded). This is the #1 command for verifying mesh health
B) It shows the CPU and memory usage of each Envoy sidecar — SYNCED means healthy resource usage, STALE means memory leak
C) It's a replacement for kubectl get pods — it shows pod status with Istio-specific columns
D) It shows the version of each Envoy sidecar — SYNCED means running the latest version
✅ Correct Answer: A

istioctl proxy-status is the mesh health dashboard. Every Envoy maintains a bidirectional gRPC stream with istiod for xDS updates. This command shows the state of each stream. If proxies show STALE or NOT SENT, you have a control plane communication problem.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Proxy Sync States — Reading the Mesh Health Dashboard
  $ istioctl proxy-status
  NAME                  CDS     LDS     EDS     RDS     ISTIOD
  anihpj-web-xxx.anihpj SYNCED  SYNCED  SYNCED  SYNCED  v1.22
  anihpj-api-yyy.anihpj SYNCED  SYNCED  SYNCED  SYNCED  v1.22
  anihpj-db-0.anihpj    STALE   STALE   STALE   STALE   v1.22 ⚠️

  CDS=Cluster, LDS=Listener, EDS=Endpoint, RDS=Route (xDS types)
  STALE → Network issue between istiod and this Envoy — INVESTIGATE!

For anihpj/jobpost, istioctl proxy-status is your first troubleshooting command. If anihpj-api shows STALE, its Envoy has lost sync with istiod -- check network connectivity. If NOT SENT, the proxy just started or istiod is overloaded. SYNCED across all anihpj pods means the mesh data plane is healthy.

Install/Config Q23 of 200
You deployed a canary Istio revision. How do you migrate the anihpj namespace to the canary revision?
A) Run istioctl upgrade — it automatically migrates all namespaces
B) Label istio.io/rev=canary on the namespace and restart pods. New pods get the canary sidecar. Old control plane stays as safety net for instant rollback
C) Delete the stable control plane first — the namespace automatically switches
D) Run istioctl migrate-ns anihpj --to=canary
✅ Correct Answer: B

Canary migration: label namespace + restart pods. Rollback: switch label back. Both control planes run simultaneously during migration — this is the key advantage over in-place upgrades.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Canary Migration — Label-Based Traffic Cutover
  kubectl label ns anihpj istio.io/rev=canary --overwrite
  kubectl rollout restart deploy -n anihpj

  BEFORE:                              AFTER:
  ┌──────────────────────┐             ┌──────────────────────┐
  │ istiod-stable (v1.22)│             │ istiod-stable (v1.22)│
  │  ↓ injects sidecar    │             │  (still running!)    │
  │ anihpj-web (v1.22)   │             │                       │
  │ anihpj-api (v1.22)   │             │ istiod-canary (v1.23)│
  └──────────────────────┘             │  ↓ injects sidecar    │
                                       │ anihpj-web (v1.23)   │
  ROLLBACK:                            │ anihpj-api (v1.23)   │
  kubectl label ns anihpj              └──────────────────────┘
    istio.io/rev=stable --overwrite
  kubectl rollout restart deploy -n anihpj
  → Pods switch back to v1.22 sidecars. Zero downtime.

For anihpj/jobpost, canary revision migration: label the anihpj namespace with istio.io/rev=canary, restart pods, and verify. Both old and new istiod run simultaneously -- if the canary revision has a bug, switch the label back to stable and restart pods. Zero-downtime rollback for the entire job board mesh.

Install/Config Q24 of 200
What happens if you skip a minor version during Istio upgrade (e.g., v1.20 → v1.22)?
A) It works fine — Istio supports any version skip
B) CRDs from skipped versions are permanently lost
C) istioctl handles it automatically through intermediate versions
D) Istio only supports upgrading ONE minor version at a time. Skipping versions risks CRD incompatibility and a broken mesh. Always upgrade sequentially
✅ Correct Answer: D

Istio ONLY supports upgrading one minor version at a time. Skipping versions risks CRD incompatibility — the new istiod expects CRD versions from the skipped release that don't exist in your cluster. Always upgrade sequentially.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Sequential Upgrades — Never Skip Minor Versions
  ✅ CORRECT PATH (sequential):
  v1.20 ──► v1.21 ──► v1.22 ──► v1.23
  (each step: istioctl upgrade, verify, restart workloads)

  ❌ WRONG PATH (skip):
  v1.20 ──────────────► v1.22
  ⚠️  CRDs from v1.21 missing — new istiod expects them
  ⚠️  API version mismatch — v1.22 may have deprecated v1.20 APIs
  ⚠️  Mesh may become unstable or non-functional

  CHECK SUPPORTED PATH:
  istioctl x precheck  ← validates version compatibility

For anihpj/jobpost, never skip minor versions when upgrading Istio. Always upgrade sequentially (v1.21, v1.22, v1.23). Skipping versions risks CRD incompatibility where the new istiod expects CRD versions from the skipped release that do not exist in your cluster -- potentially breaking the mesh.

Install/Config Q25 of 200
How do you roll back a failed Istio canary upgrade?
A) Switch namespace label back to stable revision and restart pods. The old control plane is still running — instant rollback with zero downtime
B) Run istioctl rollback
C) Delete mesh and reinstall — no safe rollback exists
D) Rollback is automatic within 5 minutes
✅ Correct Answer: A

Canary rollback is the #1 advantage over in-place upgrades — the old control plane is STILL RUNNING. Rollback is instant: change the namespace label back and restart pods. Sidecars reconnect to the stable istiod. Zero downtime.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Instant Canary Rollback — Old Control Plane Never Stopped
  BEFORE ROLLBACK:                       AFTER ROLLBACK (instant!):
  ┌──────────────────────────┐          ┌──────────────────────────┐
  │ istiod-stable (v1.22)   │          │ istiod-stable (v1.22)   │
  │   (still running!)       │          │   ◄── sidecars reconnect │
  │                           │          │                           │
  │ istiod-canary (v1.23)   │          │ istiod-canary (v1.23)   │
  │   ↓ sidecars connected   │          │   (still running too!)   │
  │ anihpj-web (v1.23)      │          │                           │
  │ anihpj-api (v1.23)      │          │ anihpj-web (v1.22) ← NEW │
  └──────────────────────────┘          │ anihpj-api (v1.22) ← NEW │
                                         └──────────────────────────┘
  COMMAND:
  kubectl label ns anihpj istio.io/rev=stable --overwrite
  kubectl rollout restart deploy -n anihpj

For anihpj/jobpost, canary rollback is instant and safe because the OLD control plane never stops running. If the canary revision causes issues, relabel the anihpj namespace back to istio.io/rev=stable, restart pods, and all sidecars reconnect to the proven stable istiod. No downtime, no data loss, no panic.

Install/Config Q27 of 200
How often does Istio automatically rotate workload mTLS certificates?
A) Certificates never expire — they are valid for the lifetime of the pod
B) By default, every 24 hours. istiod's CA issues short-lived certificates that are automatically rotated. Envoy sidecars fetch new certificates via SDS (Secret Discovery Service) before the old ones expire — no pod restart needed
C) Every 7 days — certificates are valid for one week
D) Only when you manually run istioctl certs rotate
✅ Correct Answer: B

Istio's CA issues 24-hour certificates by default. This short lifetime limits the blast radius if a certificate is compromised. Automatic rotation happens via SDS — Envoy receives new certificates without restarting.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Automatic mTLS Certificate Rotation — Every 24 Hours
  CERTIFICATE LIFECYCLE IN ISTIO:
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  istiod CA (Citadel)                                            │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ Root CA → Intermediate CA → Workload Cert (24h lifetime) │  │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                  │
  │  T+0h:  Envoy receives new cert via SDS (gRPC stream)           │
  │  T+12h: Envoy requests new cert (50% of lifetime)               │
  │  T+23h: New cert issued, old cert still valid (1h overlap)      │
  │  T+24h: Old cert expires, Envoy already using new cert ✅       │
  │                                                                  │
  │  CHECK CERT EXPIRY:                                              │
  │  istioctl proxy-config secret <pod> -n <ns> -o json | \       │
  │    jq '.dynamicActiveSecrets[].secret.tlsCertificate.\          │
  │    certificateChain.inlineBytes' | base64 -d | \                │
  │    openssl x509 -noout -dates                                    │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, mTLS certificates rotate automatically every 24 hours. Each Envoy sidecar (anihpj-web, anihpj-api, anihpj-db) receives a new certificate via SDS before the old one expires. No pod restarts, no application code changes, no manual intervention -- the mesh handles certificate lifecycle transparently.

Install/Config Q28 of 200
What command shows when your Istio certificates will expire?
A) istioctl proxy-config secret <pod> -n <ns> -o json | jq '.dynamicActiveSecrets[].secret.tlsCertificate.certificateChain.inlineBytes' | base64 -d | openssl x509 -noout -dates — this extracts the certificate from Envoy and checks its validity period
B) kubectl get certs --all-namespaces
C) istioctl certs check-expiry
D) Certificates don't expire in Istio
✅ Correct Answer: A

Envoy's workload certificate is stored in memory and accessible via istioctl proxy-config secret. Use openssl x509 to decode and check Not After dates. Essential for debugging mTLS issues and preventing cert expiry outages.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Checking Certificate Expiry — The mTLS Health Check
  # Extract cert from Envoy and check expiry:
  $ istioctl proxy-config secret anihpj-api-xxx -n anihpj -o json | \
      jq -r '.dynamicActiveSecrets[0].secret.tlsCertificate.\
      certificateChain.inlineBytes' | base64 -d | \
      openssl x509 -noout -dates -subject

  notBefore=Jun 11 10:00:00 2026 GMT
  notAfter=Jun 12 10:00:00 2026 GMT          ← 24h lifetime
  subject=spiffe://cluster.local/ns/anihpj/sa/anihpj-api

  ALL ENVOYS IN NAMESPACE:
  for pod in $(kubectl get pods -n anihpj -o name); do
    echo "=== $pod ==="
    istioctl proxy-config secret $pod -n anihpj -o json | \
      jq -r '..|.inlineBytes?|select(.)' | base64 -d | \
      openssl x509 -noout -dates 2>/dev/null
  done

For anihpj/jobpost, check certificate expiry on any Envoy with istioctl proxy-config secret piped to openssl x509. The certificate's Not After field shows exactly when it expires. Monitor this across all anihpj pods to ensure automatic rotation is working and no certificates are approaching expiry.

MeshConfig is the global mesh configuration ConfigMap (istio-system/istio). It controls: default tracing sampling, access logging, outbound traffic policy (ALLOW_ANY vs REGISTRY_ONLY), default proxy resources, and mTLS defaults. Changes apply to new sidecars; existing ones need restart.

📝 Detailed Explanation
MeshConfig — Global Mesh Settings
  kubectl get configmap istio -n istio-system -o yaml
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  mesh:                                                          │
  │    defaultConfig:             ← Default proxy settings           │

  │      proxyMetadata: {}                                          │

For anihpj/jobpost, MeshConfig in the istio-system/istio ConfigMap controls global mesh behavior. Changes apply to new sidecars immediately; existing pods need restart.

Install/Config Q30 of 200
What does meshConfig.outboundTrafficPolicy.mode: REGISTRY_ONLY do?
A) It blocks ALL outbound traffic to external services UNLESS a ServiceEntry exists for that host. This is a security best practice — pods can only reach external services explicitly registered in Istio's service registry. Without it (ALLOW_ANY), pods can reach any external host freely
B) It blocks all traffic including internal mesh traffic — nothing works
C) It only allows traffic to *.svc.cluster.local hosts — no external traffic at all, ever
D) It's the default mode — REGISTRY_ONLY is how Istio ships out of the box
✅ Correct Answer: A

REGISTRY_ONLY implements zero-trust egress. Pods can ONLY reach external services that have a ServiceEntry. Without one — connection blocked. The default ALLOW_ANY lets pods reach any external host freely. For production security, switch to REGISTRY_ONLY and create ServiceEntries for approved external dependencies like Stripe and Mailgun.

📝 Detailed Explanation
ALLOW_ANY vs REGISTRY_ONLY — Egress Security Modes
  ALLOW_ANY (default):                    REGISTRY_ONLY (hardened):
  ┌──────────────────────┐               ┌──────────────────────────┐
  │ anihpj-api → ANY URL │               │ anihpj-api → api.stripe  │
  │ ✅ api.stripe.com    │               │   ✅ (has ServiceEntry)  │
  │ ✅ smtp.mailgun.net  │               │   ✅ (has ServiceEntry)  │
  │ ✅ evil-site.xyz     │               │   ❌ BLOCKED (no entry!) │
  │ ⚠️  No control       │               │ 🔒 Zero-trust egress     │
  └──────────────────────┘               └──────────────────────────┘

  CONFIG: kubectl edit cm istio -n istio-system
  meshConfig.outboundTrafficPolicy.mode: REGISTRY_ONLY

For anihpj/jobpost, switching to REGISTRY_ONLY is the single most impactful security hardening step. After the change, the Django API can only reach api.stripe.com and smtp.mailgun.net (registered via ServiceEntry). Any other external host is blocked at the Envoy level -- implementing zero-trust egress without firewall rules.

Install/Config Q31 of 200
What is the EnvoyFilter CRD and when should you use it?
A) EnvoyFilter is the primary way to configure routing — it replaces VirtualService
B) EnvoyFilter is for beginners; advanced users should never use it
C) EnvoyFilter only works on ingress gateways, not sidecars
D) EnvoyFilter provides direct access to Envoy's filter chain configuration — it lets you modify Envoy's behavior in ways that standard Istio CRDs don't support: custom rate limiting, custom WASM plugins, Lua scripts, and advanced HTTP filter manipulation. Use it sparingly — it bypasses Istio's abstraction and can break mesh functionality if misconfigured
✅ Correct Answer: D

EnvoyFilter is the mesh escape hatch — direct access to Envoy's filter chain. Use when Istio CRDs can't express your needs (custom rate limiting, Lua scripting). But it tightly couples config to Envoy internals — use sparingly.

📝 Detailed Explanation
EnvoyFilter — The Escape Hatch (Use Sparingly)
  STANDARD PATH (preferred):              ESCAPE HATCH (last resort):
  VirtualService → DestinationRule        EnvoyFilter → raw Envoy config
  ┌────────────────────────────┐          ┌────────────────────────────┐
  │ Istio abstraction layer     │          │ Direct Envoy manipulation  │
  │ ✅ Version-independent      │          │ ⚠️  Envoy version-coupled  │
  │ ✅ Validated by istioctl    │          │ ⚠️  No validation          │
  │ ✅ Portable across upgrades │          │ ⚠️  May break on upgrade   │
  └────────────────────────────┘          └────────────────────────────┘
  
  RULE: Try VirtualService first. Try WasmPlugin second.
  Only use EnvoyFilter when nothing else works.

For anihpj/jobpost, avoid EnvoyFilter unless absolutely necessary. Use VirtualService and DestinationRule for routing, WasmPlugin for custom extensions, and only reach for EnvoyFilter when standard CRDs cannot express your needs. EnvoyFilter tightly couples configuration to Envoy internals and may break on Istio upgrades.

Install/Config Q32 of 200
What is WasmPlugin in Istio and how does it compare to EnvoyFilter?
A) WasmPlugin is an older, deprecated version of EnvoyFilter
B) WasmPlugin is the modern, safer alternative to EnvoyFilter. It lets you extend Envoy with WebAssembly (WASM) modules — custom auth, custom metrics, header manipulation — without writing raw Envoy config. WASM modules are sandboxed (memory-safe), hot-reloadable, and can be distributed via OCI registries. Prefer WasmPlugin over EnvoyFilter for custom extensions
C) WasmPlugin is only for ingress gateways; EnvoyFilter works everywhere
D) WasmPlugin requires a commercial Istio license
✅ Correct Answer: B

WasmPlugin is the modern, safer alternative to EnvoyFilter. WASM modules are sandboxed (memory-safe), hot-reloadable, and distributed via OCI registries. Write custom extensions in Rust/Go/C++ — no raw Envoy config needed.

📝 Detailed Explanation
WasmPlugin vs EnvoyFilter — The Modern Extension Pattern
  WasmPlugin (recommended):               EnvoyFilter (legacy escape):
  ┌────────────────────────────┐          ┌────────────────────────────┐
  │ apiVersion: extensions     │          │ apiVersion: networking     │
  │   .istio.io/v1alpha1       │          │   .istio.io/v1alpha3       │
  │ kind: WasmPlugin           │          │ kind: EnvoyFilter          │
  │ spec:                      │          │ spec:                      │
  │   url: oci://registry/     │          │   configPatches: [...]     │
  │     my-filter:v1.0         │          │   (raw Envoy JSON)         │
  │   phase: AUTHZ             │          │                             │
  │                             │          │ ✅ Full Envoy power         │
  │ ✅ Memory-safe sandbox      │          │ ⚠️  Footgun potential      │
  │ ✅ OCI distribution         │          │ ⚠️  Envoy version-locked    │
  └────────────────────────────┘          └────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, use WasmPlugin for custom extensions like rate limiting or custom metrics. Write the extension in Rust or Go, compile to WASM, push to an OCI registry, and reference it in WasmPlugin. The WASM module runs sandboxed inside Envoy -- memory-safe, hot-reloadable, and upgrade-safe.

  resolution: DNS                           resolution: STATIC

  └────────────────────────────┘            └────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, use DNS resolution for the AWS RDS ServiceEntry because RDS endpoints change during failover events. istiod re-resolves the hostname every ~60 seconds, automatically updating Envoy endpoints. Install/Config Q34 of 200
What is the difference between Sidecar and Ambient mode in Istio?
A) Ambient mode is the old name for sidecar mode — they're the same thing
B) Sidecar mode uses WASM; ambient mode uses Lua scripts
C) Ambient mode requires a cloud provider; sidecar mode works on-premises only
D) Sidecar mode injects an Envoy proxy into EVERY pod (2 containers per pod). Ambient mode uses a per-node ztunnel (Rust-based, lightweight) for L4 mTLS/authorization and an optional waypoint proxy for L7 policies — NO sidecar container in application pods. Ambient reduces resource overhead and simplifies operations but is newer with fewer L7 features
✅ Correct Answer: D

Ambient mesh eliminates sidecars. A per-node ztunnel (Rust-based DaemonSet) handles L4 mTLS/authorization. An optional per-namespace waypoint proxy (Envoy) handles L7 policies. Pods stay lean — no injected containers needed.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Sidecar vs Ambient — Two Mesh Architectures
  SIDECAR MODE:                           AMBIENT MODE:
  ┌──────────────────────┐               ┌──────────────────────┐
  │ anihpj-web pod        │               │ anihpj-web pod        │
  │ ┌────────┐┌─────────┐ │               │ ┌────────┐           │
  │ │Django  ││Envoy    │ │               │ │Django  │ (1/1!)    │
  │ │App     ││Sidecar  │ │               │ │App     │           │
  │ └────────┘└─────────┘ │               │ └────────┘           │
  │   2/2 containers       │               └──────────┬───────────┘
  └──────────────────────┘                          │
                                          ┌──────────┴───────────┐
  RESOURCES: ~200MB/pod                   │ ztunnel (DaemonSet)  │
  OPERATIONS: per-pod proxy               │ per-node L4 mTLS     │
  MATURITY: Production-ready              └──────────────────────┘
                                          RESOURCES: ~10MB/pod
                                          OPERATIONS: no per-pod proxy
                                          MATURITY: Maturing rapidly

For anihpj/jobpost, ambient mesh eliminates the need for per-pod sidecars. A per-node ztunnel (Rust-based, ~10MB) handles L4 mTLS and authorization. For L7 policies, deploy a waypoint proxy per namespace. Pods show 1/1 READY instead of 2/2 -- simpler operations, lower resource overhead.

Install/Config Q35 of 200
What is ztunnel in Istio's ambient mesh?
A) ztunnel is the new name for istiod — it's the control plane rewritten in Rust
B) ztunnel is a per-node, Rust-based proxy (DaemonSet) that handles L4 responsibilities in ambient mode: mTLS encryption, simple authorization, and TCP proxying between pods. It replaces the per-pod Envoy sidecar for L4, eliminating the need for sidecar injection. For L7 policies, a waypoint proxy (Envoy-based) is deployed per namespace
C) ztunnel is a Kubernetes CNI plugin — it replaces Calico/Cilium
D) ztunnel is a monitoring tool for ambient mesh metrics
✅ Correct Answer: B

ztunnel is the Rust-based per-node proxy in ambient mesh. It runs as a DaemonSet and transparently handles L4 mTLS encryption and simple authorization for all pods on the node — no sidecar injection needed.

📝 Detailed Explanation
ztunnel — The Rust-Based Heart of Ambient Mesh
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  NODE: worker-1                                                  │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ ztunnel DaemonSet pod (1 per node)                        │  │
  │  │ • Written in Rust (memory-safe, high-performance)         │  │
  │  │ • Handles L4: mTLS encryption + simple authZ             │  │
  │  │ • Intercepts pod traffic via eBPF/iptables               │  │
  │  │ • No Envoy dependency — lightweight (~10MB)              │  │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐                     │
  │  │ Pod A    │  │ Pod B    │  │ Pod C    │  (all 1/1!)          │
  │  │ (no proxy)│  │ (no proxy)│  │ (no proxy)│                     │
  │  └──────────┘  └──────────┘  └──────────┘                     │
  │                                                                  │
  │  All pod traffic → ztunnel → encrypted + authenticated          │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost on ambient mesh, ztunnel runs as a DaemonSet on every node. All pod traffic (anihpj-web, anihpj-api, anihpj-db) is transparently intercepted at the node level -- no sidecar containers, no init containers, no iptables rules per pod. Pods stay lean (1/1 Ready) while still getting mTLS encryption and L4 authorization.

Install/Config Q38 of 200
How do you configure HPA (Horizontal Pod Autoscaler) for istiod in production?
A) Istiod cannot be auto-scaled — it must run as a single replica
B) HPA is enabled by default — no configuration needed
C) Run kubectl autoscale deploy istiod -n istio-system --min=1 --max=10 --cpu-percent=80
D) Configure HPA in the IstioOperator or Helm values: set pilot.autoscaleEnabled: true, pilot.autoscaleMin: 2, pilot.autoscaleMax: 5, and CPU/memory target utilization. For production, always run at least 2 replicas for HA
✅ Correct Answer: D

Production istiod must be HA (min 2 replicas). Configure HPA via IstioOperator or Helm values. Never run a single istiod replica in production — if it crashes, new pods cannot get sidecars injected and config changes stop propagating to Envoys.

📝 Detailed Explanation
istiod HPA — Production HA Configuration
  IstioOperator HPA config:
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  spec:                                                          │
  │    components:                                                  │
  │      pilot:                                                     │
  │        k8s:                                                     │
  │          hpaSpec:                                               │
  │            minReplicas: 2    ← NEVER run 1 in production!       │
  │            maxReplicas: 5    ← Scale up under load              │
  │            metrics:                                             │
  │            - type: Resource                                     │
  │              resource:                                          │
  │                name: cpu                                        │
  │                targetAverageUtilization: 80                     │
  │                                                                  │
  │  2 REPLICAS:                                                    │
  │  ┌──────────────┐    ┌──────────────┐                          │
  │  │ istiod-1     │    │ istiod-2     │  ← If one crashes,       │
  │  │ (active)     │    │ (standby)    │    the other takes over   │
  │  └──────────────┘    └──────────────┘                          │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, configure HPA for istiod via IstioOperator or Helm values. Set pilot.autoscaleMin: 2 (never run a single replica), pilot.autoscaleMax: 5, and CPU/memory target utilization. Two replicas ensure that if one istiod crashes, the other continues serving — new anihpj pods can still get sidecars injected.

Install/Config Q39 of 200
What is the recommended way to customize Envoy proxy resource limits for all sidecars in the mesh?
A) Set meshConfig.defaultConfig.proxyMetadata or use the sidecar.istio.io/proxyCPU and sidecar.istio.io/proxyMemory pod annotations. For mesh-wide defaults, configure spec.meshConfig.defaultConfig in the IstioOperator
B) Edit each Deployment YAML and add resource limits to the istio-proxy container manually
C) Resource limits cannot be customized — Envoy always uses default values
D) Create a ResourceQuota in the istio-system namespace
✅ Correct Answer: A

Sidecar resources: ~50-200MB RAM per Envoy depending on mesh size. Tune globally via IstioOperator meshConfig.defaultConfig or per-pod via annotations sidecar.istio.io/proxyCPU and sidecar.istio.io/proxyMemory. Mesh-wide defaults apply to all new sidecars; per-pod annotations override for specific workloads.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Envoy Proxy Resource Tuning — Global vs Per-Pod
  GLOBAL (IstioOperator):                 PER-POD (annotations):
  ┌────────────────────────────┐          ┌────────────────────────────┐
  │ meshConfig:                │          │ metadata:                  │
  │   defaultConfig:           │          │   annotations:              │
  │     proxyMetadata:         │          │     sidecar.istio.io/      │
  │       ISTIO_META_          │          │       proxyCPU: "500m"     │
  │         PROXY_XDS_DEBUG:   │          │     sidecar.istio.io/      │
  │         "true"             │          │       proxyMemory: "256Mi" │
  │                            │          │                             │
  │  Applies to ALL sidecars   │          │  Overrides for THIS pod     │
  └────────────────────────────┘          └────────────────────────────┘
  
  anihpj RESOURCE PLAN:
  ┌─────────────┬──────────┬──────────┐
  │  WORKLOAD   │  CPU     │  MEMORY  │
  ├─────────────┼──────────┼──────────┤
  │  anihpj-web │  200m    │  128Mi   │  ← Lightweight (serves HTML)
  │  anihpj-api │  500m    │  256Mi   │  ← Medium (DB queries)
  │  anihpj-db  │  100m    │  64Mi    │  ← Minimal (TCP proxy)
  └─────────────┴──────────┴──────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, tune Envoy proxy resources per workload. The anihpj-web sidecar serving the job board homepage needs ~128MB RAM. The anihpj-api sidecar handling database queries needs ~256MB. Configure globally via meshConfig.defaultConfig and override per-pod via annotations.

Install/Config Q40 of 200
You installed Istio but istioctl proxy-status shows no proxies. What must you do first?
A) Run istioctl proxy-status --refresh to force a refresh
B) Deploy a pod in a namespace labeled istio-injection=enabled. proxy-status only shows proxies that have connected to istiod — if no pods have sidecars yet, the list will be empty. Label a namespace, deploy a test pod, and check again
C) Restart istiod — it needs to begin listening for proxy connections
D) proxy-status requires Prometheus to be installed — deploy Prometheus first
✅ Correct Answer: B

Proxy-status shows connected Envoys only — no sidecars deployed yet = empty list. Label a namespace with istio-injection=enabled, deploy a test pod, verify it shows 2/2 Ready, then proxy-status will show the connected Envoy with SYNCED status across all xDS types.

📝 Detailed Explanation
proxy-status — From Empty to SYNCED
  BEFORE (no pods in mesh):               AFTER (pod deployed):
  $ istioctl proxy-status                 $ kubectl label ns test istio-injection=enabled
  NAME  CDS  LDS  EDS  RDS  ISTIOD        $ kubectl run nginx --image=nginx -n test
  (empty)                                  $ istioctl proxy-status
                                           NAME                CDS     LDS     EDS     RDS
  WHY EMPTY?                              nginx.test          SYNCED  SYNCED  SYNCED  SYNCED
  • No namespace has istio-injection      
    label                                 VERIFICATION:
  • No pods have sidecars injected        $ kubectl get pods -n test
  • proxy-status lists ONLY connected      NAME    READY   STATUS
    Envoys with active xDS streams         nginx   2/2     Running  ← 2/2 = sidecar injected!

For anihpj/jobpost, after installing Istio, label the anihpj namespace with istio-injection=enabled and deploy a test pod. Run istioctl proxy-status — you should see the test pod with SYNCED status. Without any pods in the mesh, proxy-status is empty — this is expected, not an error.
