Official CNCF sub-topics: Configuring Ingress and Egress Traffic, Configuring Routing within a Service Mesh, Connecting In-Mesh Workloads to External Workloads and Services. Q41-Q80.

🚦 Category 2: Traffic — Ingress, Egress & Routing (20%)Q41-Q80

🟡 Controlling How Traffic Enters, Exits, and Routes Through Your Mesh

This category covers the core traffic management capabilities: VirtualService routing, Gateway configuration, ServiceEntry for external services, and the fundamentals of how Istio directs traffic. This is where the mesh starts to deliver real value — controlling exactly how requests flow through your services.

2.1 VirtualService — Request Routing & MatchingQ41-Q52

Traffic-I/E/R Q41 of 200
What is a VirtualService in Istio and what problem does it solve that a standard Kubernetes Service cannot?
A) A VirtualService defines HOW traffic is routed to a destination service. Unlike K8s Services (L4 round-robin), VirtualService enables L7 routing: match by URI path, headers, or query parameters; route to different subsets (v1/v2); split traffic by weight; inject faults; and configure retries/timeouts. It's the primary traffic management resource in Istio
B) A VirtualService is a replacement for K8s Service — you delete the Service and use VirtualService instead for both discovery and routing
C) VirtualService only works at the mesh edge (with Gateway) — it cannot route internal mesh traffic
D) VirtualService is a monitoring tool that visualizes traffic flow in Kiali
✅ Correct Answer: A

For anihpj/jobpost, after installing Istio, label the anihpj namespace with istio-injection=enabled and deploy a test pod. Run istioctl proxy-status -- you should see the test pod with SYNCED status. Without any pods in the mesh, proxy-status is empty -- this is expected, not an error.

VirtualService is the centerpiece of Istio traffic management. It replaces what would otherwise require application-level routing code, load balancer configurations, and deployment scripts. One VirtualService can express canary deployments, path-based routing, header-based A/B testing, and retry/timeout policies — all in one YAML file.

📝 Detailed Explanation
VirtualService — The Complete Routing Resource
  apiVersion: networking.istio.io/v1beta1
  kind: VirtualService
  metadata: {name: anihpj-api-vs, namespace: anihpj}
  spec:
    hosts: [anihpj-api]                    ← which host(s) this applies to
    http:
    - match:
      - uri: {prefix: /api/v2}              ← L7 routing: match by URL
        headers: {x-canary: {exact: "true"}} ← AND match by header
      route:
      - destination:
          host: anihpj-api
          subset: v2                        ← route to v2 subset
    - route:                                ← catch-all (no match = default)
      - destination:
          host: anihpj-api
          subset: v1
        weight: 90                          ← 90% to v1
      - destination:
          host: anihpj-api
          subset: v2
        weight: 10                          ← 10% to v2 (canary!)

For anihpj/jobpost, a VirtualService on anihpj-api enables canary deployments, path-based API versioning, and retry policies for database calls — all configured declaratively without touching Django code.

Traffic-I/E/R Q42 of 200
In a VirtualService, multiple match blocks — how are they evaluated?
A) All matches combined with AND
B) Matches evaluated randomly
C) Last match wins
D) First-match-wins, top-to-bottom. Put specific matches BEFORE catch-all routes
✅ Correct Answer: D

VirtualService matches are evaluated top-to-bottom, first-match-wins. Put specific routes first, catch-all last. If you reverse the order, the catch-all will consume ALL traffic and specific routes will never be reached.

📝 Detailed Explanation
First-Match-Wins — Why Order Matters
  http:
  - match:                             ← Evaluated FIRST
    - uri: {prefix: /api/v2}
      headers: {x-canary: {exact: "true"}}
    route: [{destination: {subset: v2}}]   ← /api/v2 + canary header → v2
  - match:                             ← Evaluated SECOND
    - uri: {prefix: /api/}
    route: [{destination: {subset: v1}}]   ← All /api/* → v1
  - route:                             ← Catch-all (LAST)
    - destination: {subset: v1}
    weight: 90                              ← 90% to v1
    weight: 10                              ← 10% to v2 (canary)

  ❌ If catch-all were FIRST: all traffic would match it,
     and the /api/v2 route would NEVER be reached!

For anihpj/jobpost, always order VirtualService matches from most specific to least specific. A header-based route for beta testers should come BEFORE the catch-all weight-based split. If the catch-all is first, it consumes all traffic and your beta testers never reach the experimental version.

Traffic-I/E/R Q43 of 200
What does URI rewrite in a VirtualService do?
A) Changes the host header for cross-domain redirects
B) Rewrites URL path before forwarding — e.g., /api/v2/users → /users. Transparent to client, enables API versioning without backend changes
C) 301 permanent redirect — client URL bar changes
D) Only for gRPC — HTTP paths cannot be rewritten
✅ Correct Answer: B

URI rewrite transparently changes the URL path before forwarding. The client sees the original URL; the backend receives the rewritten one. Essential for API versioning without backend code changes.

📝 Detailed Explanation
URI Rewrite — Client Sees One URL, Backend Gets Another
  CLIENT REQUEST:                       BACKEND RECEIVES:
  GET /api/v2/users HTTP/1.1           GET /users HTTP/1.1
  Host: jobpost.com                    Host: anihpj-api
  
  VirtualService rewrite:
  http:
  - match:
    - uri: {prefix: /api/v2}           ← Client calls /api/v2/*
    rewrite:
      uri: /                            ← Strips /api/v2 prefix!
    route:
    - destination:
        host: anihpj-api
        subset: v2                       ← Backend gets clean /users

For anihpj/jobpost, use URI rewrite for API versioning: external clients call /api/v2/jobs but the Django backend receives /jobs. This means you can version your API without changing a single line of Django URL routing -- Istio handles the path transformation transparently.

Traffic-I/E/R Q44 of 200
What is traffic mirroring (dark launch)?
A) Sends a COPY of live requests to a shadow service. Shadow response is discarded. Tests new versions with real traffic at zero risk
B) Duplicates all traffic to a backup cluster
C) Same as canary — replaces a percentage of traffic
D) Shadow service must respond within timeout or client sees errors
✅ Correct Answer: A

Traffic mirroring sends a copy of live requests to a shadow service. The shadow's response is discarded — the client only sees the primary response. Perfect for testing new versions with real production traffic at zero risk.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Traffic Mirroring — Dark Launch to Test v2 with Real Traffic
  client ──► VirtualService ──┬──► anihpj-api v1 (100% traffic, client gets v1 response)
                               │
                               └──► anihpj-api v2 (COPY of traffic, response DISCARDED)
  
  VirtualService config:
  http:
  - route:
    - destination: {host: anihpj-api, subset: v1}
      weight: 100
    mirror:
      host: anihpj-api
      subset: v2                          ← Shadow: receives copies, silent
    mirrorPercentage:
      value: 100                          ← Mirror 100% of traffic
  
  🔑 v2 receives production traffic but users NEVER see v2 responses.
  Monitor v2's error rate and latency before promoting to canary.

For anihpj/jobpost, use traffic mirroring to test the new recommendation engine. Mirror 100% of /api/jobs traffic to the ML service while users see only the existing search results. Monitor the shadow service for a week -- if it proves stable under real production load, promote it to receive real user traffic.

Traffic-I/E/R Q45 of 200
Gateway vs VirtualService — how do they work together?
A) Gateway replaces VirtualService entirely
B) Old/new names for the same resource
C) Gateway = L4-L6 (ports, TLS, hosts). VirtualService = L7 routing (where requests go). One Gateway can serve many VirtualServices
D) Gateway only for multi-cluster
✅ Correct Answer: C

Gateway handles L4-L6 (ports, protocols, TLS, hosts) — it defines WHERE to listen. VirtualService handles L7 routing — it defines WHERE matching requests go. They work as a pair: Gateway binds a port, VirtualService routes the traffic that arrives on that port.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Gateway + VirtualService — L4-L6 + L7 Working Together
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  GATEWAY (L4-L6): "I listen here"                              │
  │  apiVersion: networking.istio.io/v1beta1                        │
  │  kind: Gateway                                                   │
  │  spec:                                                           │
  │    selector: {istio: ingressgateway}  ← which proxy runs this   │
  │    servers:                                                      │
  │    - port: {number: 443, protocol: HTTPS}                       │
  │      hosts: ["api.jobpost.com"]          ← SNI host matching     │
  │      tls: {mode: SIMPLE, credentialName: jobpost-cert}          │
  └───────────────────────────┬─────────────────────────────────────┘
                              │ "I route matching traffic"
  ┌───────────────────────────┴─────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  VIRTUALSERVICE (L7):                                            │
  │  spec:                                                           │
  │    hosts: ["api.jobpost.com"]     ← MUST match Gateway hosts!    │
  │    gateways: ["anihpj-gateway"]  ← which Gateway to attach to   │
  │    http:                                                         │
  │    - match: [{uri: {prefix: /api}}]                              │
  │      route: [{destination: {host: anihpj-api, port: 8000}}]    │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, the Gateway defines WHERE to listen (port 443, protocol HTTPS, host jobpost.com) and the VirtualService defines WHERE matching requests go (route to anihpj-web). This clean separation means you can change routing rules without touching the Gateway, and add new hosts without modifying existing VirtualServices.

Traffic-I/E/R Q46 of 200
What is a ServiceEntry?
A) Only for internal mesh services
B) Auto-created for every external service
C) Deprecated — use Egress Gateway
D) Registers external services in Istio's registry. Enables mTLS origination, metrics, and routing for external deps like AWS RDS
✅ Correct Answer: D

ServiceEntry brings external services into Istio's service registry. Once registered, external services get mesh treatment: mTLS origination, metrics, and traffic policies. With REGISTRY_ONLY outbound policy, ONLY ServiceEntry-registered services are reachable — implementing zero-trust egress.

📝 Detailed Explanation
ServiceEntry — Register External Services in the Mesh
  WITHOUT ServiceEntry:                   WITH ServiceEntry:
  ┌────────────────────────┐             ┌────────────────────────────┐
  │ anihpj-api → Stripe    │             │ anihpj-api → Stripe         │
  │          → Mailgun     │             │ (Envoy: metrics, mTLS)      │
  │ (direct, no mesh)     │             │                              │
  │ ⚠️  No metrics         │             │ ServiceEntry:                │
  │ ⚠️  No mTLS            │             │ apiVersion: networking.istio│
  │ ⚠️  No egress control  │             │   .io/v1beta1               │
  └────────────────────────┘             │ kind: ServiceEntry           │
                                          │ spec:                       │
  meshConfig:                             │   hosts: [api.stripe.com]   │
    outboundTrafficPolicy:                │   ports: [{number: 443,     │
      mode: REGISTRY_ONLY                 │     protocol: HTTPS}]       │
  └─► Only ServiceEntry hosts OK          │   resolution: DNS           │
                                          │   location: MESH_EXTERNAL   │
                                          └────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, ServiceEntry registers api.stripe.com and smtp.mailgun.net in the mesh registry. Now Envoy tracks every payment API call (rate, latency, errors) and every email sent. Combined with REGISTRY_ONLY mode, all other external traffic is blocked -- full observability and zero-trust egress.

Traffic-I/E/R Q47 of 200
TCP vs HTTP VirtualService — key difference?
A) Identical — no difference
B) HTTP: L7 matching (URI, headers) + rich features. TCP: L4 only (port, subnet) + basic routing
C) TCP supports MORE features
D) TCP VirtualServices don't exist
✅ Correct Answer: B

HTTP VirtualServices understand HTTP semantics (URIs, headers, methods) and support rich L7 features. TCP VirtualServices see raw bytes and only support L4 routing by port/IP. Anihpj REST APIs use HTTP VS; PostgreSQL connections use TCP VS.

📝 Detailed Explanation
HTTP vs TCP VirtualService — Feature Comparison
  ┌─────────────────────┬───────────────────────┬───────────────────┐
  │  FEATURE            │  HTTP VirtualService  │  TCP VirtualSvc   │
  ├─────────────────────┼───────────────────────┼───────────────────┤
  │  URI path matching  │  ✅ /api/* /users     │  ❌ No L7 parsing │
  │  Header matching    │  ✅ x-canary: true    │  ❌               │
  │  Weight-based split │  ✅ 90% v1 / 10% v2  │  ❌               │
  │  Retries            │  ✅                   │  ❌               │
  │  Fault injection    │  ✅ delay/abort       │  ❌               │
  │  URI rewrite        │  ✅                   │  ❌               │
  │  Mirroring          │  ✅                   │  ❌               │
  │  CORS               │  ✅                   │  ❌               │
  │  Port matching      │  ✅                   │  ✅               │
  │  Source IP matching │  ✅                   │  ✅               │
  └─────────────────────┴───────────────────────┴───────────────────┘
  
  anihpj: REST API calls use HTTP VirtualService.
  anihpj: PostgreSQL connections use TCP VirtualService.
  K8s Service port names MUST start with protocol prefix:
    name: http-api  ← Envoy treats as HTTP
    name: tcp-pg    ← Envoy treats as TCP

For anihpj/jobpost, the Django REST API endpoints use HTTP VirtualService (match by URI path /api/*, rich L7 features). The PostgreSQL database uses TCP VirtualService (match by port 5432, L4 only). Name your K8s Service ports with protocol prefixes: http-api and tcp-postgres for correct protocol detection.

Traffic-I/E/R Q48 of 200
VirtualService hosts field — what value?
A) MUST match the K8s Service name or FQDN. Host mismatch = VirtualService silently ignored — #1 routing issue
B) Optional, defaults to "*"
C) Specifies SOURCE pod filter
D) Must be an IP address
✅ Correct Answer: A

The VirtualService hosts field MUST match the Kubernetes Service name (short or FQDN). A mismatch is the #1 reason VirtualServices silently fail — Envoy doesn't know which service the rules apply to.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Host Field Matching — The #1 VirtualService Gotcha
  ❌ VirtualService hosts: ["anihpj-api-v1"]
     kubectl get svc -n anihpj → NAME: anihpj-api
     MISMATCH! VirtualService ignored silently.

  ✅ VirtualService hosts: ["anihpj-api"]
     kubectl get svc -n anihpj → NAME: anihpj-api
     MATCH! VirtualService applies correctly.

  ALSO VALID (FQDN):
  hosts: ["anihpj-api.anihpj.svc.cluster.local"]

  DEBUGGING:
  istioctl proxy-config routes  -n anihpj -o json | \
    jq '.[].name'
  → Shows what routes Envoy actually has loaded.
  If your route is missing, check the host field!

For anihpj/jobpost, the VirtualService hosts field MUST match the Kubernetes Service name exactly. If your Service is anihpj-api but the VirtualService hosts field says anihpj-api-v1, the routing rules are silently ignored. Always verify with istioctl proxy-config routes that your routes are actually loaded in Envoy.

Traffic-I/E/R Q49 of 200
CORS policy in VirtualService — why use it?
A) Blocks all cross-origin requests by default
B) Only for gRPC
C) Handles browser CORS at mesh layer — Envoy responds to OPTIONS preflight, adds headers. No Django middleware needed
D) Managed by K8s Ingress, not VirtualService
✅ Correct Answer: C

CORS (Cross-Origin Resource Sharing) configured at the mesh layer means Envoy handles browser preflight OPTIONS requests and adds CORS response headers. No Django middleware needed — zero application code changes.

📝 Detailed Explanation
CORS at the Mesh Layer — Envoy Handles It All
  BROWSER PREFLIGHT REQUEST:
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  OPTIONS /api/jobs HTTP/1.1                                      │
  │  Origin: https://jobpost.com                                    │
  │  Access-Control-Request-Method: POST                             │
  │                                                                  │
  │  VirtualService CORS config:                                     │
  │  http:                                                           │
  │  - corsPolicy:                                                   │
  │      allowOrigins: [{exact: "https://jobpost.com"}]             │
  │      allowMethods: [GET, POST, PUT, DELETE]                     │
  │      allowHeaders: [Authorization, Content-Type]                │
  │      maxAge: 86400s                                              │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ENOY RESPONDS (no backend needed!):                             │
  │  200 OK                                                          │
  │  Access-Control-Allow-Origin: https://jobpost.com                │
  │  Access-Control-Allow-Methods: GET, POST, PUT, DELETE           │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, configure CORS at the mesh layer instead of in Django middleware. Envoy handles browser preflight OPTIONS requests, adds Access-Control headers, and caches responses for 24 hours. The Django app never sees CORS requests -- one less middleware to maintain, one less dependency in application code.

Traffic-I/E/R Q50 of 200
Two VirtualServices for same host — what happens?
A) First created wins
B) Higher priority field wins
C) Istio rejects the second one
D) MERGED — rules combined but order unspecified. Best practice: ONE VirtualService per host
✅ Correct Answer: D

Istio MERGES multiple VirtualServices for the same host — but the merge order is UNSPECIFIED. Two teams creating separate VirtualServices for different routes on the same host can produce unpredictable results. Best practice: ONE VirtualService per host.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Multiple VirtualServices — Merged but Unpredictable
  VirtualService A (team-web):              VirtualService B (team-api):
  hosts: [anihpj-api]                       hosts: [anihpj-api]
  http:                                     http:
  - match: [{uri: {prefix: /web}}]         - match: [{uri: {prefix: /api}}]
    route: [{host: anihpj-web}]              route: [{host: anihpj-api}]
  
  MERGED RESULT (order NOT guaranteed):
  http:
  - match: [{uri: {prefix: /web}}]    OR   - match: [{uri: {prefix: /api}}]
  - match: [{uri: {prefix: /api}}]         - match: [{uri: {prefix: /web}}]
  
  ✅ SOLUTION: Single VirtualService combining all routes:
  http:
  - match: [{uri: {prefix: /web}}] → route to anihpj-web
  - match: [{uri: {prefix: /api}}] → route to anihpj-api

For anihpj/jobpost, always use ONE VirtualService per host to avoid unpredictable merge behavior. If the web team and API team both create VirtualServices for anihpj-api, the merge order is unspecified and routes may conflict. Consolidate all routes for a host into a single VirtualService managed by the platform team.

Traffic-I/E/R Q51 of 200
How do you configure weight-based traffic splitting (canary deployment) in a VirtualService to send 90% of requests to v1 and 10% to v2?
A) Create two separate VirtualServices — one for v1 with weight 90 and one for v2 with weight 10
B) In a single VirtualService route block, specify TWO destinations with weight fields: destination: {subset: v1, weight: 90} and destination: {subset: v2, weight: 10}. Weights must sum to 100. This distributes the traffic stream proportionally across the subsets defined in the DestinationRule
C) Use kubectl set traffic anihpj-api --canary=v2 --weight=10 — a kubectl plugin command that generates the VirtualService automatically
D) Weight-based routing requires configuring EnvoyFilter manually — VirtualService only supports exact match routing, not weighted distribution
✅ Correct Answer: B

Weight-based routing is the foundation of canary deployments in Istio. In a single VirtualService route, add multiple destinations with weight fields that sum to 100. Istio distributes requests randomly according to these weights. No Kubernetes plugin needed — pure VirtualService YAML.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Weight-Based Traffic Splitting — Canary Deployment (90/10)
  VirtualService: anihpj-api
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  http:                                                          │
  │  - route:                                                       │
  │    - destination:                                               │
  │        host: anihpj-api                                         │
  │        subset: v1          ← stable version                     │
  │        weight: 90          ← 90% of traffic                     │
  │    - destination:                                               │
  │        host: anihpj-api                                         │
  │        subset: v2          ← canary version                     │
  │        weight: 10          ← 10% of traffic                     │
  │                                                                  │
  │  TRAFFIC FLOW:                                                   │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │  REQUEST STREAM (100 req/s)                               │  │
  │  │  │                                                        │  │
  │  │  ├──► v1 pod-1 (45 req/s)                                 │  │
  │  │  ├──► v1 pod-2 (45 req/s)   = 90 total (90%)             │  │
  │  │  ├──► v2 pod-1 (10 req/s)   = 10 total (10%)             │  │
  │  │  └──► (random distribution within each subset)            │  │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, you'd deploy the new API version (v2.1.0) as a canary with 10% traffic. Monitor istio_requests_total{destination_service="anihpj-api", destination_version="v2"} in Prometheus. If error rate stays low and latency is similar to v1, increase weight to 30% → 50% → 100% for full promotion.

Traffic-I/E/R Q52 of 200
How do you combine multiple match conditions in a VirtualService? What is the difference between conditions within the SAME match block vs conditions across SEPARATE match blocks?
A) Conditions within the SAME match block are ANDed — all must be true for the route to apply (e.g., URI starts with /api AND header x-canary=true). Conditions in SEPARATE match blocks are ORed — if ANY match block matches, its route is used (first match wins). This lets you express complex routing like: "route to v2 if (path=/api/v2 AND header=canary) OR (path=/api/v2 AND queryParam=beta)"
B) All conditions are always ORed regardless of structure — VirtualService does not support AND logic
C) All conditions are always ANDed regardless of structure — VirtualService does not support OR logic
D) Match conditions are evaluated alphabetically — the structure within match blocks is irrelevant
✅ Correct Answer: A

Single match block = AND (all conditions must match). Multiple match blocks = OR (first matching block wins). This is the core matching semantic: conditions inside one - match: array are combined with AND; separate - match: entries are evaluated top-to-bottom with OR logic.

📝 Detailed Explanation
AND vs OR in VirtualService Match Blocks
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  AND (same match block — ALL conditions must be true):           │
  │  - match:                                                        │
  │    - uri: {prefix: /api/v2}         ← condition 1               │
  │      headers:                        ← condition 2               │
  │        x-canary: {exact: "true"}    ← condition 3               │
  │      queryParams:                    ← condition 4               │
  │        beta: {exact: "enabled"}                                  │
  │    → ALL FOUR must match for this route to apply                │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
  
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  OR (separate match blocks — first match wins):                  │
  │  - match:                                                        │
  │    - uri: {prefix: /api/v2}                                     │
  │      headers: {x-canary: {exact: "true"}}                       │
  │    route: [{subset: v2}]   ← /api/v2 + canary header = v2      │
  │  - match:                                                        │
  │    - uri: {prefix: /api}                                        │
  │    route: [{subset: v1}]   ← catch-all /api/* = v1             │
  │                                                                  │
  │  REQUEST: GET /api/v2 (no canary header)                        │
  │  → First match FAILS (no header) → Second match PASSES → v1 ✅  │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, you could combine: route to v2 if URI is /api/* AND user-agent contains "Mobile" (AND within one match), OR route to beta if query param ?preview=true (OR across matches). This gives precise control over A/B testing and gradual feature rollouts.

2.2 DestinationRule — Subsets, Load Balancing & Connection ManagementQ53-Q62

Traffic-I/E/R Q53 of 200
What is a DestinationRule in Istio and how does it differ from a VirtualService?
A) DestinationRule and VirtualService are interchangeable — they do the same thing with different syntax
B) DestinationRule defines WHERE traffic goes; VirtualService defines HOW it gets there
C) DestinationRule only applies to gateways; VirtualService only applies to sidecars
D) DestinationRule defines WHAT happens at the destination: load balancing policy (ROUND_ROBIN/LEAST_REQUEST/RANDOM), connection pool limits, outlier detection (circuit breaking), TLS settings, and subset definitions (v1/v2/v3). VirtualService defines HOW traffic is routed (match by URI/headers, split by weight). They work TOGETHER — VirtualService routes to a subset, DestinationRule defines how that subset behaves
✅ Correct Answer: D

DestinationRule is the "destination policy" resource. While VirtualService answers "which service version should this request go to?", DestinationRule answers "once the request arrives, how should we handle the connection?" — load balancing, connection pooling, circuit breaking, and TLS configuration.

📝 Detailed Explanation
VirtualService vs DestinationRule — Two Halves of Traffic Management
  VirtualService ("ROUTING")              DestinationRule ("POLICY")
  ┌────────────────────────────┐         ┌────────────────────────────┐
  │ HOW traffic gets there:     │         │ WHAT happens at destination:│
  │                             │         │                             │
  │ • Match by URI/headers      │         │ • Load balancing algorithm  │
  │ • Route to subsets (v1/v2)  │         │   ROUND_ROBIN, LEAST_REQ   │
  │ • Split traffic by weight   │         │ • Connection pool limits    │
  │ • Rewrite/redirect URIs     │         │ • Outlier detection (CB)   │
  │ • Inject faults (delay/500) │         │ • TLS mode (ISTIO_MUTUAL)  │
  │ • Retries & timeouts        │         │ • Subset labels (v1/v2/v3) │
  │ • CORS, mirror, headers     │         │ • Consistent hash LB       │
  └────────────────────────────┘         └────────────────────────────┘
  
  TOGETHER:
  VirtualService: /api/v2 → subset: v2 (weight: 10)
  DestinationRule: v2 subset uses LEAST_REQUEST LB + outlier detection
  → v2 pods get 10% traffic, least-busy pod first, bad pods ejected

For anihpj/jobpost, DestinationRule defines WHAT happens when traffic reaches a service. VirtualService handles routing (send 10% to v2), DestinationRule handles connection behavior: LEAST_REQUEST LB for variable API calls, outlier detection for failing pods, and ISTIO_MUTUAL for automatic encryption -- all configured once, applied to every connection.

Traffic-I/E/R Q54 of 200
How do you define subsets (v1, v2, v3) in a DestinationRule and what are they used for?
A) Subsets are defined in VirtualService — DestinationRule only handles TLS settings
B) Subsets are defined in DestinationRule using labels that match pod labels. Each subset represents a group of pods (e.g., version=v1, version=v2). VirtualServices then route to these subsets by name, enabling canary deployments, A/B testing, and gradual migrations
C) Subsets are automatically created from Kubernetes Deployments — no manual definition needed
D) Subsets require a separate Subset CRD — you cannot define them in DestinationRule
✅ Correct Answer: B

Subsets are named groups of pods defined by label selectors in DestinationRule. Each subset (v1, v2, v3) maps to pods with specific labels. VirtualServices then reference subsets by name to route traffic to different versions without knowing pod IPs.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Subsets — Named Groups of Pods by Label
  DestinationRule: anihpj-api
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  subsets:                                                        │
  │  - name: v1             ← subset name used in VirtualService    │
  │    labels:                                                        │
  │      version: v1         ← matches pods with label version=v1    │
  │    trafficPolicy:                                                │
  │      loadBalancer: {simple: ROUND_ROBIN}                         │
  │                                                                  │
  │  - name: v2                                                       │
  │    labels: {version: v2}                                         │
  │    trafficPolicy:                                                │
  │      loadBalancer: {simple: LEAST_REQUEST}                       │
  │                                                                  │
  │  PODS IN CLUSTER:                                                │
  │  anihpj-api-abc (version=v1) → subset v1                         │
  │  anihpj-api-def (version=v1) → subset v1                         │
  │  anihpj-api-xyz (version=v2) → subset v2                         │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, subsets enable versioned deployments: v1 (stable API), v2 (canary with new features), v3 (experimental). VirtualService routes 90% to v1, 10% to v2, and header-based routing sends beta users to v3 — all referencing these subset names.

Traffic-I/E/R Q55 of 200
Which load balancing algorithms does Istio support in DestinationRule, and when should you use each?
A) Istio only supports round-robin — other algorithms require EnvoyFilter
B) Istio supports random and round-robin only — least-request is deprecated
C) Istio supports ROUND_ROBIN (default, equal distribution), LEAST_REQUEST (send to pod with fewest in-flight requests — best for variable-duration API calls), RANDOM (purely random), and PASSTHROUGH (no LB, forward to original destination). Also supports consistentHash for session affinity based on HTTP headers/cookies/IP
D) Load balancing is handled by kube-proxy — Istio does not provide its own LB algorithms
✅ Correct Answer: C

ROUND_ROBIN (default, equal distribution for uniform workloads), LEAST_REQUEST (best for variable-duration requests like API calls), RANDOM (simple, no state), PASSTHROUGH (for TCP), and consistentHash (session affinity without cookies). LEAST_REQUEST is ideal for anihpj-api because API calls have varying response times.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Load Balancing Algorithms — When to Use Each
  ┌──────────────────┬─────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ALGORITHM       │  BEST FOR                                    │
  ├──────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  ROUND_ROBIN     │  Uniform workloads (similar request cost)    │
  │  (default)       │  Simple, predictable distribution            │
  ├──────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  LEAST_REQUEST   │  Variable-duration API calls                 │
  │  ⭐ RECOMMENDED  │  Prevents overloading slow pods              │
  │                  │  anihpj-api: /jobs/search vs /jobs/create    │
  ├──────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  RANDOM          │  Stateless microservices, simple setup       │
  ├──────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  PASSTHROUGH     │  TCP passthrough, no L7 LB                   │
  ├──────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  consistentHash  │  Session affinity (same user → same pod)     │
  │  (on header/IP)  │  Sticky sessions without cookies             │
  └──────────────────┴─────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, use LEAST_REQUEST on the anihpj-api DestinationRule -- job search queries are fast, but job creation writes to PostgreSQL and takes longer. LEAST_REQUEST naturally sends traffic to the least-busy API pod, preventing slow pods from being overwhelmed.

Traffic-I/E/R Q56 of 200
What is consistent hashing in DestinationRule and how does it enable session affinity?
A) Consistent hashing maps requests to the SAME backend pod based on a hash of HTTP headers, cookies, or source IP. This provides session affinity (sticky sessions) WITHOUT application-level cookies — the mesh layer ensures the same user always reaches the same pod, useful for stateful workloads and cache locality
B) Consistent hashing ensures all pods receive exactly the same number of requests — it's the same as round-robin
C) Consistent hashing is a security feature that hashes TLS certificates — not related to load balancing
D) Consistent hashing requires an external Redis cluster to store hash mappings — it cannot work with HTTP headers alone
✅ Correct Answer: A

Consistent hashing provides sticky sessions at the mesh layer. Hash on HTTP header (e.g., x-user-id), cookie, or source IP — same hash always maps to same pod. This means you don't need to modify your Django app to use sticky sessions — Envoy handles it transparently.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Consistent Hashing — Same User Always Hits Same Pod
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  DestinationRule: anihpj-api                                     │
  │  trafficPolicy:                                                  │
  │    loadBalancer:                                                 │
  │      consistentHash:                                             │
  │        httpHeaderName: x-user-id   ← hash on user ID header     │
  │                                                                  │
  │  User "alice":  hash("alice")  → Pod A (ALWAYS)                 │
  │  User "bob":    hash("bob")    → Pod B (ALWAYS)                 │
  │  User "charlie":hash("charlie")→ Pod C (ALWAYS)                 │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ✅ Pod A maintains local cache for alice's data                 │
  │  ✅ No Redis or memcached needed for session stickiness          │
  │  ✅ Works even when pods scale up/down (consistent ring)        │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, consistent hashing on x-user-id header means a logged-in user always hits the same API pod. This keeps their session data in local pod cache without needing Redis for session stickiness -- reducing infrastructure complexity while maintaining user experience.

Traffic-I/E/R Q57 of 200
What are connection pool settings in DestinationRule and why are they critical for production?
A) Connection pool settings control the color of the Envoy dashboard — they are cosmetic only
B) Connection pool settings limit the number of concurrent connections (maxConnections), pending requests (http1MaxPendingRequests), and active retries (maxRetries) to a destination. They prevent cascading failures: if a backend slows down, connection limits prevent ALL callers from piling on and exhausting their own resources
C) Connection pools are auto-tuned by Istio based on pod resources — manual configuration is not needed
D) Connection pool settings only apply to gateways — sidecar proxies ignore them
✅ Correct Answer: B

Connection pool settings are your primary defense against cascading failures. If anihpj-db slows down without connection limits, 50 API pods each open 100 connections = 5000 connections overwhelming the database. With limits, excess requests are queued or rejected quickly — protecting the downstream service.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Connection Pool Settings — Preventing Cascading Failures
  WITHOUT LIMITS (cascading failure):        WITH LIMITS (protected):
  ┌────────────────────────────┐             ┌────────────────────────────┐
  │ 50× anihpj-api pods        │             │ 50× anihpj-api pods        │
  │ Each opens 100 connections │             │ Each LIMITED to 10 conns   │
  │ → 5000 connections to DB! │             │ → MAX 500 connections       │
  │ → DB OOM, crashes 💥       │             │ → DB stays healthy ✅       │
  └────────────────────────────┘             └────────────────────────────┘
  
  DestinationRule: anihpj-db
  trafficPolicy:
    connectionPool:
      tcp:
        maxConnections: 100          ← Total TCP connections
      http:
        http1MaxPendingRequests: 50  ← Queue depth
        maxRequestsPerConnection: 10 ← Keep-alive limit
        maxRetries: 3                ← Retry budget

For anihpj/jobpost, connection pools protect the PostgreSQL database. Without limits, 50 API pods each opening 100 connections = 5,000 DB connections overwhelming PostgreSQL. With maxConnections: 100 and http1MaxPendingRequests: 50, excess connections queue -- the job board stays responsive under peak load.

Traffic-I/E/R Q58 of 200
What is outlier detection (circuit breaking) in DestinationRule and how does it protect the mesh?
A) Outlier detection monitors for unusual CPU usage patterns on nodes — it's a node-level feature
B) Outlier detection removes pods from the mesh that have been running for too long — it's a pod lifecycle feature
C) Outlier detection is a security feature that detects malicious traffic patterns and blocks IPs
D) Outlier detection (circuit breaking) monitors pods for consecutive errors (e.g., 5 consecutive 5xx responses). When a pod exceeds the threshold, Envoy ejects it from the load balancing pool for a configurable time (baseEjectionTime). This prevents requests from being sent to failing pods and allows them to recover. After the ejection period, the pod is gradually reintroduced
✅ Correct Answer: D

Outlier detection is Istio's circuit breaker. If a pod returns 5 consecutive 5xx errors, Envoy ejects it from the pool for 30s (default). No requests reach the failing pod. After ejection expires, the pod gets a trial request — if it succeeds, it rejoins the pool.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Outlier Detection — Ejecting Failing Pods Automatically
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  DestinationRule: anihpj-api                                     │
  │  trafficPolicy:                                                  │
  │    outlierDetection:                                             │
  │      consecutive5xxErrors: 5    ← Eject after 5 failures        │
  │      interval: 30s              ← Check window                   │
  │      baseEjectionTime: 60s      ← How long pod stays ejected    │
  │      maxEjectionPercent: 50     ← Never eject >50% of pods      │
  │                                                                  │
  │  Pod v1-A: ❌❌❌❌❌ (5× 500) → EJECTED for 60s                 │
  │  Pod v1-B: ✅✅✅ (healthy) → Stays in pool                     │
  │  Pod v2-A: ❌❌ (2× 500, under threshold) → Still in pool       │
  │                                                                  │
  │  TRAFFIC FLOW: All traffic → v1-B and v2-A (v1-A excluded)      │
  │  After 60s: v1-A gets ONE trial request → if ✅ → rejoins pool  │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, outlier detection protects API consumers from failing pods. If one anihpj-api pod starts returning 500s due to a memory leak, it gets ejected after 5 consecutive failures. Combined with Kubernetes liveness probes, this creates defense-in-depth for service reliability.

For anihpj/jobpost, outlier detection protects API consumers from failing pods. If one anihpj-api pod starts returning 500s due to a memory leak, it gets ejected after 5 consecutive failures — users are routed to healthy pods. The pod has 60s to recover (or be restarted by Kubernetes) before being retried.

Traffic-I/E/R Q59 of 200
What are the TLS settings available in DestinationRule and what does ISTIO_MUTUAL mean?
A) TLS settings only support DISABLE (no TLS) and SIMPLE (one-way TLS) — ISTIO_MUTUAL is not a valid option
B) DISABLE = no TLS, plaintext. SIMPLE = standard one-way TLS (server cert, client trusts CA). MUTUAL = mutual TLS where client presents its own certificate. ISTIO_MUTUAL = Istio-managed mutual TLS using automatically provisioned SPIFFE certificates — this is what Istio's automatic mTLS uses internally
C) ISTIO_MUTUAL is only for ingress gateways — sidecars always use SIMPLE
D) TLS mode must match PeerAuthentication exactly — DestinationRule TLS is a duplicate setting
✅ Correct Answer: B

ISTIO_MUTUAL is the magic behind automatic mTLS. It tells Envoy to use the SPIFFE certificate that istiod's CA automatically provisions. You don't manage certs — Envoy presents its workload identity (spiffe://cluster.local/ns/anihpj/sa/anihpj-api) and the destination Envoy validates it.

📝 Detailed Explanation
TLS Modes in DestinationRule — From Plaintext to Auto-mTLS
  ┌──────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  TLS MODE        │  WHAT IT DOES                                 │
  ├──────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  DISABLE         │  No TLS — plaintext only (⚠️ insecure)       │
  ├──────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  SIMPLE          │  Server presents cert, client trusts CA       │
  │                  │  One-way TLS (like HTTPS websites)            │
  ├──────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  MUTUAL          │  BOTH sides present certs — manual cert mgmt  │
  │                  │  Need to provide clientCertificate, key, CA   │
  ├──────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  ISTIO_MUTUAL ⭐ │  AUTO mTLS using Istio-provisioned SPIFFE    │
  │                  │  No cert management — istiod CA handles all   │
  │                  │  This is what PeerAuthentication STRICT uses  │
  └──────────────────┴──────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, ISTIO_MUTUAL is the standard TLS mode for all internal services. When anihpj-web calls anihpj-api, Envoy automatically presents its SPIFFE certificate and the API Envoy validates it -- all without Django knowing anything about certificates.

For anihpj/jobpost, ISTIO_MUTUAL is the standard TLS mode for all internal services. When anihpj-web calls anihpj-api, the web's Envoy automatically presents its SPIFFE certificate (spiffe://cluster.local/ns/anihpj/sa/anihpj-web) and the API's Envoy validates it — all without Django knowing anything about certificates.

Traffic-I/E/R Q60 of 200
What is locality-aware load balancing and how does it reduce latency and cost in multi-zone deployments?
A) Locality-aware LB prefers sending traffic to pods in the SAME zone/region first. If the local zone has healthy pods, traffic stays local (lower latency, no cross-AZ data transfer costs). If the local zone is degraded, traffic fails over to other zones. Configured via localityLbSetting in DestinationRule with outlier detection as the failover trigger
B) Locality-aware LB always distributes traffic evenly across all zones for maximum resilience
C) Locality-aware LB requires a cloud provider-specific plugin — not available in open-source Istio
D) Locality-aware LB only works for gRPC traffic — HTTP/1.1 is not supported
✅ Correct Answer: A

Locality-aware LB minimizes cross-zone traffic. Requests stay in the same zone whenever possible, reducing latency and eliminating cross-AZ data transfer costs. If the local zone becomes unhealthy (outlier detection triggers), traffic automatically fails over to other zones.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Locality-Aware LB — Stay Local, Fail Over When Needed
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  WITHOUT locality LB: 33% traffic crosses zones (extra $, ms)  │
  │                                                                  │
  │  WITH locality LB: <1% cross-zone (only during failures)       │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ┌──────────────────┐   ┌──────────────────┐                   │
  │  │ ZONE: us-east-1a  │   │ ZONE: us-east-1b  │                   │
  │  │ anihpj-api (v1)   │   │ anihpj-api (v1)   │                   │
  │  │ anihpj-web ───►   │   │                    │                   │
  │  │ (LOCAL traffic)   │   │ (FAILOVER only)    │                   │
  │  └──────────────────┘   └──────────────────┘                   │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
  
  DestinationRule:
  trafficPolicy:
    outlierDetection: {...}        ← Detects zone failures
    localityLbSetting:
      distribute:                   ← Override for failover
      - from: us-east-1a/*
        to: {us-east-1b/*: 100}   ← Fail all to zone-b

For anihpj/jobpost, locality-aware LB minimizes cross-AZ costs. API pods in us-east-1a serve web requests in the same zone -- zero cross-AZ data charges. Only when zone 1a is degraded does traffic fail over to zone 1b automatically.

For anihpj/jobpost, locality-aware LB minimizes cross-AZ costs. API pods in us-east-1a serve requests from the web pod in the same zone — zero latency and zero cross-AZ data charges. Only when zone 1a is completely degraded does traffic fail over to zone 1b.

Traffic-I/E/R Q61 of 200
How do you configure a DestinationRule for the anihpj-api service with LEAST_REQUEST load balancing AND outlier detection that ejects pods after 5 consecutive 5xx errors?
A) You cannot combine LB policy and outlier detection — they are mutually exclusive
B) Configure outlier detection in VirtualService and LB policy in DestinationRule — they must be in separate resources
C) Configure both in a single DestinationRule: set trafficPolicy.loadBalancer.simple: LEAST_REQUEST AND trafficPolicy.outlierDetection.consecutive5xxErrors: 5 with baseEjectionTime. The rule applies to all subsets unless overridden at the subset level
D) Outlier detection requires Prometheus — you must deploy monitoring first
✅ Correct Answer: C

All traffic policies coexist in one DestinationRule. The top-level trafficPolicy applies as default to all subsets; individual subsets can override specific settings. LEAST_REQUEST + outlier detection is the recommended production combination for API services.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Complete anihpj-api DestinationRule — Production Ready
  apiVersion: networking.istio.io/v1beta1
  kind: DestinationRule
  metadata: {name: anihpj-api-dr, namespace: anihpj}
  spec:
    host: anihpj-api
    trafficPolicy:
      loadBalancer:
        simple: LEAST_REQUEST        ← Send to least-busy pod
      connectionPool:
        tcp: {maxConnections: 100}
        http: {http1MaxPendingRequests: 50}
      outlierDetection:
        consecutive5xxErrors: 5      ← Eject after 5 failures
        interval: 30s
        baseEjectionTime: 60s
        maxEjectionPercent: 50
      tls:
        mode: ISTIO_MUTUAL           ← Auto mTLS
    subsets:
    - name: v1
      labels: {version: v1}
    - name: v2
      labels: {version: v2}
      trafficPolicy:                 ← v2 can override!
        loadBalancer: {simple: ROUND_ROBIN}

For anihpj/jobpost, this DestinationRule is the production baseline for anihpj-api. LEAST_REQUEST handles variable-duration API calls, outlierDetection ejects failing pods, connectionPool protects the database, and ISTIO_MUTUAL ensures all traffic is encrypted — four layers of resilience in one resource.

Traffic-I/E/R Q62 of 200
What happens if a DestinationRule references a subset that has no matching pods (e.g., label version: v3 but no pods have that label)?
A) Traffic is silently dropped — no error, no routing
B) Traffic falls back to the default subset or a random subset automatically
C) Istio rejects the DestinationRule — it won't apply if a subset has no matching pods
D) Envoy returns 503 Service Unavailable (no healthy upstream) for requests routed to that subset. The DestinationRule itself is accepted (it's valid YAML), but Envoy has zero endpoints for that subset. Always verify subsets match actual pod labels before deploying VirtualServices that reference them
✅ Correct Answer: D

Empty subsets → 503 errors. The DestinationRule is valid CRD YAML, so kubectl accepts it. But Envoy resolves subsets to pod endpoints — if a subset has zero matching pods, any VirtualService routing to it will cause 503 "no healthy upstream" errors.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Empty Subset → 503 — The Silent Routing Failure
  DestinationRule defines subset v3 (labels: {version: v3})
  But kubectl get pods -n anihpj -l version=v3 → No resources found
  
  VirtualService routes 10% traffic to subset v3:
  → 10% of requests get 503 Service Unavailable!
  
  DEBUG:
  $ istioctl proxy-config cluster anihpj-api-xxx -n anihpj \
      --fqdn anihpj-api -o json | jq '.[].name'
  "outbound|8000|v1|anihpj-api.anihpj.svc.cluster.local"  ← Has endpoints
  "outbound|8000|v2|anihpj-api.anihpj.svc.cluster.local"  ← Has endpoints
  "outbound|8000|v3|anihpj-api.anihpj.svc.cluster.local"  ← 0 ENDPOINTS!
  
  FIX: Label pods or remove the unused subset.

For anihpj/jobpost, this is the #1 canary debugging issue: DestinationRule subset labels must match actual pod labels. Always verify with istioctl proxy-config cluster that each subset has non-zero endpoints before routing production traffic. Empty subset = silent 503s.

For anihpj/jobpost, this is the #1 canary debugging issue: you create a v2 subset, deploy v2 pods, but the label in the DestinationRule (version: v2) doesn't match the pod label (app-version: v2). Always verify with istioctl proxy-config cluster that each subset has non-zero endpoints before sending production traffic.

2.3 Gateway & ServiceEntry — Mesh Entry, Exit & External ServicesQ63-Q80

Traffic-I/E/R Q63 of 200
What is an Istio Gateway resource and how does it differ from a Kubernetes Ingress?
A) Istio Gateway and K8s Ingress are identical — just different YAML syntax for the same thing
B) An Istio Gateway is an L4-L6 load balancer that configures the Envoy proxy at the mesh edge. It defines ports, protocols (HTTP/HTTPS/TLS/TCP/gRPC), and hosts (SNI-based routing). Unlike K8s Ingress (HTTP/HTTPS only), Gateway supports all protocols including raw TCP and mTLS passthrough. It also integrates natively with Istio's VirtualService for L7 routing — Gateway handles L4-L6, VirtualService handles L7
C) Istio Gateway is a replacement for cloud load balancers — it provisions AWS ALB/NLB automatically
D) Istio Gateway is deprecated — use Kubernetes Gateway API instead
✅ Correct Answer: B

Istio Gateway is far more capable than K8s Ingress. It handles all L4 protocols (TCP, TLS, HTTP, HTTPS, gRPC), supports SNI-based host routing, mTLS passthrough, and integrates seamlessly with VirtualService for L7 routing. For anihpj/jobpost, one Gateway handles HTTPS on 443 for jobpost.com while another handles TCP on 5432 for direct database access.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Istio Gateway vs K8s Ingress — Feature Comparison
  ┌─────────────────────┬───────────────────────┬───────────────────┐
  │  FEATURE            │  K8s Ingress          │  Istio Gateway    │
  ├─────────────────────┼───────────────────────┼───────────────────┤
  │  HTTP/HTTPS         │  ✅                   │  ✅               │
  │  TCP                │  ❌ (needs custom)    │  ✅               │
  │  gRPC               │  ❌                   │  ✅               │
  │  mTLS Passthrough   │  ❌                   │  ✅               │
  │  SNI-based routing  │  Limited              │  ✅               │
  │  L7 routing         │  Path-based only      │  ✅ Full VS power │
  │  Traffic splitting  │  ❌                   │  ✅ (via VS)      │
  │  Multiple protocols │  ❌ (per-Ingress)     │  ✅ (per-server)  │
  │  per Gateway         │                       │                   │
  └─────────────────────┴───────────────────────┴───────────────────┘
Traffic-I/E/R Q64 of 200
How do you configure an Istio Ingress Gateway to expose the anihpj job board on HTTPS (port 443) with TLS termination?
A) Create a Gateway resource defining port 443 with protocol HTTPS, hosts (e.g., jobpost.com), and TLS mode SIMPLE with a Kubernetes Secret containing the TLS certificate. Then create a VirtualService bound to this Gateway that routes matching traffic to anihpj-web
B) Run istioctl expose deploy anihpj-web --port=443 --tls — Istio auto-configures everything
C) Add a TLS section to the anihpj-web Deployment — no Gateway needed for HTTPS
D) Create a K8s Ingress with TLS — Istio Gateway cannot handle TLS termination
✅ Correct Answer: A

Gateway + VirtualService = complete HTTPS ingress. Gateway defines the port and TLS config; VirtualService routes traffic to the backend. The TLS cert lives in a Kubernetes Secret referenced by the Gateway.

📝 Detailed Explanation
HTTPS Ingress — Gateway + VirtualService + TLS Secret
  ① CREATE TLS SECRET:
  kubectl create secret tls jobpost-cert -n istio-system \
    --cert=/path/to/tls.crt --key=/path/to/tls.key
  
  ② GATEWAY (L4-L6):
  apiVersion: networking.istio.io/v1beta1
  kind: Gateway
  metadata: {name: jobpost-gateway, namespace: istio-system}
  spec:
    selector: {istio: ingressgateway}
    servers:
    - port: {number: 443, name: https, protocol: HTTPS}
      hosts: [jobpost.com]
      tls:
        mode: SIMPLE
        credentialName: jobpost-cert    ← References K8s Secret
  
  ③ VIRTUALSERVICE (L7):
  apiVersion: networking.istio.io/v1beta1
  kind: VirtualService
  metadata: {name: jobpost-vs, namespace: anihpj}
  spec:
    hosts: [jobpost.com]
    gateways: [istio-system/jobpost-gateway]
    http:
    - route:
      - destination: {host: anihpj-web, port: {number: 8000}}

For anihpj/jobpost, this Gateway + VirtualService pattern exposes the job board securely on https://jobpost.com. The TLS certificate lives in a Kubernetes Secret (jobpost-cert), the Gateway handles TLS termination on port 443, and the VirtualService routes traffic to the Django web pods -- all without touching nginx or Django settings.

What is an Istio Egress Gateway and how do you configure it to route all external API calls from anihpj through a controlled exit point?
A) Egress gateway is automatically deployed with the default profile — no configuration needed
B) Egress gateway only works with cloud load balancers — not available in on-prem clusters
C) Egress gateway replaces all ServiceEntry resources — you no longer need ServiceEntry when using egress
D) An Egress Gateway is a dedicated Envoy proxy at the mesh exit. Configure it by: 1) Deploying the egress gateway pod, 2) Creating a Gateway resource for egress, 3) Creating ServiceEntry for each external host, 4) Creating VirtualService to route external traffic to the egress gateway, 5) Optionally setting meshConfig.outboundTrafficPolicy.mode: REGISTRY_ONLY to force all external traffic through the gateway
✅ Correct Answer: D

Egress Gateway provides controlled, auditable outbound traffic. All external calls from mesh pods route through a dedicated Envoy proxy — enabling host whitelisting (ServiceEntry), metrics on external dependencies, and a single egress IP for firewall rules.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Egress Gateway — Controlled Mesh Exit
  anihpj-api → Egress Gateway → Internet → api.stripe.com
               ┌──────────────────────────┐
               │ Only ServiceEntry hosts  │
               │ ✅ api.stripe.com         │
               │ ❌ unknown-host.xyz      │
               │ 📊 Metrics on all calls  │
               │ 🔒 Single egress IP      │
               └──────────────────────────┘
  
  CONFIG: Gateway (egress) + ServiceEntry + VirtualService
  meshConfig.outboundTrafficPolicy.mode: REGISTRY_ONLY

For anihpj/jobpost, the Egress Gateway ensures the Django API can ONLY call approved external services (Stripe for payments, Mailgun for email notifications). Without it, a compromised API pod could exfiltrate data to any external server. With it, only ServiceEntry-registered hosts are reachable -- zero-trust egress.

Traffic-I/E/R Q66 of 200
How do you configure the Gateway servers field — what do port, protocol, and hosts mean?
A) Gateway servers are only for egress — ingress uses a different configuration format
B) port = the port number the gateway Envoy listens on. protocol = HTTP/HTTPS/TLS/TCP (determines L7 vs L4 handling). hosts = SNI hostnames this server handles (e.g., "*.jobpost.com"). One Gateway can have multiple servers for different ports and hosts
C) The hosts field is optional — if omitted, the gateway handles ALL hosts on that port
D) Gateway servers are auto-configured from VirtualService hosts — manual configuration is not needed
✅ Correct Answer: B

Gateway servers define what the Envoy proxy listens on. port + protocol + hosts = "listen on this port, for this protocol, for these hostnames." Multiple server blocks let one Gateway handle HTTPS on 443 for jobpost.com AND TCP on 5432 for database access.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Gateway Servers — Multiple Ports, Multiple Protocols
  spec:
    selector: {istio: ingressgateway}
    servers:
    - port: {number: 443, name: https-web, protocol: HTTPS}
      hosts: [jobpost.com, www.jobpost.com]
      tls: {mode: SIMPLE, credentialName: jobpost-cert}
    - port: {number: 443, name: https-api, protocol: HTTPS}
      hosts: [api.jobpost.com]
      tls: {mode: SIMPLE, credentialName: api-cert}
    - port: {number: 5432, name: tcp-db, protocol: TCP}
      hosts: [db.jobpost.com]

For anihpj/jobpost, one Gateway serves HTTPS on 443 for jobpost.com web traffic AND a separate server block handles TCP on 5432 for direct database admin access. Each server has its own TLS certificates and host matching -- all from one Gateway resource, one Envoy proxy.

Traffic-I/E/R Q67 of 200
What are the TLS modes in an Istio Gateway and when do you use PASSTHROUGH vs SIMPLE vs MUTUAL?
A) SIMPLE = standard HTTPS (gateway terminates TLS, backend gets plaintext). PASSTHROUGH = gateway forwards TLS untouched to backend based on SNI (backend terminates TLS — needed for mTLS between client and backend). MUTUAL = gateway requires client certificate AND presents server certificate (mTLS at the edge). AUTO_PASSTHROUGH = gateway auto-detects TLS based on SNI
B) PASSTHROUGH is just SIMPLE with a different name — they work identically
C) MUTUAL mode is only for egress — ingress gateways cannot do mutual TLS
D) TLS mode in Gateway is cosmetic — Envoy always terminates TLS regardless of the setting
✅ Correct Answer: A

PASSTHROUGH is critical when the backend needs to handle its own TLS (e.g., the backend does client certificate validation). SIMPLE is the standard HTTPS termination pattern. MUTUAL enables edge mTLS for client authentication at the mesh boundary.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Gateway TLS Modes — Where TLS Terminates
  SIMPLE:                     PASSTHROUGH:                MUTUAL:
  Client ──TLS──► GW ──http──► Backend    Client ──TLS──► GW ──TLS──► Backend
  (GW terminates)             (GW forwards untouched)     (Both sides have certs)
  
  Use SIMPLE for:             Use PASSTHROUGH for:        Use MUTUAL for:
  • Standard HTTPS            • Backend mTLS              • Client cert auth
  • TLS offloading            • SNI-based routing         • Zero-trust edge

For anihpj/jobpost, use SIMPLE TLS mode on the ingress Gateway to terminate HTTPS for jobpost.com -- Envoy handles the TLS handshake, then forwards plain HTTP to Django. For the admin database connection, use PASSTHROUGH mode so TLS is end-to-end from the admin client to PostgreSQL.

Why would you deploy multiple gateways (separate ingress gateway deployments) in the same cluster?
A) Multiple gateways are not supported — only one ingress gateway per cluster
B) Multiple gateways are required for multi-cluster — single-cluster only needs one
C) Separate gateways for different security domains: a public API gateway (internet-facing, with WAF) vs an internal admin gateway (VPC-only, no public IP). Each can have different TLS certificates, different scaling, different resource limits, and run on dedicated nodes
D) Multiple gateways are only needed when you exceed 100 VirtualServices per gateway
✅ Correct Answer: C

Security isolation via separate gateways. Public gateway on internet-facing nodes with WAF; admin gateway on internal-only nodes. Different scaling policies (public: 10 replicas, admin: 2). Different TLS certs (public: Let's Encrypt, admin: internal CA).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Multiple Gateways — Security Domain Isolation
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  PUBLIC GATEWAY (internet-facing)     ADMIN GATEWAY (internal)   │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────┐        ┌──────────────────────┐   │
  │  │ Selector: gw=public       │        │ Selector: gw=admin   │   │
  │  │ Nodes: edge-pool          │        │ Nodes: internal-pool │   │
  │  │ TLS: Let's Encrypt        │        │ TLS: internal CA     │   │
  │  │ WAF/rate-limit: enabled   │        │ Auth: mTLS required  │   │
  │  │ Replicas: 10              │        │ Replicas: 2          │   │
  │  │ Serves: jobpost.com       │        │ Serves: admin.internal│   │
  │  └──────────────────────────┘        └──────────────────────┘   │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, deploy two ingress gateways: a public gateway (internet-facing, with Let's Encrypt certs, 5 replicas) for jobpost.com and a private admin gateway (VPC-only, with internal CA certs, 2 replicas) for the Django admin panel -- different security domains, different scaling, different TLS.

Without vs With ServiceEntry — External Service Visibility
  WITHOUT ServiceEntry:                   WITH ServiceEntry:
  ┌──────────────────────┐               ┌──────────────────────────┐
  │ anihpj-api → Stripe  │               │ anihpj-api → Stripe       │
  │ (direct, no metrics) │               │ ┌──────────────────────┐ │
  │ ⚠️  No observability  │               │ │ Envoy intercepts!     │ │

  │ ⚠️  Can't control     │               │ │ • mTLS origination   │ │
  └──────────────────────┘               │ │ • Policy enforced     │ │
                                          │ └──────────────────────┘ │
                                          │ ✅ Full mesh visibility   │
                                          └──────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, ServiceEntry registers api.stripe.com and smtp.mailgun.net in the mesh registry. Now Envoy tracks every payment API call (rate, latency, errors) and every email sent -- observability for external dependencies that was previously invisible. Combined with REGISTRY_ONLY mode, all other external traffic is blocked.

Traffic-I/E/R Q70 of 200
What is the resolution field in ServiceEntry and when do you use DNS vs STATIC?
A) Resolution only matters for HTTP services — TCP services always use STATIC
B) DNS resolution means "resolve once at creation" — STATIC means "resolve periodically"
C) STATIC resolution is deprecated — all ServiceEntries should use DNS
D) DNS: istiod periodically re-resolves the hostname to IP addresses (good for cloud services with changing IPs like AWS RDS). STATIC: you manually specify fixed endpoints (good for fixed-IP services or when DNS is unreliable). NONE: traffic bypasses Envoy completely for this host
✅ Correct Answer: D

DNS resolution is the go-to for cloud services (RDS, ElastiCache) where IPs change during failover. STATIC is for on-prem services with fixed IPs. DNS mode periodically re-resolves; STATIC uses your hardcoded list.

📝 Detailed Explanation
ServiceEntry Resolution — DNS vs STATIC
  DNS (dynamic):                           STATIC (fixed):
  hosts: [rds-001.aws.com]                hosts: [ldap.internal]
  resolution: DNS                          resolution: STATIC
  location: MESH_EXTERNAL                  endpoints:
  └─► istiod resolves every ~60s          - address: 10.0.1.50
      → IP changes on RDS failover        - address: 10.0.2.50
      → Envoy updated automatically        → Fixed IPs, no DNS lookup

For anihpj/jobpost, use DNS resolution for the AWS RDS ServiceEntry because RDS endpoints change during failover events. istiod re-resolves the hostname every ~60 seconds, automatically updating Envoy endpoints -- no manual intervention needed when AWS shifts the RDS IP.

Traffic-I/E/R Q71 of 200
What does location: MESH_EXTERNAL vs MESH_INTERNAL mean in ServiceEntry?
A) MESH_EXTERNAL = service is OUTSIDE the cluster (e.g., api.stripe.com, AWS RDS). Traffic is routed through egress gateway (if configured). MESH_INTERNAL = service is INSIDE the cluster but NOT part of the mesh (e.g., a non-Istio pod, a VM in the same network). mTLS is NOT used for MESH_EXTERNAL by default
B) MESH_INTERNAL means "inside the pod" — it's for localhost services
C) Location only affects telemetry labels — routing behavior is unchanged
D) MESH_EXTERNAL requires an egress gateway — it won't work without one
✅ Correct Answer: A

MESH_EXTERNAL tells Istio this service is truly external (internet, another DC). MESH_INTERNAL means it's inside the cluster but not in the mesh (non-injected pods, VMs). This affects mTLS behavior and egress routing decisions.

📝 Detailed Explanation
MESH_EXTERNAL vs MESH_INTERNAL
  MESH_EXTERNAL:                          MESH_INTERNAL:
  api.stripe.com (internet)              legacy-app.pod (cluster, no sidecar)
  aws-rds.xxx.rds.amazonaws.com           vm-payroll.internal (VM in same VPC)
  └─► Routed via egress (if configured)  └─► Direct Envoy routing
  └─► No mTLS (external service)         └─► Can use mTLS if service supports
  └─► Metrics: source reported           └─► Metrics: full source+dest

For anihpj/jobpost, mark Stripe as MESH_EXTERNAL (truly outside the cluster) and a legacy payroll VM as MESH_INTERNAL (inside the VPC but not in the mesh). This distinction affects mTLS behavior: external services get source-side metrics only; internal services get full bidirectional metrics.

You created a ServiceEntry but external calls still bypass the mesh. What must you additionally configure to FORCE all external traffic through Istio?

B) ServiceEntry automatically forces all traffic through the mesh — no additional configuration needed

ALLOW_ANY vs REGISTRY_ONLY — Zero-Trust Egress
  ALLOW_ANY (default):                    REGISTRY_ONLY (zero-trust):
  Pods → ANY host ✅                      Pods → api.stripe.com (has SE) ✅
  ServiceEntry = optional                 Pods → unknown.biz (no SE) ❌ BLOCKED
  Security: NONE                          Security: ONLY registered hosts

For anihpj/jobpost, switching from ALLOW_ANY (default) to REGISTRY_ONLY is the single most impactful security hardening step. After the change, only api.stripe.com and smtp.mailgun.net are reachable -- any other external host is blocked at the Envoy level. This implements zero-trust egress without firewall rules.

C) ServiceEntry REGISTERS the external service (makes it known to the mesh). An egress gateway VirtualService then ROUTES traffic for that service through dedicated egress pods. D) ServiceEntry and egress gateway are the same resource with different API versions
