ServiceEntry = "what external services are allowed." Egress Gateway = "how external traffic exits the mesh." Together they form a complete egress control plane: ServiceEntry whitelists hosts, VirtualService routes them through the egress gateway, and the gateway provides a single exit IP with full metrics.

📝 Detailed Explanation
ServiceEntry + Egress Gateway — Complete Egress Control
  anihpj-api → Envoy sidecar → Egress Gateway → Internet
                                  │
               ServiceEntry:       │  Gateway (egress):
               hosts: [stripe]     │  port: 443, protocol: HTTPS
               resolution: DNS     │  hosts: [*]
                                   │
               VirtualService:     │
               hosts: [stripe]     │
               gateways: [egress]  │
               → Route to gateway  │

For anihpj/jobpost, ServiceEntry whitelists which external services are allowed (Stripe, Mailgun), and the Egress Gateway provides the dedicated exit path. All external traffic exits through a single set of pods with a single source IP -- simplifying corporate firewall rules and providing centralized egress metrics.

Traffic-I/E/R Q75 of 200
What is a canary deployment in Istio and how does the 90/10 split work in practice?
A) Canary means deploying code to random pods — you can't control which pods get new code
B) A canary deployment runs the NEW version (v2) alongside the STABLE version (v1), sending a small percentage of traffic to v2. VirtualService splits traffic by weight (90% v1, 10% v2). You monitor v2 error rate and latency. If healthy, increase v2 weight (30% → 50% → 100%). If unhealthy, set v2 weight to 0 — instant rollback
C) Canary requires a separate Kubernetes cluster — you can't run two versions in the same cluster
D) Canary deployment only works with Deployment rolling updates — VirtualService cannot control version routing
✅ Correct Answer: B

Canary with Istio is elegant: deploy v2 pods (separate Deployment), label them version=v2, add v2 subset to DestinationRule, set VirtualService weight to 10% v2. Monitor. Promote or rollback by changing one number. No traffic disruption.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Canary Deployment — Progressive Traffic Shift
  PHASE 1: 90/10 (canary)     PHASE 2: 50/50       PHASE 3: 0/100 (promote)
  ┌──────────────────┐       ┌──────────────────┐  ┌──────────────────┐
  │ v1 ████████████████│       │ v1 ██████████     │  │ v2 ████████████████│
  │ v2 ██             │       │ v2 ██████████     │  │ (v1 scaled down)  │
  └──────────────────┘       └──────────────────┘  └──────────────────┘
  
  ROLLBACK: v2 weight → 0 (instant, no redeploy!)

For anihpj/jobpost, canary deployments are the standard release pattern. Deploy anihpj-api-v2 alongside v1, label pods version: v2, define the v2 subset, set VirtualService weights to 90/10. Monitor v2 error rate in Prometheus for 30 minutes. If healthy, increase to 50/50, then 0/100. Rollback is instant: set v2 weight to 0 -- no redeploy needed.

Traffic-I/E/R Q76 of 200
What is Blue/Green deployment and how does it differ from canary in Istio?
A) Blue/Green is the same as canary — just different terminology
B) Blue/Green requires two clusters — one blue, one green
C) Blue/Green deploys new code to the same pods as the old code — they share resources
D) Blue/Green runs TWO complete environments (blue=current, green=new). Traffic is 100% switched from blue to green INSTANTLY via VirtualService update. Unlike canary (gradual % shift), blue/green is an all-at-once cutover. Rollback is equally instant — switch VirtualService back to blue
✅ Correct Answer: D

Blue/Green = instant full switch. Canary = gradual percentage shift. Blue/Green is simpler but riskier (all users switch at once). Canary is safer but requires monitoring infrastructure. Istio VirtualService makes both trivial — just change the route destination or weight.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Blue/Green vs Canary — Two Deployment Strategies
  BLUE/GREEN (instant switch):            CANARY (gradual shift):
  ┌──────────────────────┐              ┌──────────────────────┐
  │ BEFORE:  blue=100%   │              │ BEFORE: v1=100%      │
  │ AFTER:   green=100%  │              │ PHASE1: v1=90,v2=10 │
  │ (one VirtualService  │              │ PHASE2: v1=50,v2=50 │
  │  update = cutover)   │              │ PHASE3: v2=100%     │
  └──────────────────────┘              └──────────────────────┘
  
  BLUE/GREEN VirtualService change:      CANARY VirtualService change:
  route: [{host: x, subset: blue}]       route: [{subset: v1, weight: 90},
  → route: [{host: x, subset: green}]           {subset: v2, weight: 10}]

For anihpj/jobpost, blue/green is used for major version migrations (e.g., Django 4.2 to 5.0). Deploy the complete green environment, test internally via header-based routing, then switch 100% of traffic from blue to green with a single VirtualService edit. If issues arise, switch back instantly -- both environments stay running.

Traffic-I/E/R Q77 of 200
What is A/B testing (header-based routing) in Istio and how do you implement it?
A) A/B testing requires a third-party analytics tool — Istio cannot route by headers
B) A/B testing routes users to different versions based on HTTP headers (e.g., x-user-group: beta → v2, everyone else → v1). Configure a VirtualService match on headers, route matching users to the experimental version. Unlike canary (random split), A/B testing is deterministic — the same user always sees the same version
C) A/B testing only works with cookies — HTTP headers are not supported
D) A/B testing is the same as canary — both use random weight-based splitting
✅ Correct Answer: B

A/B testing routes specific users to experimental versions based on headers, cookies, or query params. Unlike canary (random), A/B is deterministic — beta testers always hit v2. Istio VirtualService header matching makes this trivial: match on x-user-group header, route to experimental subset.

📝 Detailed Explanation
A/B Testing — Header-Based Deterministic Routing
  VirtualService: anihpj-api
  http:
  - match:
    - headers:
        x-user-group: {exact: "beta"}    ← Beta users
    route: [{subset: v2}]                ← Always v2
  - route: [{subset: v1}]                ← Everyone else → v1
  
  User "alice" (header: x-user-group=beta) → ALWAYS v2
  User "bob"   (no header)                 → ALWAYS v1

For anihpj/jobpost, A/B testing enables testing new UI features with specific user groups. Add x-user-group: beta header to beta testers requests (via browser extension or auth proxy), and VirtualService routes them to the experimental API version. Non-beta users continue on stable v1 -- zero risk of breaking the main user base.

Traffic-I/E/R Q78 of 200
What is traffic mirroring (dark launch) and how is it configured in VirtualService?
A) Traffic mirroring sends a COPY of live requests to a shadow service. The shadow's response is DISCARDED — the client only sees the primary response. Configure via mirror and mirrorPercentage in VirtualService. Perfect for testing new versions with production traffic at zero user risk
B) Traffic mirroring duplicates all traffic to a backup cluster for disaster recovery
C) Traffic mirroring requires the shadow service to respond — otherwise the client gets errors
D) Traffic mirroring is the same as traffic splitting with 50/50 weight
✅ Correct Answer: A

Dark launch = test with real traffic, zero user impact. Envoy sends a copy to the shadow service and discards its response. Users never see shadow responses. Ideal for testing new API versions with production traffic patterns before any user-facing canary.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Traffic Mirroring — Dark Launch to Shadow Service
  VirtualService:
  http:
  - route:
    - destination: {host: anihpj-api, subset: v1}
      weight: 100
    mirror:
      host: anihpj-api
      subset: v2              ← Shadow service
    mirrorPercentage: {value: 100}
  
  REQUEST FLOW:
  client → Envoy ──► v1 (response to client)
                  └─► v2 (response DISCARDED)
  
  🔑 v2 receives production traffic but users NEVER see its responses.
  Monitor v2 metrics for errors/latency before promoting to canary.

For anihpj/jobpost, dark launch the new recommendation engine: mirror 100% of /api/jobs traffic to the ML service while users only see the existing search results. Monitor the ML service latency and error rate under real production load for a week. Only when it proves stable do you route real users to it.

Traffic-I/E/R Q79 of 200
How do you configure TCP traffic management for PostgreSQL connections through Istio?
A) TCP traffic is automatically managed — no VirtualService needed for databases
B) Use the same HTTP VirtualService — TCP and HTTP use identical configuration
C) Use a TCP VirtualService: spec.tcp instead of spec.http. Match by port and source subnet (no URI/header matching). Route to destination. TCP VirtualServices support L4 routing, TLS origination, and basic load balancing but NOT L7 features like retries, fault injection, or header-based routing
D) TCP requires EnvoyFilter — VirtualService only supports HTTP
✅ Correct Answer: C

TCP VirtualService handles L4 protocols (PostgreSQL, MySQL, Redis, MongoDB). Match by port and source subnet only — no URI/header parsing. Simpler than HTTP VS but also less capable. The K8s Service port name MUST start with the protocol prefix (e.g., tcp-postgres).

📝 Detailed Explanation
TCP VirtualService — Layer 4 Traffic Management
  apiVersion: networking.istio.io/v1beta1
  kind: VirtualService
  metadata: {name: anihpj-db-tcp, namespace: anihpj}
  spec:
    hosts: [anihpj-db]
    tcp:                              ← TCP block (not http)
    - match:
      - port: 5432
        sourceSubnet: 10.244.0.0/16  ← Only from pod network
      route:
      - destination:
          host: anihpj-db
          port: {number: 5432}
          subset: v1
  
  ⚠️  K8s Service port name MUST start with protocol:
      name: tcp-postgres  ← NOT "postgres" or "db"

For anihpj/jobpost, PostgreSQL traffic uses a TCP VirtualService because database connections are Layer 4. The K8s Service port MUST be named tcp-postgres (not just "postgres") for Istio to recognize it as TCP. mTLS and metrics still apply -- but no L7 features like header matching or retries.

Traffic-I/E/R Q80 of 200
You deployed a canary for anihpj-api (90% v1, 10% v2) but Kiali shows ALL traffic going to v1. What is the most likely cause?
A) Kiali is broken — restart the Kiali pod
B) Canary only works with header-based routing — weight-based splitting is not supported
C) The VirtualService has incorrect weight syntax — weights must be specified as percentages with % sign
D) The DestinationRule subset labels don't match any pods (e.g., v2 subset has version: v2 but v2 pods have app-version: v2). Envoy silently routes all traffic to v1 because v2 subset has zero endpoints. Verify with istioctl proxy-config cluster <pod> --fqdn anihpj-api -o json — check endpoint counts per subset
✅ Correct Answer: D

Subset label mismatch = silent routing failure. DestinationRule defines v2 with label version=v2, but v2 pods use label app-version=v2. Envoy creates the v2 cluster but finds zero endpoints. VirtualService still sends 10% to v2 — but with 0 endpoints, those requests fail or Envoy silently routes them elsewhere.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Debugging Canary Routing — The Label Mismatch Pattern
  ① CHECK SUBSETS HAVE ENDPOINTS:
  $ istioctl proxy-config cluster anihpj-web-xxx -n anihpj \
      --fqdn anihpj-api.anihpj.svc.cluster.local -o json | \
      jq '.[] | {name: .name, endpoints: (.endpoints | length)}'
  
  {"name":"outbound|8000|v1|anihpj-api", "endpoints": 3}  ← v1 has 3
  {"name":"outbound|8000|v2|anihpj-api", "endpoints": 0}  ← v2 has 0! ❌
  
  ② CHECK POD LABELS vs SUBSET LABELS:
  $ kubectl get pods -n anihpj -l version=v2
  No resources found  ← subset expects label "version=v2"
  
  $ kubectl get pods -n anihpj --show-labels | grep v2
  anihpj-api-v2-xxx  app-version=v2  ← pods have "app-version" ❌
  
  ③ FIX: Align labels
  kubectl label pod anihpj-api-v2-xxx version=v2 -n anihpj
  OR fix the DestinationRule subset label to match actual pod labels

For anihpj/jobpost, when your canary shows 0% traffic reaching v2 despite correct VirtualService weights, ALWAYS check istioctl proxy-config cluster first. The #1 cause is a label mismatch between DestinationRule subset and actual pod labels. Fix the labels and traffic flows correctly -- no VirtualService change needed.

🔄 Category 3: Traffic — Resilience, Shifting & Policies (15%)Q81-Q110

🟡 Making Your Mesh Bulletproof — Resilience, Testing & Deployment Strategies

This category covers the mesh's self-healing capabilities: circuit breaking to eject failing pods, retries to handle transient failures, timeouts to prevent resource exhaustion, and fault injection to test resilience. Plus advanced traffic shifting: canary deployments, A/B testing, blue/green deployments, and traffic mirroring.

3.1 Circuit Breaking, Timeouts & RetriesQ81-Q90

Resilience Q81 of 200
What is circuit breaking (outlier detection) in Istio and how does the ejection process work?
A) Circuit breaking permanently removes a pod from the mesh — the pod must be manually re-added
B) Circuit breaking only applies to ingress gateways — sidecar proxies don't support it
C) Circuit breaking monitors pods for consecutive errors (e.g., 5 consecutive 5xx). When a pod exceeds the threshold, Envoy ejects it from the load balancing pool for baseEjectionTime (default 30s). During ejection, zero requests go to that pod. After ejection expires, the pod gets a trial request; if successful, it rejoins the pool. This prevents cascading failures and gives failing pods time to recover
D) Circuit breaking requires an external monitoring system — Envoy cannot detect pod errors on its own
✅ Correct Answer: C

Circuit breaking is automatic and local to each Envoy. No external monitoring needed — Envoy tracks error responses and ejects pods independently. Each Envoy maintains its own ejection list. This means different Envoys may have ejected different pods based on their unique request history.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Circuit Breaker Lifecycle — Detect, Eject, Trial, Rejoin
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  NORMAL:    Pod A  ✅✅✅ (healthy, serving traffic)            │
  │                                                                  │
  │  DEGRADED:  Pod A  ❌❌❌❌❌ (5 consecutive 5xx errors)         │
  │             → EJECTED for baseEjectionTime (30s)                 │
  │             → All traffic diverted to Pod B and Pod C            │
  │                                                                  │
  │  RECOVERY:  After 30s → Pod A gets ONE trial request            │
  │             → If ✅ → rejoins load balancing pool               │
  │             → If ❌ → ejected again (with exponential backoff)  │
  │                                                                  │
  │  PROTECTION: maxEjectionPercent: 50                              │
  │  → Never eject more than 50% of pods (maintains capacity)       │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, circuit breaking is the automatic safety net. If an anihpj-api pod starts failing due to a database connection issue, Envoy ejects it within seconds. Users are routed to healthy pods while Kubernetes restarts the failing one. This happens per-Envoy, per-pod -- no central controller needed.

Resilience Q82 of 200
What is the difference between consecutive5xxErrors and consecutiveGatewayErrors in outlier detection?
A) consecutive5xxErrors counts ANY 5xx response (500, 502, 503, 504) from the upstream pod. consecutiveGatewayErrors only counts 502 (Bad Gateway), 503 (Service Unavailable), and 504 (Gateway Timeout) — errors that indicate the upstream is unreachable or timing out, not application-level 500s. Gateway errors suggest network/pod failure; 5xx includes application bugs
B) consecutive5xxErrors counts errors for 5 seconds; consecutiveGatewayErrors counts for 5 minutes
C) consecutive5xxErrors applies to sidecars; consecutiveGatewayErrors applies to ingress gateways
D) They are identical — just different names for the same thing in different API versions
✅ Correct Answer: A

consecutive5xxErrors catches application bugs (500 Internal Server Error). consecutiveGatewayErrors catches connectivity issues (502/503/504). Use gateway errors for detecting pod crashes and network failures; use 5xx for detecting application-level degradation.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Error Types — What Triggers Ejection
  ┌─────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ERROR TYPE         │  TRIGGERS                                  │
  ├─────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  500 (Internal)     │  Application bug, unhandled exception      │
  │                     │  → consecutive5xxErrors ✅                 │
  │                     │  → consecutiveGatewayErrors ❌             │
  ├─────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  502 (Bad Gateway)  │  Upstream pod crashed / not listening      │
  │  503 (Unavailable)  │  Upstream overloaded / no healthy endpoints │
  │  504 (Timeout)      │  Upstream too slow / network issue         │
  │                     │  → BOTH error types count these ✅          │
  └─────────────────────┴───────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, use consecutiveGatewayErrors to detect crashed API pods (502/503) and consecutive5xxErrors to detect application bugs (500). Gateway errors suggest the pod is unreachable -- eject quickly. 5xx errors suggest the application is broken -- eject but investigate the code.

Resilience Q83 of 200
How do retries work in Istio VirtualService and what is a retry budget?
A) Retries are unlimited by default — Envoy keeps retrying until the request succeeds
B) Retries only work for idempotent methods (GET, HEAD) — POST/PUT cannot be retried
C) The retry budget limits how many pods can participate in retries simultaneously
D) VirtualService retries specify: attempts (max retry count, e.g., 3), perTryTimeout (timeout per attempt), and retryOn (which status codes trigger retries). The retry budget prevents retry storms: retryPercentage limits what fraction of requests can be retried at once, and minRetriesPerConnection ensures a minimum retry capacity
✅ Correct Answer: D

Retries WITHOUT a budget = cascading failure. If a backend slows down and every caller retries 3 times, you've just tripled the load on an already-struggling service. The retry budget limits the retry ratio to prevent this amplification effect.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Retries + Retry Budget — Preventing Retry Storms
  VirtualService:
  http:
  - retries:
      attempts: 3               ← Max 3 retries per request
      perTryTimeout: 2s         ← Each attempt has 2s timeout
      retryOn: 5xx,connect-failure  ← When to retry
  
  DestinationRule (retry budget):
  trafficPolicy:
    connectionPool:
      http:
        maxRetries: 3           ← Max concurrent retries
  
  WITHOUT BUDGET: 100 req/s + 3 retries each = 400 req/s on backend 💥
  WITH BUDGET: limited retry concurrency → backend protected ✅

For anihpj/jobpost, retries with a budget protect the job board from cascading failures. If PostgreSQL hiccups for 500ms, Envoy retries the API call and the user never notices. But with maxRetries: 3 in the connection pool, retries cannot amplify load beyond 3x -- preventing the thundering herd problem.

Resilience Q84 of 200
What is the difference between request timeout (VirtualService) and connection timeout (DestinationRule)?
A) They are the same — just configured in different resources for historical reasons
B) Request timeout (VirtualService) limits how long Envoy waits for a complete HTTP response after sending the request. If the backend doesn't respond within this time, Envoy returns 504. Connection timeout (DestinationRule) limits how long Envoy waits to establish a TCP connection to the backend. Connection timeout is typically shorter (1-5s); request timeout depends on expected API response time (5-30s)
C) Connection timeout applies to HTTP; request timeout applies to TCP — they handle different protocols
D) Request timeout is deprecated — use connection timeout for both
✅ Correct Answer: B

Connection timeout = "how long to wait for TCP handshake." Request timeout = "how long to wait for HTTP response." Connection timeouts catch dead backends fast (1-2s). Request timeouts prevent slow backends from hogging resources (set based on your SLO, e.g., 10s for an API).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Two Timeouts — TCP Connection vs HTTP Response
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  CONNECTION TIMEOUT (DestinationRule, TCP-level):                │
  │  Envoy → SYN → Backend                                           │
  │  If no SYN-ACK within X seconds → connection FAILED             │
  │  Default: 10s  |  Typical: 1-3s                                  │
  │                                                                  │
  │  REQUEST TIMEOUT (VirtualService, HTTP-level):                   │
  │  Envoy → GET /api/jobs → Backend                                 │
  │  If no HTTP response within Y seconds → 504 Gateway Timeout     │
  │  Default: 0 (disabled)  |  Typical: 5-30s depending on API      │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
  
  DestinationRule:                       VirtualService:
  trafficPolicy:                         http:
    connectionPool:                       - timeout: 15s
      tcp:                                - retries:
        connectTimeout: 3s                  perTryTimeout: 5s

For anihpj/jobpost, set connectTimeout: 2s on the anihpj-db DestinationRule (fast failure if PostgreSQL is unreachable) and timeout: 15s on the anihpj-api VirtualService (long enough for complex job searches, short enough to prevent resource exhaustion). Two separate timeouts, two separate concerns.

Resilience Q85 of 200
How does fault injection work in Istio and what are the two types?
A) HTTP abort: Envoy returns a specified HTTP error code (e.g., 503) to a percentage of requests — simulating backend failures. HTTP delay: Envoy adds a fixed delay to a percentage of requests — simulating network latency or slow backends. Both are configured in VirtualService fault block and are essential for chaos engineering and resilience testing
B) Fault injection replaces pods with intentionally broken ones — it modifies Deployments
C) Fault injection only works with a separate chaos engineering tool like LitmusChaos
D) Fault injection permanently breaks the service — it's not configurable per-request
✅ Correct Answer: A

Fault injection tests your mesh's resilience. Inject 503 errors to verify circuit breakers trigger. Inject delays to verify timeouts work. Both are PER-REQUEST and configurable by percentage — you can test with 10% of traffic while 90% flows normally.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Fault Injection — Chaos Engineering Built Into Istio
  VirtualService fault injection:
  http:
  - fault:
      delay:
        percentage: {value: 50}     ← 50% of requests affected
        fixedDelay: 5s              ← Add 5 second delay
      abort:
        percentage: {value: 10}     ← 10% of requests affected
        httpStatus: 503             ← Return 503 Service Unavailable
  
  TESTING SCENARIO:
  • Inject 5s delay on 50% of /api/search → test client timeouts
  • Inject 503 on 10% of /api/jobs → test retry logic
  • Combine: abort + delay on different routes
  • ✅ Test resilience WITHOUT breaking production code!

For anihpj/jobpost, use fault injection during staging tests: inject 2s delays on 30% of /api/jobs traffic to verify the web frontend handles slow responses gracefully. Inject 503 errors on 5% to verify the CircuitBreakerFallback UI component displays properly. Chaos engineering without chaos engineering tools.

Resilience Q86 of 200
What does maxEjectionPercent protect against in outlier detection?
A) It limits how many pods can be ejected per minute — rate limiting ejections
B) It limits how long a pod can stay ejected — prevents permanent ejection
C) It limits what PERCENTAGE of the total upstream pool can be ejected at once. If set to 50 and you have 10 pods, at most 5 can be ejected simultaneously — even if 8 are returning errors. This prevents a cascading scenario where ALL pods get ejected (due to a shared dependency failure) and the service becomes completely unavailable
D) It limits what percentage of REQUESTS trigger ejection checks — not all requests are evaluated
✅ Correct Answer: C

maxEjectionPercent is the "blast radius limiter." Without it, if all pods share a common dependency that's failing, every pod could be ejected — taking the service completely offline. With maxEjectionPercent: 50, at least half the pods stay in rotation, maintaining partial service.

📝 Detailed Explanation
maxEjectionPercent — Protecting Against Total Outage
  WITHOUT maxEjectionPercent:             WITH maxEjectionPercent: 50
  ┌────────────────────────┐             ┌────────────────────────┐
  │ 10 pods, all share DB   │             │ 10 pods, 5 ejected max  │
  │ DB goes down → all 10   │             │ DB goes down → 5 ejected │
  │   pods return 503       │             │   (error counts rise)    │
  │ → ALL 10 ejected 💥     │             │ 5 pods still serving ✅  │
  │ → Service 100% DOWN     │             │ → 50% capacity remains   │
  └────────────────────────┘             └────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, set maxEjectionPercent: 50 so that even if ALL API pods share a failing database connection, at most HALF are ejected. The service degrades to 50% capacity instead of going completely offline -- users see slower responses, not errors.

Resilience Q87 of 200
What is baseEjectionTime and how does exponential backoff work in outlier detection?
A) baseEjectionTime is the maximum time a pod can be ejected — pods are never ejected longer than this
B) baseEjectionTime is fixed — pods are always ejected for exactly this duration
C) baseEjectionTime only applies to ingress gateways — sidecar proxies use a different mechanism
D) baseEjectionTime is the initial ejection duration (default 30s). If a pod is ejected, returns, and gets ejected AGAIN, the ejection time DOUBLES (exponential backoff): 30s → 60s → 120s → 240s, up to maxEjectionTime. This prevents thrashing — a pod that repeatedly fails is ejected for longer periods
✅ Correct Answer: D

Exponential backoff prevents thrashing. A pod that repeatedly fails after rejoining is kept out longer each time. This gives operators time to notice and investigate, while preventing the pod from degrading overall service quality.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Exponential Ejection Backoff — Progressive Isolation
  EJECTION HISTORY:
  1st ejection:  30s  (baseEjectionTime)
  2nd ejection:  60s  (30 × 2)
  3rd ejection: 120s  (60 × 2)
  4th ejection: 240s  (120 × 2)
  5th ejection: 300s  (capped at maxEjectionTime)
  
  🎯 PURPOSE: A pod that keeps failing needs progressively longer
  recovery time. This prevents "flapping" — rejoining and immediately
  failing again — which degrades the user experience.

For anihpj/jobpost, exponential backoff prevents a flapping API pod from degrading the entire service. A pod that repeatedly fails after rejoining is kept out for progressively longer periods (30s, 60s, 120s), giving operators time to investigate while the remaining pods serve all traffic.

Resilience Q88 of 200
How does the connection pool in DestinationRule prevent cascading failures differently from outlier detection?
A) Connection pool and outlier detection are the same mechanism — just different names
B) Connection pool is PROACTIVE — it limits concurrent connections/requests BEFORE they reach the backend, preventing overload. Outlier detection is REACTIVE — it responds to errors that ALREADY occurred by ejecting pods. Together: connection pool prevents the flood, outlier detection ejects pods that are already drowning
C) Connection pool handles HTTP; outlier detection handles TCP — they operate at different layers
D) Connection pool is configured per-pod; outlier detection is global — different scopes
✅ Correct Answer: B

Connection pool = proactive (throttle before overload). Outlier detection = reactive (eject after failures). Both are essential. Connection pool limits prevent the initial overload; outlier detection handles pods that fail despite the limits.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Proactive vs Reactive Protection
  CONNECTION POOL (proactive):            OUTLIER DETECTION (reactive):
  ┌────────────────────────────┐         ┌────────────────────────────┐
  │ Traffic: 5000 req/s → DB   │         │ Pod B starts returning 503 │
  │ Pool limit: 100 conns      │         │ After 5 consecutive:       │
  │ → 4900 reqs queued/rejected│         │ → Pod B EJECTED            │
  │ → DB never overloaded ✅   │         │ → Traffic only to healthy  │
  └────────────────────────────┘         │   Pod A and Pod C ✅       │
                                          └────────────────────────────┘
  TOGETHER: Connection pool prevents overload → fewer errors
           Outlier detection handles the errors that DO occur

For anihpj/jobpost, connection pools and outlier detection work together: connection pools prevent the initial overload (proactive), outlier detection ejects pods that fail despite limits (reactive). Both are configured in the same DestinationRule -- two lines of defense in one resource.

Resilience Q89 of 200
What happens when you set timeout: 0 (default) in a VirtualService?
A) Infinite timeout — Envoy will wait FOREVER for the backend to respond. This is dangerous in production because a slow or hung backend will consume Envoy worker threads indefinitely, eventually exhausting resources and blocking other requests. ALWAYS set explicit timeouts in production
B) Zero timeout means requests are immediately rejected — nothing gets through
C) Zero timeout means Envoy auto-calculates an optimal timeout based on historical latency
D) Zero timeout uses the connection timeout from DestinationRule as the default
✅ Correct Answer: A

Default timeout of 0 = infinite = DANGER. A hung backend ties up Envoy worker threads forever. Eventually all threads are blocked, and the sidecar stops processing ANY requests — even to healthy backends. Always set explicit timeouts: 5-15s for APIs, 30-60s for long-running operations.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Why timeout: 0 is Dangerous — Thread Exhaustion
  WITHOUT TIMEOUT (timeout: 0):
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Envoy has 2 worker threads                                      │
  │                                                                  │
  │  T0: Thread 1 → GET /api/slow (backend hangs forever)           │
  │      Thread 2 → GET /api/fast (backend responds in 10ms)        │
  │                                                                  │
  │  T1: Thread 1 still waiting...                                   │
  │      Thread 2 → GET /api/fast ✅ (responds)                     │
  │                                                                  │
  │  T5: Thread 1 STILL waiting...                                   │
  │      Thread 2 → GET /api/slow (also hangs!)                     │
  │                                                                  │
  │  💥 BOTH threads blocked → Envoy stops responding entirely      │
  │      → Health checks fail → Pod restarted                       │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
  
  WITH TIMEOUT (timeout: 10s):
  Thread 1 → GET /api/slow → waits 10s → 504 → Thread FREE ✅

For anihpj/jobpost, NEVER leave timeout at 0 (default) on production VirtualServices. If the PostgreSQL query behind /api/jobs/search hangs, Envoy worker threads block forever. Set timeout: 15s on anihpj-api -- if the database is slow, return a 504 to the user instead of hanging indefinitely.

Resilience Q90 of 200
How do you configure retries specifically for 5xx and connection failures on the anihpj-api VirtualService?
A) Retries are enabled by default — no configuration needed for anihpj-api
B) Add retry: true to the VirtualService — all failure types are retried automatically
C) Retries only work at the DestinationRule level — VirtualService cannot configure retries
D) Add retries block in VirtualService HTTP route: attempts: 3, perTryTimeout: 2s, retryOn: "5xx,connect-failure,refused-stream". This tells Envoy to retry up to 3 times when the backend returns 5xx, fails to connect, or resets the TCP stream. Pair with DestinationRule maxRetries in connection pool for budget control
✅ Correct Answer: D

Retries save the user from transient failures. If anihpj-db hiccups for 500ms, a retry to anihpj-api may succeed on the second attempt without the user noticing. But limit retries (3 max) and keep perTryTimeout short (2s) to avoid amplifying load.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Retry Configuration for anihpj-api
  VirtualService: anihpj-api
  http:
  - retries:
      attempts: 3
      perTryTimeout: 2s
      retryOn: "5xx,connect-failure,refused-stream"
    route: [{destination: {host: anihpj-api, subset: v1}}]
  
  DestinationRule: anihpj-api
  trafficPolicy:
    connectionPool:
      http:
        maxRetries: 3    ← Budget: max concurrent retries
  
  FLOW: Request fails with 503 → retry 1 → retry 2 → retry 3
        → If all fail → return 503 to caller
        → If any succeeds → return 200 to caller (user never knew!)

3.2 Traffic Shifting & Advanced Deployment PatternsQ91-Q100

Resilience Q91 of 200
How do you promote a canary from 10% to 100% after verifying it's healthy, and how do you roll back if it's not?
A) Canary promotion requires deleting the v1 Deployment and scaling up v2 — VirtualService weights can't be changed after creation
B) Promote: progressively increase v2 weight in VirtualService (10% → 30% → 50% → 100%), monitoring error rate at each step. Once at 100%, scale down v1 Deployment. Rollback: set v2 weight to 0 — traffic instantly returns to 100% v1. No pod restarts, no DNS changes, just one YAML edit
C) Promotion requires istioctl canary-promote command — it's a CLI-only operation
D) Rollback requires restoring from etcd backup — VirtualService changes are irreversible
✅ Correct Answer: B

Istio makes canary promotion/rollback a one-line VirtualService change. No infrastructure changes, no DNS updates, no pod restarts. The entire canary lifecycle is managed through VirtualService weight edits — the cleanest deployment pattern in cloud-native.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Canary Lifecycle — From 10% to 100% (or Rollback)
  PHASE 1 (canary):  v1:90, v2:10   ← Deploy v2, test with 10%
  PHASE 2 (ramp):    v1:50, v2:50   ← Increasing confidence
  PHASE 3 (promote): v2:100         ← v2 is new stable
  PHASE 4 (cleanup): scale down v1  ← Remove old version
  
  ROLLBACK ANYTIME: v2:0, v1:100    ← Instant, no side effects!
  
  COMMAND (promote): kubectl edit vs anihpj-api -n anihpj
  COMMAND (rollback): same command, change weight to 0

For anihpj/jobpost, the canary lifecycle is managed entirely through VirtualService weight edits. Deploy v2 pods, test with 10%, ramp to 50%, promote to 100%. The entire process takes minutes, not hours. Rollback is equally fast: set v2 weight to 0 and all traffic instantly returns to v1 -- no DNS changes, no pod restarts, no deployment rollbacks.

Resilience Q92 of 200
What metrics should you monitor during a canary deployment to decide whether to promote or rollback?
A) RED Metrics: Rate (requests/s to v2), Errors (5xx rate on v2 vs v1), Duration (p50/p99 latency on v2 vs v1). Also monitor: CPU/memory of v2 pods, application-level error logs, and business metrics (e.g., job applications submitted successfully). If v2 error rate > v1 or p99 latency significantly higher → rollback
B) Only CPU and memory — application metrics are not relevant for canary decisions
C) Canary decisions are manual — no metrics are needed, just wait 24 hours
D) Only the number of pods running — if v2 pods are Running, promote automatically
✅ Correct Answer: A

RED metrics (Rate, Errors, Duration) are the golden signals for canary evaluation. Compare v2 against v1 baseline. Istio's metrics make this easy: filter by destination_version="v2" in Prometheus. If v2 looks healthy across all three signals, promote. If any signal is worse, rollback.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Canary Decision Matrix — RED Metrics
  PROMETHEUS QUERIES:
  Rate:   sum(rate(istio_requests_total{dest="anihpj-api",version="v2"}[5m]))
  Errors: sum(rate(istio_requests_total{dest="anihpj-api",version="v2",
                    response_code=~"5.."}[5m])) / rate(...)
  Latency: histogram_quantile(0.99, 
             sum(rate(istio_request_duration_milliseconds_bucket{
               dest="anihpj-api",version="v2"}[5m])) by (le))
  
  PROMOTE IF:                ROLLBACK IF:
  ✅ Error rate ~= v1        ❌ Error rate > 2× v1
  ✅ p99 latency ~= v1       ❌ p99 latency > 2× v1
  ✅ No crash loops          ❌ v2 pods restarting

For anihpj/jobpost, monitor canary health with Prometheus queries filtered by destination_version="v2". Compare error rate, p99 latency, and request rate against the v1 baseline. If v2 error rate spikes above 1% while v1 stays at 0.1%, rollback immediately. Istio metrics make this comparison trivial -- both versions report independently.

Resilience Q93 of 200
How does locality-aware load balancing with failover work in Istio?
A) Locality-aware LB always sends traffic to the nearest zone even if it's unhealthy
B) Locality-aware LB requires a cloud load balancer — it doesn't work within Istio directly
C) Locality-aware LB uses outlier detection as the health signal AND localityLbSetting for priority. Traffic prefers pods in the same zone/region (lower latency, no cross-AZ cost). If the local zone's pods are all ejected (unhealthy), traffic fails over to other zones. You can also configure weighted distribution across zones via distribute rules
D) Locality-aware LB is only for multi-cluster — single-cluster multi-zone doesn't benefit from it
✅ Correct Answer: C

Locality LB + outlier detection = cost-optimized resilience. Stay local when healthy (no cross-AZ cost), fail over when degraded. This is how you build multi-zone Istio deployments that minimize latency and cloud costs while maintaining availability.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Locality-Aware LB with Failover
  NORMAL (all healthy):                   FAILOVER (zone-a degraded):
  ┌────────────────┐                     ┌────────────────┐
  │ ZONE-A (local) │                     │ ZONE-A ❌ (all ejected) │
  │ anihpj-web →   │                     │ anihpj-web ─────────┐  │
  │   anihpj-api-a │ 100% local          │                    │  │
  └────────────────┘                     └────────────────────│──┘
  ┌────────────────┐                     ┌────────────────┐   │
  │ ZONE-B          │                     │ ZONE-B          │◄──┘
  │ anihpj-api-b   │  0% (standby)       │ anihpj-api-b   │ 100%
  └────────────────┘                     └────────────────┘
  
  Config: outlierDetection + localityLbSetting
  Result: Zero cross-AZ cost in normal ops, automatic failover

For anihpj/jobpost in a multi-AZ cloud deployment, locality-aware LB keeps API traffic within the same availability zone. Web pods in us-east-1a call API pods in us-east-1a -- zero cross-AZ latency and zero data transfer costs. Only when all zone-1a API pods are ejected does traffic fail over to zone-1b.

Resilience Q94 of 200
How do you configure HTTP redirect in a VirtualService and how does it differ from HTTP rewrite?
A) Redirect and rewrite are identical — both change the URL transparently
B) Redirect: Envoy returns 301/302 to the CLIENT with a new URL — the client's browser URL bar changes and it makes a new request. Rewrite: Envoy changes the URL INTERNALLY before forwarding to the backend — the client never sees the new URL. Redirect is used for URL changes visible to users; rewrite is for transparent API versioning
C) Redirect only works with GET requests; rewrite works with all HTTP methods
D) Redirect is a Gateway feature; rewrite is a VirtualService feature
✅ Correct Answer: B

Redirect changes the client's URL (301/302). Rewrite changes the backend URL transparently. Use redirect for permanent URL migrations (HTTP→HTTPS, old→new domain). Use rewrite for API versioning (/v2/users → /users) without exposing the internal path to clients.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Redirect vs Rewrite — Client-Visible vs Transparent
  REDIRECT (client sees new URL):          REWRITE (client unaware):
  Client: GET /old-path                    Client: GET /api/v2/users
  Envoy: 301 → Location: /new-path        Envoy: rewrites to /users
  Browser: GET /new-path                   Backend: GET /users
  URL bar: /new-path ✅                    URL bar: /api/v2/users (unchanged)
  
  VirtualService redirect:                VirtualService rewrite:
  http:                                   http:
  - match: [{uri: {exact: /old}}]        - match: [{uri: {prefix: /v2}}]
    redirect:                              rewrite: {uri: /}
    uri: /new                              route: [{host: api-v2}]
    authority: new.example.com

For anihpj/jobpost, use HTTP redirect to permanently move jobpost.com to the new domain jobs.anihpj.com (301 redirect), or to enforce HTTPS by redirecting HTTP:80 to HTTPS:443. Use URI rewrite for API versioning: external clients call /api/v2/jobs but the backend Django receives /jobs -- transparent versioning without backend changes.

Resilience Q95 of 200
Why would you combine fault injection delays with circuit breaking in a resilience test?
A) Inject delays to simulate a slow backend → verify that REQUEST TIMEOUTS trigger properly (not hanging forever). Combine with circuit breaking to verify that after timeouts, the circuit OPENS and the slow pod is ejected. This validates the FULL resilience chain: delay → timeout → circuit open → pod ejection → failover to healthy pods
B) Fault injection and circuit breaking cannot be used together — they conflict
C) Delays make circuit breakers close faster — they accelerate the recovery process
D) Combining them is only useful for gRPC — HTTP services don't need both
✅ Correct Answer: A

The full resilience test: inject 5s delay → request timeout fires (2s) → Envoy retries (to another pod) → original pod accumulates timeout errors → outlier detection ejects it → traffic flows to healthy pods only. This validates your ENTIRE resilience configuration in one test.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Full Resilience Test — Delay + Timeout + Circuit Breaker
  TEST SETUP:
  VirtualService (fault injection): 5s delay on 100% of /api/test
  VirtualService (timeout): 2s request timeout
  DestinationRule (circuit breaker): eject after 5 consecutive errors
  
  TEST FLOW:
  ① Request arrives → Envoy injects 5s delay before forwarding
  ② Backend takes 5s to respond → Envoy timeout fires at 2s → 504
  ③ 504 counts toward consecutive errors
  ④ After 5× 504s → Pod EJECTED (circuit breaker)
  ⑤ Subsequent requests → routed to healthy pods ✅
  
  VALIDATED: timeout → error counting → circuit breaker ejection

For anihpj/jobpost, run a full resilience test before every production release: inject delays to verify timeouts fire, inject aborts to verify circuit breakers eject, then check that healthy pods take over. This validates your entire resilience configuration -- timeouts, retries, circuit breakers, and failover -- in one automated test.

Resilience Q96 of 200
What is the connection pool maxRequestsPerConnection setting and why is it important for HTTP/1.1 and HTTP/2?
A) It limits total requests per pod lifetime — after the limit, the pod is restarted
B) It only matters for HTTP/2 — HTTP/1.1 doesn't support connection reuse
C) It's a security setting to prevent DDoS — not related to performance
D) It limits how many requests can be sent over a SINGLE HTTP connection before Envoy closes it. For HTTP/1.1 (one request at a time per connection), setting this forces connection rotation to prevent head-of-line blocking. For HTTP/2 (multiplexed), higher values improve connection reuse. Setting it to 1 effectively disables keep-alive
✅ Correct Answer: D

maxRequestsPerConnection controls connection reuse. HTTP/1.1: set to 1 to force new connections (avoids head-of-line blocking). HTTP/2: set higher (100+) to leverage multiplexing. Tuning this balances connection overhead vs. request distribution fairness.

📝 Detailed Explanation
maxRequestsPerConnection — HTTP/1.1 vs HTTP/2
  HTTP/1.1 (one request at a time per connection):
  maxRequestsPerConnection: 1
  → Each request = new TCP connection
  → Higher overhead BUT better load distribution
  → No head-of-line blocking
  
  HTTP/2 (multiplexed, many requests per connection):
  maxRequestsPerConnection: 100
  → 100 requests over one TCP connection
  → Lower overhead, better connection reuse
  → BUT: one slow request can block others on same connection
  
  DEFAULT: 0 (unlimited — connection stays open forever)

For anihpj/jobpost, set maxRequestsPerConnection: 1 for HTTP/1.1 connections to the Django web pods to avoid head-of-line blocking (one slow request blocking others on the same connection). For internal gRPC connections, set it higher (100+) to leverage HTTP/2 multiplexing and reduce connection overhead.

Resilience Q97 of 200
How does Istio handle gRPC traffic management differently from HTTP/1.1?
A) gRPC and HTTP/1.1 are handled identically — Istio makes no distinction
B) gRPC uses HTTP/2 as transport, so Istio can apply L7 routing (match by gRPC method path, headers). But gRPC has SPECIAL retry semantics: retryOn: "cancelled,deadline-exceeded,internal,resource-exhausted,unavailable" — only certain gRPC status codes are safe to retry. Also, gRPC streams require HTTP/2 connection-level settings in DestinationRule
C) gRPC requires a separate Istio addon — it's not supported natively
D) gRPC only works through gateways — sidecar proxies only handle HTTP/1.1
✅ Correct Answer: B

gRPC uses HTTP/2, which Istio natively supports. Key difference: gRPC status codes map to specific failure modes — only retry on UNAVAILABLE (transient), not INTERNAL (application bug). gRPC streams require HTTP/2 connection pool settings and proper stream timeout configuration.

📝 Detailed Explanation
gRPC Traffic Management in Istio
  VirtualService for gRPC:
  http:
  - match:
    - uri: {prefix: /jobpost.JobService/}  ← gRPC method path
    route: [{destination: {host: anihpj-api, subset: v2}}]
    retries:
      attempts: 3
      perTryTimeout: 2s
      retryOn: "cancelled,deadline-exceeded,unavailable"
      # NOT internal, data-loss, invalid-argument
  
  DestinationRule for gRPC:
  trafficPolicy:
    connectionPool:
      http:
        http2MaxRequests: 1000        ← HTTP/2 stream limit
        maxRequestsPerConnection: 100

For anihpj/jobpost, gRPC is used for internal service-to-service communication (e.g., between the web and API services). Istio handles gRPC routing natively: match by gRPC method path like /jobpost.JobService/ListJobs, retry only on UNAVAILABLE (transient) not INTERNAL (code bug), and configure HTTP/2 connection pool settings for streaming performance.

Resilience Q98 of 200
What is the Sidecar resource in Istio and how does it improve performance in large meshes?
A) The Sidecar resource scopes what a sidecar proxy knows about. By default, every Envoy knows about ALL services in the mesh (all clusters, all endpoints). At scale (1000+ services), this consumes massive Envoy memory. The Sidecar resource limits Envoy's scope to only the services this workload actually communicates with — dramatically reducing memory and CPU usage
B) The Sidecar resource creates an additional sidecar container for high-traffic pods
C) The Sidecar resource replaces the Envoy proxy with a lightweight alternative
D) The Sidecar resource is only for ingress gateways — regular sidecars cannot be scoped
✅ Correct Answer: A

Sidecar resource = Envoy memory optimization. Without it, an Envoy in a 500-service mesh holds ALL 500 clusters and all their endpoints — ~500MB+ RAM. With Sidecar scoping to "only the 5 services this workload talks to," memory drops to ~50MB. Critical at scale.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Sidecar Resource — Scoping Envoy Configuration
  WITHOUT Sidecar (default):              WITH Sidecar scoping:
  ┌────────────────────────────┐         ┌────────────────────────────┐
  │ Envoy knows ALL 500 services│         │ Envoy knows ONLY 5 services │
  │ • 500 clusters              │         │ • anihpj-api                │
  │ • 5000+ endpoints           │         │ • anihpj-db                 │
  │ • 500 listeners             │         │ • anihpj-redis              │
  │ • ~500MB RAM                │         │ • istiod                    │
  │ • Slow config updates       │         │ • prometheus                │
  └────────────────────────────┘         │ • ~50MB RAM ✅              │
                                          │ • Fast config updates ✅    │
  Sidecar resource:                      └────────────────────────────┘
  apiVersion: networking.istio.io/v1beta1
  kind: Sidecar
  metadata: {name: anihpj-web-scope, namespace: anihpj}
  spec:
    egress:
    - hosts: [anihpj/*, istio-system/*]  ← Only these services

For anihpj/jobpost, as the mesh grows to 50+ microservices, use the Sidecar resource to scope each workload Envoy to only the 5-10 services it actually communicates with. The anihpj-web Envoy only needs to know about anihpj-api, istiod, and Prometheus -- not all 50 services. This reduces Envoy memory from ~200MB to ~30MB per pod.

Test your resilience, don't just configure it.

A) Just use circuit breaking — it handles everything from slow responses to crashes
B) Use retries + infinite timeout — let the system keep trying until it works

No single feature covers all failures. Timeouts prevent resource exhaustion from hung backends. Retries handle transient network blips. Circuit breakers isolate consistently failing pods. Resilience Q101 of 200
How do you configure percentage-based fault injection to test how your service handles partial degradation — where only a FRACTION of requests experience delays or aborts?
A) Use the percentage.value field in VirtualService fault block. Set delay.percentage.value: 20 to add latency to 20% of requests, or abort.percentage.value: 10 to return HTTP errors for 10%. This tests partial degradation — 80% of users see normal performance while 20% experience the injected condition
B) Fault injection always affects ALL traffic — percentage-based injection requires EnvoyFilter
C) Percentages are configured in DestinationRule, not VirtualService — fault injection doesn't support per-request percentages
D) Percentage-based injection requires deploying a separate chaos engineering tool — Istio only supports all-or-nothing fault injection
✅ Correct Answer: A

Percentage-based fault injection lets you test resilience on a fraction of traffic while most users are unaffected. This is production-safe chaos engineering: inject 5s delay on 10% of /api/search requests to verify the web frontend timeout/retry logic works, while 90% of users see normal response times.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Percentage-Based Fault Injection — Production-Safe Testing
  VirtualService: anihpj-api
  http:
  - fault:
      delay:
        percentage: {value: 10}    ← 10% of requests affected
        fixedDelay: 5s             ← 5 second delay injected
      abort:
        percentage: {value: 5}     ← 5% of requests affected
        httpStatus: 503            ← Return 503 Service Unavailable
    route: [{destination: {host: anihpj-api, subset: v1}}]
  
  TRAFFIC FLOW (100 req/s):
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  85 req/s → Normal (no fault) → v1 responds in 200ms ✅         │
  │  10 req/s → 5s DELAY injected → v1 responds in 5.2s ⚠️         │
  │   5 req/s → 503 ABORT injected → client sees error ❌           │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
  
  🔑 90% of users unaffected — you can run this in production
     to continuously validate resilience without impacting UX.

For anihpj/jobpost, use percentage-based fault injection in staging first: inject 500ms delays on 20% of /api/jobs requests to verify the web UI shows loading spinners correctly. Then in production, inject tiny percentages (1-2%) to continuously verify circuit breakers and retry logic work under real conditions.

Resilience Q102 of 200
What is a retry budget and how does retryPercentage in the connection pool prevent retry storms?
A) A retry budget limits total retries across the entire mesh — it's a global istiod setting
B) Retry budget only applies to gateways — sidecar proxies retry without limits
C) The retry budget increases retry attempts for high-priority requests — it amplifies retries
D) The retry budget (configured via connectionPool.http.maxRetries in DestinationRule) limits concurrent retries. Without it, if a backend slows down and 100 callers each retry 3 times, you get 400 requests — 4x amplification. With maxRetries: 10, only 10 retries can be in-flight at once, preventing the thundering herd from overwhelming an already-struggling backend
✅ Correct Answer: D

The retry budget is the critical safety valve. VirtualService says "retry up to 3 times per request." The connection pool budget says "but only N retries can be active simultaneously." Without the budget, retries amplify load during degradation — turning a slow backend into a completely crashed one.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Retry Budget — Preventing the Thundering Herd
  WITHOUT BUDGET (retry storm):            WITH BUDGET (controlled):
  ┌────────────────────────────┐          ┌────────────────────────────┐
  │ 100 req/s → anihpj-db slow │          │ 100 req/s → anihpj-db slow │
  │ Each caller retries 3×     │          │ maxRetries: 10 active      │
  │ → 400 req/s on DB! 💥      │          │ → 110 req/s on DB ✅       │
  │ → DB crashes completely    │          │ → DB degrades gracefully   │
  │ → Cascade failure          │          │ → Some users see latency   │
  └────────────────────────────┘          └────────────────────────────┘
  
  DestinationRule: anihpj-db
  trafficPolicy:
    connectionPool:
      http:
        maxRetries: 10   ← Budget: max concurrent retries
  VirtualService: anihpj-api
  http:
  - retries:
      attempts: 3        ← Max retries PER request
      perTryTimeout: 2s

For anihpj/jobpost, set maxRetries: 10 on the anihpj-db DestinationRule. If PostgreSQL slows down, only 10 additional retry requests can be in-flight simultaneously — preventing the API tier from overwhelming the database with retry amplification during an already-degraded state.

Resilience Q103 of 200
What is the difference between perTryTimeout and request timeout in Istio retry configuration?
A) They are identical — perTryTimeout is just an alias for request timeout in older API versions
B) perTryTimeout (in the retries block) limits each INDIVIDUAL retry attempt — if you retry 3 times with perTryTimeout: 2s, each attempt gets 2s (total up to 6s). Request timeout (in the route block) limits the ENTIRE operation including all retries — if timeout: 5s, all retries must complete within 5s total. perTryTimeout must be LESS than the request timeout
C) perTryTimeout is for gRPC only — HTTP uses request timeout exclusively
D) Request timeout replaces perTryTimeout when retries are configured — they cannot coexist
✅ Correct Answer: B

perTryTimeout × attempts ≤ request timeout. If perTryTimeout is 2s and attempts is 3, the total could be 6s — so your request timeout must be at least 6s. If request timeout is 5s, only 2 retries can complete (2s + 2s + 2s = 6s > 5s — third retry times out).

📝 Detailed Explanation
perTryTimeout vs Request Timeout — Nested Time Limits
  VirtualService: anihpj-api
  http:
  - timeout: 10s              ← TOTAL time for request + all retries
    retries:
      attempts: 3              ← Up to 3 retries
      perTryTimeout: 2s        ← Each attempt gets 2s max
    route: [{destination: {host: anihpj-api}}]
  
  TIMELINE (successful on 2nd retry):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ Attempt 1: ████ (2s, failed with 503)                │
  │ Retry 1:   ████ (2s, failed with 503)                │
  │ Retry 2:   ██░░ (1.5s, SUCCESS!)                     │
  │ Total: 5.5s < 10s timeout ✅                          │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
  
  RULE: perTryTimeout × (1 + attempts) ≤ timeout
        2s × (1 + 3) = 8s ≤ 10s ✅

For anihpj/jobpost, set timeout: 10s and perTryTimeout: 2s with attempts: 3. Each API call to PostgreSQL gets up to 3 tries at 2s each, with an absolute ceiling of 10s total — users wait at most 10 seconds for a response before getting a definitive error.

Resilience Q104 of 200
What is retryOn and which HTTP/gRPC status codes should you configure for safe retries?
A) retryOn should include ALL 5xx codes — every server error is safe to retry
B) retryOn only works with gRPC — HTTP retries are automatic and cannot be configured
C) retryOn specifies which conditions trigger a retry: 5xx (all server errors), connect-failure (TCP connection failed), refused-stream (TCP reset), cancelled, deadline-exceeded, unavailable (gRPC only), resource-exhausted (gRPC only). NEVER retry on 4xx (client errors — your request is wrong, retrying won't help)
D) retryOn is a Gateway-only setting — sidecar proxies use fixed retry logic
✅ Correct Answer: C

Safe retries = only on transient failures. 5xx means the server failed but may recover. connect-failure means the network blipped. 4xx means YOUR request is wrong — retrying with the same request will get the same 4xx. Always exclude 4xx from retryOn.

📝 Detailed Explanation
retryOn — Which Errors Are Safe to Retry
  ✅ SAFE TO RETRY (transient failures):
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  5xx              — Server error (may recover)                   │
  │  connect-failure  — TCP connection failed (network blip)        │
  │  refused-stream   — TCP connection reset                        │
  │  cancelled        — Request cancelled by caller                 │
  │  deadline-exceeded— gRPC timeout                                │
  │  unavailable      — gRPC: service temporarily unavailable       │
  │  resource-exhausted— gRPC: rate limited, may recover            │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
  
  ❌ NOT SAFE TO RETRY (client errors):
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  4xx              — Client error (your request is wrong)         │
  │  internal          — gRPC: server bug (won't recover)           │
  │  data-loss         — gRPC: data corruption                      │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
  
  VirtualService:
  retries:
    retryOn: "5xx,connect-failure,refused-stream"

For anihpj/jobpost, configure retryOn: "5xx,connect-failure,refused-stream" on the anihpj-api VirtualService. If PostgreSQL momentarily drops a connection, Envoy transparently retries — the user's job application submission succeeds without them knowing about the transient failure.

Resilience Q105 of 200
How do circuit breakers, retries, and timeouts work together as a layered resilience strategy?
A) They are alternatives — choose one: circuit breaker OR retries OR timeouts
B) They are configured in sequence: timeouts fire first, then retries, then circuit breakers — only one activates per request
C) Circuit breakers make retries and timeouts unnecessary — just use circuit breaking for all resilience
D) They are layered defense: Timeouts (Layer 1) prevent waiting forever on hung backends. Retries (Layer 2) handle transient failures within the timeout window. Circuit breakers (Layer 3) detect consistently failing backends and eject them. Together: timeout catches the hang, retry gives it a second chance, circuit breaker isolates it if it keeps failing
✅ Correct Answer: D

The three layers work in concert. Timeout = "don't wait forever." Retry = "try again, maybe it was a blip." Circuit breaker = "this backend keeps failing, stop sending requests." Each layer handles a different failure mode; together they create defense-in-depth.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Layered Resilience — Timeout → Retry → Circuit Breaker
  REQUEST FLOW WITH ALL THREE LAYERS:
  
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ ① Request sent to anihpj-api pod                                │
  │                                                                  │
  │ ② TIMEOUT CHECK: "Has 10s elapsed since request started?"       │
  │    ├─ YES → 504 Gateway Timeout (stop, don't retry)             │
  │    └─ NO → continue                                              │
  │                                                                  │
  │ ③ RESPONSE: 503 Service Unavailable                             │
  │                                                                  │
  │ ④ RETRY CHECK: "Is 503 in retryOn list? Attempts remaining?"    │
  │    ├─ YES → RETRY (go back to step ①, attempts counter +1)      │
  │    └─ NO → Return error to caller                               │
  │                                                                  │
  │ ⑤ CIRCUIT BREAKER CHECK: "Did this pod return 5 consecutive     │
  │    5xx errors?"                                                  │
  │    ├─ YES → EJECT pod for 60s (all subsequent requests skip it) │
  │    └─ NO → Pod stays in pool                                    │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, configure all three layers on the anihpj-api service: timeout=10s (prevents hanging), retries=3 with perTryTimeout=2s (handles blips), outlierDetection with consecutive5xxErrors=5 (ejects failing pods). This provides complete resilience coverage for the job board API.

Resilience Q106 of 200
How do TCP retries differ from HTTP retries in Istio, and what retry capabilities does TCP have?
A) TCP VirtualServices support only maxConnectAttempts and basic connection retries — they retry at the TCP connection level (SYN/SYN-ACK). TCP has NO concept of HTTP status codes, so retryOn, perTryTimeout, and status-code-based retries are NOT available. TCP retries are simpler but less granular than HTTP retries
B) TCP and HTTP retries are identical — both support the same retryOn conditions
C) TCP supports MORE retry options than HTTP because it's lower-level
D) TCP traffic cannot be retried at all — retries are HTTP-only
✅ Correct Answer: A

TCP retries are connection-level only. If the SYN to PostgreSQL fails, Envoy retries the TCP handshake. But it cannot retry on "query returned an error" because TCP has no visibility into application-level protocol. For database traffic, TCP retries handle network blips but not application errors.

📝 Detailed Explanation
TCP Retries — Connection-Level Only
  TCP VirtualService retries:              HTTP VirtualService retries:
  ┌────────────────────────────┐          ┌────────────────────────────┐
  │ tcp:                       │          │ http:                       │
  │ - match: [{port: 5432}]   │          │ - retries:                  │
  │   retries:                 │          │     attempts: 3             │
  │     attempts: 3            │          │     perTryTimeout: 2s       │
  │                            │          │     retryOn: 5xx,...        │
  │ Retries on:                │          │                             │
  │ ✅ TCP connect failure     │          │ Retries on:                 │
  │ ❌ Application errors      │          │ ✅ HTTP status codes        │
  │ ❌ Query timeouts          │          │ ✅ Connect failures         │
  │ ❌ Status-code based       │          │ ✅ Per-try timeouts         │
  └────────────────────────────┘          └────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, configure TCP retries on the PostgreSQL VirtualService with attempts: 3 to handle transient network issues. But application-level database errors (query timeouts, constraint violations) cannot be retried at the TCP layer — those must be handled in the Django application code.

Resilience Q107 of 200
How do you inject HTTP abort faults to test that your CircuitBreakerFallback UI component correctly renders when the API returns 503 errors?
A) You cannot inject specific HTTP status codes — fault injection always returns 500
B) Deploy a broken version of the API that returns 503 — Istio cannot simulate errors
C) Configure VirtualService fault.abort with httpStatus: 503 and a percentage. For testing fallback UI, inject 503 on 100% of requests temporarily. For production resilience validation, inject on 1-5%. Envoy returns the specified HTTP status directly — the backend never receives the request, so you can test error paths without deploying broken code
D) HTTP abort only works with 500 and 503 — other status codes like 504 or 429 cannot be injected
✅ Correct Answer: C

HTTP abort injection is the cleanest way to test error handling. Envoy returns the error directly without forwarding to the backend — you can test every error state (503, 504, 500, 429) without deploying broken code or taking down services. Perfect for frontend fallback UI testing.

📝 Detailed Explanation
HTTP Abort Fault Injection — Testing Error States
  VirtualService: anihpj-api (staging)
  http:
  - fault:
      abort:
        percentage: {value: 100}   ← Affects ALL requests (testing)
        httpStatus: 503            ← Return 503 Service Unavailable
    route: [{destination: {host: anihpj-api}}]
  
  TESTING SCENARIOS:
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  STATUS │  TEST WHAT                                            │
  ├─────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  503    │  CircuitBreakerFallback component → "Try again later" │
  │  504    │  Timeout fallback → "Request timed out"              │
  │  500    │  Generic error page → "Something went wrong"         │
  │  429    │  Rate limit UI → "Too many requests, please wait"    │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
  
  🔑 Backend NEVER receives the request — safe to test in any env

For anihpj/jobpost, use HTTP abort injection in the staging environment to validate every error state: inject 503 to test the "service unavailable" banner, 504 to test timeout messaging, 500 to test the generic error page. Test all at 100% in staging, then run at 1% in production for continuous validation.

Resilience Q108 of 200
How can you use Istio fault injection to implement chaos engineering without a separate chaos tool?
A) Istio cannot do chaos engineering — you must use LitmusChaos or Gremlin
B) Combine VirtualService fault injection (delays + aborts at configurable percentages) with circuit breakers and retries. Run progressive chaos experiments: inject increasing delays on the API, verify timeouts fire and circuit breakers eject, then verify the system recovers when injection stops. This validates the ENTIRE resilience chain using only Istio CRDs — no external tools needed
C) Chaos engineering requires killing pods — fault injection only simulates, it doesn't test real failures
D) Fault injection is limited to 50% of traffic — you cannot run full-scale chaos experiments
✅ Correct Answer: B

Istio's built-in fault injection is a complete chaos engineering toolkit. Inject delays to simulate slow backends, inject aborts to simulate failures, combine with circuit breakers to validate recovery. All through VirtualService YAML — no additional tools, no pod killing, no infrastructure changes.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Chaos Engineering with Istio — Progressive Experiment
  EXPERIMENT: Validate resilience of job board API
  
  PHASE 1 (mild):   Inject 1s delay on 10% of requests
                    → Verify: users see slightly slower responses
                    → Circuit breakers: NOT triggered ✅
  
  PHASE 2 (medium): Inject 3s delay on 30% of requests
                    → Verify: timeouts fire, retries activate
                    → Circuit breakers: some pods ejected ⚠️
  
  PHASE 3 (severe): Inject 5s delay on 50% + 503 abort on 10%
                    → Verify: circuit breakers eject failing pods
                    → Healthy pods serve 100% of remaining traffic
                    → Users see degraded but not broken service ✅
  
  PHASE 4 (recovery): Stop all fault injection
                    → Verify: ejected pods rejoin pool
                    → All metrics return to baseline ✅

For anihpj/jobpost, run a weekly chaos experiment in staging: inject increasing delays and aborts, validate the full resilience chain (timeout → retry → circuit breaker → failover), verify automated recovery. This builds confidence that production resilience configurations actually work under real failure conditions.

Resilience Q109 of 200
How does rate limiting at the Istio mesh layer differ from application-level rate limiting?
A) Istio provides Envoy-native rate limiting: local rate limiting (per-Envoy, simple token bucket) via EnvoyFilter, and global rate limiting (mesh-wide, coordinated) via an external rate limit service. Unlike app-level rate limiting (which requires code in every service), mesh-level rate limiting is configured once and enforced transparently at the proxy layer — no application code changes
B) Istio rate limiting is only available as a paid enterprise feature
C) Rate limiting in Istio only works for ingress traffic — internal mesh traffic cannot be rate limited
D) Istio rate limiting replaces Kubernetes ResourceQuota — they serve the same purpose
✅ Correct Answer: A

Mesh-level rate limiting is configured once, enforced everywhere. Local rate limiting uses Envoy's built-in token bucket — simple, per-proxy, no external dependency. Global rate limiting coordinates across all Envoys via an external gRPC service — consistent limits across the entire mesh.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Rate Limiting — Local vs Global
  LOCAL (per-Envoy, no external service):     GLOBAL (mesh-wide, coordinated):
  ┌────────────────────────────┐             ┌────────────────────────────┐
  │ EnvoyFilter: token bucket  │             │ Envoy → RateLimit Service  │
  │ • 100 req/s per proxy      │             │ • 1000 req/s across mesh   │
  │ • Simple, fast, no deps    │             │ • Consistent enforcement    │
  │ • Best for: per-pod limits │             │ • Best for: API-wide limits │
  └────────────────────────────┘             └────────────────────────────┘
  
  EnvoyFilter (local rate limit):
  actions: [{remote_address: {}}]  ← Limit per client IP
  tokenBucket: {maxTokens: 100, tokensPerFill: 10, fillInterval: 1s}

For anihpj/jobpost, configure local rate limiting on the ingress gateway: 100 req/s per client IP on /api/jobs to prevent a single user from overwhelming the job search. For global limits (1000 req/s across all gateways), deploy a rate limit service and configure global rate limiting — protecting the entire job board from abuse.

Resilience Q110 of 200
You want to design a complete resilience testing pipeline for anihpj-api that runs automatically before every production deployment. Which sequence of Istio features provides comprehensive validation?
A) Just run fault injection at 100% — if it survives, it's ready for production
B) Resilience testing is manual — you cannot automate it with Istio features alone
C) Deploy canary, wait 24 hours, check error rate — that's sufficient resilience testing
D) Progressive resilience pipeline: 1) Inject delays (10%→30%→50%) — validate timeout + retry behavior. 2) Inject HTTP aborts (5%→10%→20%) — validate circuit breaker ejection + recovery. 3) Inject combined delay+abort (simulate real-world degradation) — validate layered defense. 4) Validate locality failover by ejecting all pods in one zone. 5) Run at 0% faults — verify full recovery. All steps automated via VirtualService updates in CI/CD
✅ Correct Answer: D

A complete resilience pipeline validates every layer: timeouts, retries, circuit breakers, and failover. Start mild, escalate progressively, verify recovery. Automate in CI/CD with VirtualService YAML changes. This ensures every deployment is resilience-tested before reaching production users.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Automated Resilience Testing Pipeline — CI/CD Integration
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  CI/CD PIPELINE — Pre-deployment Resilience Validation          │
  │                                                                  │
  │  STEP 1: DEPLOY canary (v2, 0% traffic)                         │
  │  STEP 2: INJECT delays (10%, 30%, 50% at 0.5s, 2s, 5s)         │
  │          → ASSERT: timeouts fire within 2s, retries succeed     │
  │  STEP 3: INJECT aborts (5%, 10%, 20% at 503, 504, 500)         │
  │          → ASSERT: circuit breakers eject, error rate < 5%      │
  │  STEP 4: COMBINED (delay 3s + abort 503, both at 30%)          │
  │          → ASSERT: healthy pods serve 100%, degraded pods out   │
  │  STEP 5: ZONE FAILOVER (eject all zone-a pods)                  │
  │          → ASSERT: traffic shifts to zone-b within 5s           │
  │  STEP 6: RECOVERY (remove all faults)                           │
  │          → ASSERT: all metrics return to baseline within 30s    │
  │  STEP 7: PROMOTE canary to 100% (all tests passed)              │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, integrate this pipeline into your CI/CD: every PR triggers the resilience test suite against a staging canary. Only deployments that pass all 6 steps are promoted to production. This guarantees that every release has been battle-tested against realistic failure scenarios before users ever see it.
