🟣 Zero-Trust Service Mesh Security — mTLS, Authentication & Authorization

This is the most heavily weighted domain (25%). It covers everything related to securing service-to-service communication: mutual TLS (mTLS) for transport encryption and identity, PeerAuthentication for mesh-wide mTLS policy, RequestAuthentication for end-user JWT validation, AuthorizationPolicy for fine-grained access control, and certificate management. Every service in the anihpj/jobpost mesh communicates over mTLS with cryptographically verified SPIFFE identities.

4.1 mTLS & PeerAuthentication — Mutual TLS FundamentalsQ111-Q126

Security Q111 of 200
What is mutual TLS (mTLS) and how does it differ from standard TLS (HTTPS)?
A) mTLS and standard TLS are identical — both only encrypt data in transit
B) In standard TLS, only the SERVER proves its identity (like HTTPS websites). In mTLS, BOTH client AND server present certificates and prove their identities — the server verifies the client IS who it claims to be, not just that the connection is encrypted
C) mTLS is weaker than TLS because it uses two certificates instead of one
D) Standard TLS: server presents certificate (one-way trust). mTLS: BOTH sides present certificates — the client verifies the server AND the server verifies the client. This provides encryption, authentication, and identity — the foundation of zero-trust networking. In Istio, mTLS uses SPIFFE certificates automatically provisioned by istiod's CA
✅ Correct Answer: D

Standard TLS (HTTPS) = server proves identity, client is anonymous. mTLS = both sides prove identity cryptographically. This is the foundation of zero-trust: every service has a verifiable identity, and policies can be written based on WHO is calling, not just WHERE the connection comes from.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Standard TLS vs mTLS — One-Way vs Mutual Trust
  STANDARD TLS (HTTPS):                    mTLS (Istio):
  ┌──────────────────────┐                ┌──────────────────────────────┐
  │ Client ──► Server    │                │ Client ◄────mTLS────► Server │
  │         ◄── Cert     │                │  ┌──────────────────────┐    │
  │ (server identified,  │                │  │ ① Client → Server:   │    │
  │  client anonymous)   │                │  │    "I am anihpj-web" │    │
  └──────────────────────┘                │  │    (SPIFFE cert)     │    │
                                           │  │ ② Server → Client:   │    │
  Use for: public websites                │  │    "I am anihpj-api" │    │
  (anyone can browse)                     │  │    (SPIFFE cert)     │    │
                                           │  │ ③ Encrypted channel │    │
                                           │  └──────────────────────┘    │
                                           │ Both identities VERIFIED     │
                                           └──────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, mTLS means every communication between anihpj-web, anihpj-api, and anihpj-db is encrypted AND authenticated. The web pod proves "I am the web service account" and the API pod proves "I am the API service account" before any data is exchanged — zero-trust networking enforced at the proxy layer.

Security Q112 of 200
What are the three PeerAuthentication mTLS modes and when should you use each?
A) STRICT: Only accept mTLS connections — plaintext is REJECTED. Use for production, internal services. PERMISSIVE: Accept BOTH mTLS and plaintext — useful during migration to allow non-mesh clients. DISABLE: No mTLS — plaintext only (⚠️ insecure, only for specific use cases like external health checks). The default is PERMISSIVE to enable gradual adoption
B) STRICT and PERMISSIVE are identical — DISABLE is the only different mode
C) STRICT mode is the default — you must explicitly enable PERMISSIVE if needed
D) DISABLE mode still encrypts traffic but skips identity verification — it's safe for production
✅ Correct Answer: A

PERMISSIVE is the default for a reason: it allows gradual mTLS adoption. You can install Istio, enable injection, and services that support mTLS will use it while non-mesh clients can still connect. Once all clients are in the mesh, switch to STRICT for full zero-trust enforcement.

📝 Detailed Explanation
PeerAuthentication Modes — Migration Path
  PHASE 1: PERMISSIVE (default)           PHASE 2: STRICT (hardened)
  ┌────────────────────────────┐         ┌────────────────────────────┐
  │ Mesh pod → Mesh pod: mTLS ✅│         │ Mesh pod → Mesh pod: mTLS ✅│
  │ Non-mesh → Mesh pod: plain ✅│        │ Non-mesh → Mesh pod: ❌     │
  │ (gradual migration)        │         │ (REFUSED — must be in mesh)│
  └────────────────────────────┘         └────────────────────────────┘
  
  PeerAuthentication (mesh-wide STRICT):
  apiVersion: security.istio.io/v1beta1
  kind: PeerAuthentication
  metadata: {name: default, namespace: istio-system}
  spec:
    mtls:
      mode: STRICT

For anihpj/jobpost, start with PERMISSIVE during initial Istio adoption — this allows monitoring tools and legacy clients to still reach the job board without mTLS. Once all clients are mesh-injected, switch to STRICT for full zero-trust enforcement across all anihpj services.

Security Q113 of 200
What are the PeerAuthentication scopes and how does scope precedence work?
A) PeerAuthentication only supports mesh-wide scope — namespace and workload settings are not supported
B) Namespace scope overrides mesh scope, and workload scope overrides namespace — the most specific policy wins
C) Three scopes with workload > namespace > mesh precedence: Mesh-wide (applied in istio-system root namespace) sets the default. Namespace-wide overrides for a specific namespace. Workload-specific (selector-based) overrides for specific pods. This enables flexible policies: STRICT mesh-wide, PERMISSIVE for a test namespace, DISABLE for a specific health-check pod
D) All scopes are merged — the most secure setting from each scope is used
✅ Correct Answer: C

Scope precedence: workload > namespace > mesh. More specific policies override broader ones. This lets you have STRICT mesh-wide but PERMISSIVE for a namespace still being migrated, or DISABLE for a specific health-check endpoint that doesn't support mTLS.

📝 Detailed Explanation
PeerAuthentication Scope Precedence
  Mesh-wide (istio-system):  mode: STRICT     ← Default for everything
  └─► Namespace "test":      mode: PERMISSIVE ← Override for test ns
  └─► Namespace "anihpj":    (inherits STRICT) ← Uses mesh default
      └─► Workload "health": mode: DISABLE     ← Override for health pod
  
  RESULT:
  • anihpj-web → anihpj-api: STRICT mTLS (mesh default)
  • test-pod → test-svc: PERMISSIVE (namespace override)
  • health-check → anihpj-api: DISABLE (workload override)

For anihpj/jobpost, set mesh-wide STRICT for production, PERMISSIVE for the anihpj-dev namespace during development, and DISABLE for the Prometheus health-check endpoint that doesn't support mTLS. This gives you zero-trust by default with targeted exceptions.

Security Q114 of 200
What is SPIFFE and how does it provide workload identity in Istio's mTLS?
A) SPIFFE is a Kubernetes-specific identity format — it only works within a single cluster
B) SPIFFE (Secure Production Identity Framework for Everyone) provides a universal workload identity standard. In Istio, every pod gets a SPIFFE ID in the format spiffe://<trust-domain>/ns/<namespace>/sa/<serviceaccount>. This ID is embedded in the X.509 certificate that Envoy presents during mTLS — cryptographically binding the pod's identity to its ServiceAccount. AuthorizationPolicies then use this SPIFFE ID to allow/deny access
C) SPIFFE is an alternative to mTLS — you use one or the other
D) SPIFFE IDs are randomly generated and not tied to any Kubernetes resource
✅ Correct Answer: B

SPIFFE is the identity backbone of Istio's zero-trust model. Every ServiceAccount gets a unique, cryptographically verifiable identity. AuthorizationPolicy says "allow principals with SPIFFE ID ending in /sa/anihpj-api" — and Envoy enforces it by checking the certificate presented during mTLS.

📝 Detailed Explanation
SPIFFE Identity — From ServiceAccount to Certificate
  ServiceAccount: anihpj-api (namespace: anihpj)
  └─► SPIFFE ID: spiffe://cluster.local/ns/anihpj/sa/anihpj-api
      └─► Embedded in X.509 certificate (SAN field)
          └─► Envoy presents this cert during mTLS handshake
              └─► Destination Envoy verifies: "Is this cert signed by Istio CA?"
                  "Does the SPIFFE ID match the expected identity?"
  
  AuthorizationPolicy using SPIFFE:
  rules:
  - from:
    - source:
        principals: ["cluster.local/ns/anihpj/sa/anihpj-api"]
    to:
    - operation: {ports: ["5432"]}
  → ONLY pods with the anihpj-api ServiceAccount can access port 5432

For anihpj/jobpost, every pod gets a unique SPIFFE identity based on its ServiceAccount. The anihpj-web SA becomes spiffe://cluster.local/ns/anihpj/sa/anihpj-web, anihpj-api SA becomes .../sa/anihpj-api. AuthorizationPolicies then use these identities to enforce least-privilege access between services.

Security Q115 of 200
How does istiod's CA (Citadel) automatically provision and rotate mTLS certificates?
A) istiod's internal CA automatically: 1) Generates a self-signed root CA on first install. 2) Issues intermediate CA certificates. 3) Provisions workload certificates to every Envoy via SDS (Secret Discovery Service) over gRPC. 4) Automatically rotates workload certificates every 24 hours — Envoy receives the new cert before the old one expires, with zero downtime
B) Istio does not have a built-in CA — certificates must be manually provisioned from an external CA
C) Certificates are generated once at pod startup and never rotated — pods must restart for new certs
D) Certificate rotation requires manually running istioctl certs rotate — it's not automatic
✅ Correct Answer: A

istiod's CA is fully automatic. You install Istio, and it generates a root CA, issues workload certs, and rotates them every 24 hours — all without any administrator intervention. Each Envoy receives new certs via SDS streaming before the old ones expire. Zero downtime, zero manual steps.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Automatic Certificate Lifecycle — istiod CA + SDS
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  istiod CA (Citadel)                                            │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ Root CA (self-signed, 10yr)                               │  │
  │  │  └─► Intermediate CA (signed by Root, 1yr)                │  │
  │  │      └─► Workload Cert (signed by Intermediate, 24h)      │  │
  │  │          └─► Pushed to Envoy via SDS (gRPC stream)        │  │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ROTATION TIMELINE:                                              │
  │  T+0h:   New cert issued (valid 24h)                            │
  │  T+12h:  Envoy requests new cert (50% of lifetime)              │
  │  T+23h:  New cert received, old cert still valid (1h overlap)   │
  │  T+24h:  Old cert expires, Envoy already using new cert ✅      │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, this means every anihpj-web, anihpj-api, and anihpj-db Envoy automatically receives and rotates certificates. You never touch certificates — istiod handles the entire lifecycle. The 24-hour rotation limits the blast radius if a certificate is compromised.

Security Q116 of 200
What is the certificate chain in Istio's mTLS and why is it a chain rather than a single certificate?
A) The chain is just for backward compatibility — a single certificate would work fine
B) Multiple certificates are needed because Envoy requires one cert per port — it's a technical limitation
C) The chain is a backup mechanism — if one CA fails, the next in the chain takes over
D) The chain provides trust delegation: Root CA (offline, highly secured) signs the Intermediate CA, which signs workload certificates. The Root CA is never online — only the Intermediate CA is in the hot path. If the Intermediate CA is compromised, you revoke it and reissue from the Root without replacing every workload certificate. This is standard PKI best practice
✅ Correct Answer: D

The chain separates trust (Root CA, offline) from operations (Intermediate CA, online). If the Intermediate CA is compromised, the Root CA (which was never exposed) can revoke and reissue without changing every workload certificate. This is how enterprise PKI works.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Certificate Chain — Root → Intermediate → Workload
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ROOT CA (offline, air-gapped, 10-year validity)                │
  │  🔒 Stored in HSM or secure vault — NEVER exposed to network    │
  │  │                                                               │
  │  └─► INTERMEDIATE CA (online, 1-year validity)                  │
  │      🔑 Runs inside istiod — signs all workload certs           │
  │      │                                                           │
  │      └─► WORKLOAD CERTS (per-pod, 24h validity)                │
  │          📜 Presented by Envoy during mTLS handshake            │
  │                                                                  │
  │  VERIFICATION: Remote Envoy checks:                              │
  │  Workload Cert → signed by Intermediate CA → signed by Root CA   │
  │  → Chain of trust verified ✅                                    │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost in production, use a custom Root CA stored in a secure vault. istiod uses the Intermediate CA for daily operations. This means even if the Istio control plane is compromised, the Root CA remains secure — you can revoke the Intermediate CA and reissue without rebuilding the entire mesh.

4.1b mTLS Advanced — Debugging, Custom CA & External ServicesQ117-Q126

Security Q117 of 200
What happens when PeerAuthentication is STRICT but one side (the client) does NOT have a sidecar or its certificate is invalid?
A) The connection falls back to plaintext — STRICT mode is only advisory
B) The connection is REFUSED — Envoy on the destination side rejects the plaintext connection because STRICT mode requires mTLS. The client receives a "connection refused" or TLS handshake error. This is intentional: STRICT enforces zero-trust, and any workload without a valid mesh identity is blocked
C) The connection succeeds but is logged as a security violation — traffic still flows
D) STRICT mode auto-injects a sidecar into the client pod — it's self-healing
✅ Correct Answer: B

STRICT means STRICT. No certificate = no connection. The destination Envoy sees plaintext traffic and immediately rejects it. This is the enforcement mechanism of zero-trust: workloads without mesh identity are completely isolated. Always use PERMISSIVE during migration; switch to STRICT only when ALL clients are in the mesh.

📝 Detailed Explanation
STRICT Mode Enforcement — No Cert = No Connection
  PeerAuthentication: STRICT (mesh-wide)
  
  Mesh pod → Mesh pod:     ✅ mTLS handshake succeeds
  Non-mesh → Mesh pod:     ❌ Connection REFUSED
  Old cert → Mesh pod:     ❌ TLS error (cert expired/invalid)
  
  DEBUGGING:
  $ kubectl logs anihpj-api-xxx -c istio-proxy | grep TLS
  [warning] TLS handshake failed: client did not present certificate
  
  FIX:
  • Option A: Set namespace to PERMISSIVE temporarily
  • Option B: Inject sidecar into client pod
  • Option C: Use PeerAuthentication workload-level DISABLE for specific pods

For anihpj/jobpost, set mesh-wide STRICT only after ALL anihpj-web, anihpj-api, and anihpj-db pods have sidecars injected and verified. Before switching, run istioctl authn tls-check to audit which connections would break under STRICT. Switch during a maintenance window with rollback ready.

Security Q118 of 200
How do you debug mTLS issues when services that should communicate are failing with connection errors?
A) Use istioctl authn tls-check <pod> -n <ns> to verify mTLS configuration between specific pods. Use istioctl proxy-config secret <pod> -n <ns> to inspect certificates and check expiry. Use istioctl proxy-config cluster to verify TLS mode. Check PeerAuthentication policies with kubectl get peerauthentication --all-namespaces
B) mTLS issues always show in kubectl describe pod — no istioctl commands are needed
C) Restart both pods — mTLS issues are always transient and resolve on restart
D) Check Kiali — it's the only way to debug mTLS issues in Istio
✅ Correct Answer: A

The three-command mTLS debug sequence: 1) istioctl authn tls-check to verify two pods can establish mTLS, 2) istioctl proxy-config secret to inspect certificates, 3) kubectl get peerauthentication --all-namespaces to audit policies.

📝 Detailed Explanation
mTLS Debugging — Three-Step Diagnostic Sequence
  ① CHECK mTLS BETWEEN TWO PODS:
  $ istioctl authn tls-check anihpj-web-xxx -n anihpj anihpj-api.anihpj.svc.cluster.local:8000
  STATUS: OK ✅  mTLS: ISTIO_MUTUAL  Cert Expiry: 2026-06-13T10:00:00Z
  
  ② INSPECT CERTIFICATES:
  $ istioctl proxy-config secret anihpj-api-xxx -n anihpj -o json | \
      jq '.dynamicActiveSecrets[].secret.tlsCertificate'
  → Shows: certificate chain, validation time, SAN (SPIFFE ID)
  
  ③ AUDIT ALL PeerAuthentication POLICIES:
  $ kubectl get peerauthentication --all-namespaces
  NAMESPACE      NAME      MODE
  istio-system   default   STRICT
  anihpj         (none)    (inherits STRICT)

For anihpj/jobpost, when web-to-API communication breaks, run istioctl authn tls-check between the web and API pods. It validates the full mTLS handshake and certificate chain, pointing to exactly where the failure occurs — certificate expiry, mode mismatch, or missing sidecar.

Security Q119 of 200
How do you configure a custom CA (your own root certificate) instead of Istio's self-signed CA?
A) Custom CA requires purchasing an enterprise Istio license — self-signed is the only option in open-source
B) Edit the istio ConfigMap and paste your certificate — no other changes needed
C) Create a Kubernetes Secret (cacerts) in the istio-system namespace containing your CA certificate, key, and cert chain. istiod detects this Secret at startup and uses YOUR CA instead of generating a self-signed one. All workload certificates are then signed by your CA — enabling cross-cluster mesh federation with a shared trust domain
D) Custom CA only works with HashiCorp Vault — Istio cannot use other CAs
✅ Correct Answer: C

Plug in your own CA by creating a Secret named cacerts before installing Istio (or restart istiod after). This enables enterprise PKI integration: your security team's CA signs all mesh workload certificates, enabling cross-cluster federation and compliance with corporate certificate policies.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Custom CA — Enterprise PKI Integration
  CREATE CUSTOM CA SECRET:
  kubectl create secret generic cacerts -n istio-system \
    --from-file=ca-cert.pem \          ← Root CA certificate
    --from-file=ca-key.pem \           ← Root CA private key
    --from-file=root-cert.pem \        ← Root cert (for chain)
    --from-file=cert-chain.pem         ← Intermediate chain
  
  THEN install/restart Istio:
  istioctl install --set profile=default
  (istiod detects cacerts Secret → uses YOUR CA instead of self-signed)
  
  VERIFY:
  $ istioctl proxy-config secret anihpj-web -n anihpj -o json | \
      jq -r '..|.inlineBytes?|select(.)' | base64 -d | openssl x509 -noout -issuer
  issuer=CN=MyCorp Intermediate CA  ← YOUR CA, not Istio's default

For anihpj/jobpost in enterprise environments, use a custom CA so all mesh certificates chain to the corporate PKI. This enables cross-cluster mesh federation (multiple clusters sharing the same trust domain) and satisfies compliance requirements for certificate management.

Security Q120 of 200
How do you configure mTLS origination for external services — making mesh-to-external traffic encrypted?
A) External mTLS requires an egress gateway — sidecar proxies cannot originate mTLS to external services
B) All external traffic is automatically encrypted — mTLS origination is the default
C) Set PeerAuthentication to STRICT — it applies to external services too
D) Create a ServiceEntry for the external host, then create a DestinationRule with tls.mode: MUTUAL and provide clientCertificate, privateKey, and caCertificates from Kubernetes Secrets. The mesh Envoy will originate mTLS to the external service — encrypting traffic even to services outside the cluster
✅ Correct Answer: D

ServiceEntry registers the external service, DestinationRule with MUTUAL TLS mode configures mTLS origination. The mesh Envoy presents a client certificate to the external service — encrypting traffic end-to-end even to services outside Kubernetes. Perfect for regulated industries requiring encryption everywhere.

📝 Detailed Explanation
mTLS Origination to External Services
  ServiceEntry:                            DestinationRule:
  apiVersion: networking.istio.io/v1beta1  apiVersion: networking.istio.io/v1beta1
  kind: ServiceEntry                       kind: DestinationRule
  spec:                                    spec:
    hosts: [api.partner.com]               host: api.partner.com
    ports: [{number: 443, protocol: HTTPS}]  trafficPolicy:
    resolution: DNS                          tls:
    location: MESH_EXTERNAL                   mode: MUTUAL
                                              clientCertificate: /etc/certs/client.pem
                                              privateKey: /etc/certs/client.key
                                              caCertificates: /etc/certs/ca.pem
  
  FLOW: anihpj-api → Envoy (originates mTLS) → api.partner.com (validates client cert)
  ✅ Full encryption from mesh to external service

For anihpj/jobpost, configure mTLS origination for external payment API calls (Stripe) and email service (Mailgun). This ensures payment data and user emails are encrypted end-to-end — satisfying PCI-DSS and GDPR requirements for data in transit.

Security Q121 of 200
How do you apply PeerAuthentication at the workload (pod) level to override namespace or mesh settings for a specific Deployment?
A) Workload-level PeerAuthentication is not supported — only mesh and namespace scope exist
B) Create a PeerAuthentication resource with a selector.matchLabels targeting specific pods. The workload-level policy overrides namespace and mesh policies for those pods. Example: selector: {matchLabels: {app: health-check}} with mode: DISABLE to allow plaintext health checks while the rest of the mesh uses STRICT
C) Add an annotation security.istio.io/mtls: disable to the pod — PeerAuthentication resource is not needed
D) Workload-level settings must be configured in the Deployment YAML under spec.template.spec.security
✅ Correct Answer: B

Workload-level PeerAuthentication targets specific pods via label selectors. This is the most granular scope — override mesh-wide STRICT with DISABLE for a health-check pod that doesn't support mTLS, or set PERMISSIVE for a specific Deployment during migration.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Workload-Level PeerAuthentication — Granular Override
  apiVersion: security.istio.io/v1beta1
  kind: PeerAuthentication
  metadata: {name: health-check-pa, namespace: anihpj}
  spec:
    selector:
      matchLabels:
        app: health-check     ← Targets ONLY pods with this label
    mtls:
      mode: DISABLE           ← These pods: no mTLS required
  
  RESULT:
  Mesh-wide: STRICT
  anihpj ns: (inherits STRICT)
  └─► health-check pods: DISABLE ← Override for specific workload
  └─► All other anihpj pods: STRICT

For anihpj/jobpost, use workload-level PeerAuthentication to disable mTLS for the Prometheus health-check endpoint that doesn't support TLS, while keeping STRICT mTLS for all other anihpj services. This maintains zero-trust for the mesh while accommodating specific requirements.

Security Q122 of 200
What are the risks of enabling mesh-wide STRICT mTLS on a production cluster, and how do you safely migrate?
A) STRICT mTLS will BREAK all non-mesh traffic instantly. Safe migration: 1) Start with PERMISSIVE (default), 2) Verify all workloads have sidecars, 3) Use istioctl authn tls-check to audit all connections, 4) Switch to STRICT during a maintenance window, 5) Monitor for TLS handshake errors in Envoy logs. Have a rollback plan: switch back to PERMISSIVE immediately if issues arise
B) STRICT mTLS is safe to enable at any time — Istio auto-detects non-mesh clients and falls back
C) Enable STRICT immediately after installing Istio — no migration needed because all pods are already in the mesh
D) STRICT mTLS only affects ingress gateways — internal mesh traffic is unaffected
✅ Correct Answer: A

STRICT mTLS is a one-way door. Once enabled, any client without a valid mesh certificate is blocked. Always audit first with istioctl authn tls-check, migrate gradually (namespace by namespace if needed), and have an instant rollback plan.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Safe mTLS STRICT Migration — Four-Phase Plan
  PHASE 1: AUDIT (PERMISSIVE mode)
  $ istioctl authn tls-check anihpj-web anihpj-api.anihpj:8000
  → Identifies all connections that would break under STRICT
  
  PHASE 2: FIX
  • Inject sidecars into all pods that need mTLS
  • Add PeerAuthentication DISABLE for pods that cannot support mTLS
  
  PHASE 3: MIGRATE (maintenance window)
  $ kubectl apply -f peerauthentication-strict.yaml
  → Monitor: kubectl logs -n anihpj -l app=anihpj-api -c istio-proxy | grep TLS
  
  PHASE 4: VERIFY
  $ istioctl authn tls-check --all
  → All connections show ISTIO_MUTUAL ✅
  
  ROLLBACK: kubectl apply -f peerauthentication-permissive.yaml

For anihpj/jobpost, migrate to STRICT mTLS after all services are meshed. Audit first, migrate during a low-traffic window, monitor Envoy logs for TLS errors, and keep the PERMISSIVE PeerAuthentication YAML ready for instant rollback if the job board shows connection errors.

Security Q123 of 200
What happens to mTLS during an Istio upgrade? Can services with different sidecar versions still communicate securely?
A) Different sidecar versions cannot communicate — you must upgrade all pods simultaneously
B) mTLS is disabled during upgrades — traffic falls back to plaintext until all pods are restarted
C) mTLS continues to work DURING upgrades because certificates are signed by the same CA and trust domain. Sidecars with different Envoy versions can still establish mTLS — the SPIFFE identity and certificate validation are version-independent. However, new xDS features may require both sides to be on the newer version
D) Upgrades require regenerating all certificates — mTLS is temporarily broken during rotation
✅ Correct Answer: C

mTLS is backward-compatible across Istio versions. The SPIFFE identity format and certificate validation haven't changed — an Envoy v1.22 sidecar can establish mTLS with an Envoy v1.23 sidecar. This is why canary upgrades work: old and new sidecars coexist securely.

📝 Detailed Explanation
mTLS Across Versions — Backward Compatible
  DURING CANARY UPGRADE:
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  istiod-stable (v1.22)        istiod-canary (v1.23)             │
  │  │                             │                                  │
  │  ├─► anihpj-web (Envoy 1.22)  ├─► anihpj-api (Envoy 1.23)      │
  │  │                             │                                  │
  │  └─── mTLS ✅ ────────────────┘                                  │
  │  Same trust domain (cluster.local), same SPIFFE format,          │
  │  same CA (istiod manages both intermediate CAs)                  │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, canary upgrades are safe for mTLS. Migrate the anihpj-api namespace to the new revision first while anihpj-web stays on the old — mTLS continues to work between them. This enables gradual, zero-downtime upgrades while maintaining full encryption.

Security Q124 of 200
How can you selectively disable mTLS for specific ports while keeping it enabled for others?
A) Port-level mTLS configuration is not supported — it's all-or-nothing per workload
B) Use different PeerAuthentication resources for different ports — each port gets its own policy
C) Add traffic.sidecar.istio.io/excludeInboundPorts annotation to exclude ports from the mesh entirely
D) Use portLevelMtls in PeerAuthentication to set per-port mTLS modes. Example: port 8080 = STRICT (API traffic), port 9090 = DISABLE (metrics endpoint). This provides granular control — the API port requires mTLS while the Prometheus metrics port accepts plaintext scraping
✅ Correct Answer: D

Port-level mTLS gives you granular control. Keep STRICT on application ports (8080, 8000, 5432) while allowing plaintext on monitoring ports (9090 for Prometheus, 9091 for health checks). This is cleaner than per-workload overrides — one policy, multiple port-specific rules.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Port-Level mTLS — Granular Per-Port Control
  apiVersion: security.istio.io/v1beta1
  kind: PeerAuthentication
  metadata: {name: default, namespace: anihpj}
  spec:
    mtls:
      mode: STRICT              ← Default for all ports
    portLevelMtls:
      9090:
        mode: PERMISSIVE        ← Prometheus metrics: allow plaintext
      9091:
        mode: DISABLE           ← Health check: plaintext only
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  PORT 8080 (API):   mTLS STRICT ✅                               │
  │  PORT 5432 (DB):    mTLS STRICT ✅                               │
  │  PORT 9090 (Metrics): PERMISSIVE (Prometheus can scrape)        │
  │  PORT 9091 (Health):  DISABLE (kubelet probe)                   │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, use port-level mTLS to keep STRICT on the Django API port (8000) and PostgreSQL port (5432), while allowing PERMISSIVE on port 9090 for Prometheus metrics scraping. This maintains zero-trust for business traffic while accommodating monitoring infrastructure that cannot do mTLS.

Security Q125 of 200
How does mTLS work with non-Istio workloads — can a pod without a sidecar communicate with a mesh pod under STRICT mTLS?
A) Non-Istio workloads are automatically issued certificates — mTLS works transparently
B) Under STRICT mTLS, a pod WITHOUT a sidecar CANNOT communicate with a mesh pod — the connection is refused because the client cannot present a valid SPIFFE certificate. Solutions: 1) Inject a sidecar into the client pod, 2) Set the namespace to PERMISSIVE, 3) Use port-level DISABLE for specific ports, 4) For VMs, use WorkloadGroup to bring external workloads into the mesh

D) Add the non-Istio pod's IP to an allowlist in PeerAuthentication — this bypasses mTLS

STRICT mTLS is a hard boundary. Non-mesh clients are locked out. This is the enforcement mechanism of zero-trust. For external workloads (VMs, bare metal), use WorkloadGroup to bring them into the mesh with proper SPIFFE identities.

Non-Mesh → Mesh Communication Under STRICT
  STRICT mTLS (namespace: anihpj):
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ✅ Pod WITH sidecar → Mesh pod: mTLS works                     │
  │  ❌ Pod WITHOUT sidecar → Mesh pod: Connection REFUSED          │
  │  ❌ External VM (no mesh) → Mesh pod: Connection REFUSED        │
  │                                                                  │
  │  SOLUTIONS:                                                      │
  │  ① kubectl label ns client-ns istio-injection=enabled (inject)  │

For anihpj/jobpost, if a legacy monitoring tool outside the mesh must scrape metrics from anihpj-api, either inject a sidecar into the monitoring pod or set PERMISSIVE on the metrics port. Never disable mTLS mesh-wide — always use the most targeted exception possible.

Zero plaintext traffic under STRICT means full compliance
B) If PeerAuthentication is set to STRICT, mTLS is guaranteed — no verification needed
C) Check kubectl get pods — if all pods show 2/2 Ready, mTLS is working
D) mTLS verification requires a third-party security scanning tool — Istio tools cannot verify it
✅ Correct Answer: A

📝 Detailed Explanation
mTLS Health Verification — Three-Layer Audit
  ① CLI (point check):
  $ istioctl authn tls-check anihpj-web anihpj-api:8000
  STATUS: OK  mTLS: ISTIO_MUTUAL  Cert: 2026-06-13T10:00Z
  
  ② KIALI (visual):
  Graph → Display → Security → lock icons on every edge 🔒
  Green lock = mTLS active, Gray lock = plaintext

  sum(rate(istio_requests_total{connection_security_policy="mutual_tls"}[5m]))
