4.2 AuthorizationPolicy & RequestAuthentication — Access Control & JWTQ127-Q142

Security Q127 of 200
What is an AuthorizationPolicy in Istio and how does it enforce zero-trust access control?
A) AuthorizationPolicy is Istio's replacement for Kubernetes RBAC — it controls kubectl access to the cluster
B) AuthorizationPolicy only works at the ingress gateway — internal mesh traffic cannot be controlled
C) AuthorizationPolicy is purely advisory — it logs violations but does not block traffic
D) AuthorizationPolicy is Istio's service-to-service RBAC. It defines WHO (source principals, namespaces, IPs) can do WHAT (HTTP methods, paths, ports) to WHOM (destination hosts). Rules are ALLOW, DENY, or CUSTOM (delegate to external service). Enforced by Envoy at the proxy layer — every request is checked BEFORE reaching the application. Multiple policies are OR'd: if ANY ALLOW rule matches, the request is permitted
✅ Correct Answer: D

AuthorizationPolicy implements least-privilege access between services. Unlike Kubernetes RBAC (which controls API server access), AuthorizationPolicy controls APPLICATION traffic — who can call your API, who can access your database. Enforced at the Envoy proxy layer, not in application code.

📝 Detailed Explanation
AuthorizationPolicy — Service-to-Service Access Control
  AuthorizationPolicy: anihpj-db-protect (namespace: anihpj)
  spec:
    selector: {matchLabels: {app: anihpj-db}}
    action: ALLOW
    rules:
    - from:
      - source:
          principals: ["cluster.local/ns/anihpj/sa/anihpj-api"]
      to:
      - operation:
          ports: ["5432"]
          methods: ["POST"]
  
  RESULT:
  ✅ anihpj-api → anihpj-db:5432: ALLOWED (matches rule)
  ❌ anihpj-web → anihpj-db:5432: DENIED (no matching rule)
  ❌ any-other → anihpj-db:5432: DENIED (default deny)

For anihpj/jobpost, AuthorizationPolicy enforces that ONLY the anihpj-api ServiceAccount can connect to the PostgreSQL database on port 5432. The web frontend cannot bypass the API and access the database directly — even if it knows the password. This is zero-trust access control at the network layer.

Security Q128 of 200
What is the difference between ALLOW, DENY, and CUSTOM actions in AuthorizationPolicy?
A) ALLOW: Permit matching requests (default if action is omitted, but default-deny applies when no ALLOW policies exist). DENY: Explicitly block matching requests — DENY takes precedence over ALLOW, so a DENY rule overrides any ALLOW. CUSTOM: Delegate the decision to an external authorization service (ext_authz) via HTTP or gRPC — enables integration with OPA, custom authZ logic
B) ALLOW and DENY are identical — CUSTOM is the only distinct action
C) DENY rules are deprecated — only ALLOW should be used in modern Istio
D) CUSTOM action requires a paid Istio license — it's not available in open-source
✅ Correct Answer: A

ALLOW = whitelist (permit matching). DENY = blacklist (block matching, overrides ALLOW). CUSTOM = delegate to external service. DENY is essential for security: block known malicious IPs or compromised ServiceAccounts regardless of any ALLOW policies.

📝 Detailed Explanation
AuthorizationPolicy Actions — Precedence
  POLICY EVALUATION ORDER (per request):
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ① Check DENY policies: If ANY DENY rule matches → REJECT (403) │
  │  ② Check ALLOW policies: If ANY ALLOW rule matches → PERMIT     │
  │  ③ If no ALLOW policies exist → PERMIT (default allow)          │
  │  ④ If ALLOW policies exist but none match → REJECT (default deny)│
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
  
  DENY always wins — it overrides ALLOW.
  
  CUSTOM:
  Envoy → HTTP/gRPC → External AuthZ Service → ALLOW/DENY response
  Use for: OPA (Open Policy Agent), custom business logic authZ

For anihpj/jobpost, use ALLOW rules for normal access control (API can access DB), DENY rules for blocking compromised IPs or during incident response, and CUSTOM with OPA for complex policies like "only HR team members can view salary data in the job posting system."

Security Q129 of 200
How does source matching work in AuthorizationPolicy — what can you match against?
A) Source matching only supports IP addresses — SPIFFE principals and namespaces are not supported
B) Match by principals (SPIFFE IDs like cluster.local/ns/anihpj/sa/anihpj-api), namespaces (anihpj or istio-system), ipBlocks (CIDR ranges), and requestPrincipals (JWT claims). You can combine multiple conditions within a rule — all must match for the rule to apply. Also supports notPrincipals and notNamespaces for exclusion
C) Source matching requires EnvoyFilter — AuthorizationPolicy cannot match by source
D) Principals matching only works with Kubernetes ServiceAccounts — custom SPIFFE IDs are not supported
✅ Correct Answer: B

Source matching is the WHO in "who can do what." Principals identify specific workloads by SPIFFE ID (ServiceAccount). Namespaces group workloads. IP blocks capture external traffic. Combine them for precise access control.

📝 Detailed Explanation
AuthorizationPolicy Source Matching — All Options
  rules:
  - from:
    - source:
        principals: ["cluster.local/ns/anihpj/sa/anihpj-api"]
        # ↑ Exact SPIFFE ID match
        namespaces: ["anihpj", "istio-system"]
        # ↑ Any workload in these namespaces
        ipBlocks: ["10.244.0.0/16", "192.168.1.0/24"]
        # ↑ CIDR ranges
        notPrincipals: ["cluster.local/ns/default/sa/evil-sa"]
        # ↑ Exclude this specific SA
        requestPrincipals: ["*"]  # ← Match any valid JWT principal

For anihpj/jobpost, lock down the database: principals: ["cluster.local/ns/anihpj/sa/anihpj-api"] and namespaces: ["anihpj"]. This means ONLY the API ServiceAccount in the anihpj namespace can access PostgreSQL — all other services, namespaces, and external IPs are blocked.

Security Q130 of 200
How do you use DENY policies to block specific malicious actors while keeping ALLOW policies for legitimate traffic?
A) DENY policies replace ALLOW policies — you cannot have both types active simultaneously
B) Create a separate namespace for DENY policies — they cannot coexist with ALLOW in the same namespace
C) Create an AuthorizationPolicy with action: DENY targeting the malicious source (specific SPIFFE ID, IP block, or namespace). DENY policies are evaluated BEFORE ALLOW policies and take precedence. Example: block a compromised ServiceAccount with a DENY rule, while ALLOW rules continue to permit legitimate services. This implements blocklisting on top of allowlisting
D) DENY is the default action — no explicit DENY policy is needed, just omit the ALLOW
✅ Correct Answer: C

DENY + ALLOW = blocklist + allowlist working together. ALLOW defines who can access; DENY explicitly blocks specific threats. DENY always wins — even if an ALLOW policy matches, a matching DENY policy overrides. This is essential for incident response.

📝 Detailed Explanation
DENY + ALLOW — Blocklisting on Top of Allowlisting
  ALLOW (standing policy):                 DENY (incident response):
  ┌────────────────────────────┐          ┌────────────────────────────┐
  │ action: ALLOW              │          │ action: DENY               │
  │ from:                      │          │ from:                       │
  │ - source:                  │          │ - source:                   │
  │     namespaces: [anihpj]   │          │     ipBlocks: [10.0.5.7/32]│
  │                            │          │                             │
  │ Allows all anihpj services │          │ Blocks compromised IP      │
  └────────────────────────────┘          │ even though ALLOW matches   │
                                           └────────────────────────────┘
  
  RESULT: 10.0.5.7 → DENIED (DENY overrides ALLOW)
          All other anihpj services → ALLOWED

For anihpj/jobpost, keep standing ALLOW policies for normal operations, and deploy DENY policies during incident response to instantly block a compromised IP or ServiceAccount. The DENY policy takes effect immediately without changing any ALLOW rules — clean separation between normal operations and emergency response.

Security Q131 of 200
What is RequestAuthentication and how does it validate JWT tokens at the mesh layer?
A) RequestAuthentication validates end-user credentials (JWT tokens) at the mesh edge. It specifies: issuer (who issued the token), jwksUri (where to fetch public keys for signature verification), and audiences (expected audience claim). Envoy validates the JWT before forwarding to the backend — if invalid, the request is rejected with 401. Unlike PeerAuthentication (service identity via mTLS), RequestAuthentication handles user identity via tokens
B) RequestAuthentication replaces PeerAuthentication — you use one or the other
C) RequestAuthentication only works with OAuth2 — JWT is not supported natively
D) RequestAuthentication stores user credentials in Envoy — it acts as a session store
✅ Correct Answer: A

RequestAuthentication = end-user auth (JWT validation at the proxy). PeerAuthentication = service auth (mTLS/SPIFFE). Together: the mesh knows WHO the service is (SPIFFE) AND WHO the user is (JWT), enabling fine-grained policies like "API SA can access DB, AND the user must have a valid JWT with admin role."

📝 Detailed Explanation
RequestAuthentication — JWT Validation at the Mesh Layer
  RequestAuthentication: anihpj-api-jwt (namespace: anihpj)
  spec:
    selector: {matchLabels: {app: anihpj-api}}
    jwtRules:
    - issuer: "https://auth.jobpost.com"        ← Who issued the token
      jwksUri: "https://auth.jobpost.com/.well-known/jwks.json"
      audiences: ["jobpost-api"]                ← Expected audience
      fromHeaders: [{name: Authorization}]
  
  FLOW:
  Client → Authorization: Bearer  → Ingress Gateway
  → Envoy validates: signature? issuer? audience? expiry?
  → VALID: Forward to anihpj-api with headers
  → INVALID: 401 Unauthorized (backend never sees the request)

For anihpj/jobpost, RequestAuthentication on the ingress gateway validates user JWT tokens before any request reaches Django. If the token is expired, invalid, or from the wrong issuer, Envoy returns 401 — the Django application never processes an unauthenticated request. This offloads authentication to the mesh layer.

Security Q132 of 200
How do you combine RequestAuthentication (JWT) + AuthorizationPolicy to require both a valid JWT AND specific claims?
A) JWT validation and authorization cannot be combined — they are independent and cannot reference each other
B) AuthorizationPolicy has a built-in JWT validator — you don't need a separate RequestAuthentication
C) Set AuthorizationPolicy action to JWT — it handles both validation and claims checking
D) Create a RequestAuthentication to validate the JWT (signature, issuer, expiry). Then create an AuthorizationPolicy with ALLOW rules that check JWT claims via requestPrincipals and when conditions. Example: requestPrincipals: ["*"] (any valid JWT) AND when: [{key: request.auth.claims[group], values: ["admin"]}]. The RequestAuthentication validates the token; the AuthorizationPolicy enforces claim-based access
✅ Correct Answer: D

RequestAuthentication validates the token (is it real? not expired?). AuthorizationPolicy checks claims (is the user an admin?). Together: "any valid JWT from our auth server" + "user must have group=admin claim" = full RBAC on top of authentication.

📝 Detailed Explanation
JWT + AuthorizationPolicy — AuthN + AuthZ Combined
  RequestAuthentication (AuthN):           AuthorizationPolicy (AuthZ):
  jwtRules:                                action: ALLOW
  - issuer: auth.jobpost.com               rules:
    jwksUri: .../.well-known/jwks.json     - from:
                                             - source:
  → Validates: JWT is real, not expired        requestPrincipals: ["*"]
                                             to:
  AuthorizationPolicy:                      - operation:
  → Checks: Does user have admin group?         paths: ["/api/admin/*"]
                                             when:
                                             - key: request.auth.claims[group]
                                               values: ["admin", "superadmin"]
  
  RESULT:
  ✅ Valid JWT + group=admin + /api/admin/* → ALLOWED
  ❌ Valid JWT + group=user + /api/admin/* → DENIED (wrong group)
  ❌ No JWT + /api/admin/* → DENIED (no auth)

For anihpj/jobpost, use RequestAuthentication to validate JWT tokens from the auth server, and AuthorizationPolicy to restrict /api/admin/* endpoints to users with group=admin claim. This separates concerns: AuthN verifies identity, AuthZ enforces permissions — all at the mesh layer, zero Django code changes.

Security Q133 of 200
How does JWT (end-user identity) differ from mTLS/SPIFFE (service identity) in Istio's security model?
A) JWT and mTLS are interchangeable — both identify the same entity
B) mTLS/SPIFFE identifies the CALLING SERVICE (e.g., "this request came from the anihpj-api ServiceAccount"). JWT identifies the END USER (e.g., "this request was made by user alice@jobpost.com"). They serve different purposes: mTLS answers "which service is calling?" (service-to-service auth), JWT answers "which user is making the request?" (end-user auth). Both can coexist — a request carries BOTH a SPIFFE certificate AND a JWT token
C) JWT replaces mTLS for internal traffic — services authenticate with JWT instead of certificates
D) mTLS is for external traffic; JWT is for internal traffic — they operate in different domains
✅ Correct Answer: B

Two layers of identity: service identity (mTLS/SPIFFE) tells you WHICH microservice is calling, user identity (JWT) tells you WHICH user initiated the request. Both are cryptographically verified. Together they enable policies like "the API service can access the database, but only for requests from users with the 'premium' subscription."

📝 Detailed Explanation
Two Identity Layers — Service + User
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  REQUEST: POST /api/jobs (create job posting)                    │
  │                                                                  │
  │  LAYER 1 — SERVICE IDENTITY (mTLS/SPIFFE):                      │
  │  Certificate: spiffe://cluster.local/ns/anihpj/sa/anihpj-web    │
  │  → "This request came from the WEB frontend service"            │
  │                                                                  │
  │  LAYER 2 — USER IDENTITY (JWT):                                  │
  │  Header: Authorization: Bearer eyJhbGciOi...                    │
  │  Claims: {sub: "alice", group: "employer", exp: 1730000000}     │
  │  → "This request was made by user ALICE (an employer)"          │
  │                                                                  │
  │  AUTHORIZATION POLICY CAN CHECK BOTH:                            │
  │  • principals: [*/sa/anihpj-web] AND request.auth.claims[group]=employer │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, mTLS ensures only the legitimate anihpj-web service can call the API, and JWT ensures the end user (employer or job seeker) is authenticated. This two-layer identity model enables sophisticated policies: "the web service can access the API, but only for users with verified email addresses."

Security Q134 of 200
How do you forward JWT claims to backend services so the application can use them without verifying the JWT again?
A) Set outputPayloadToHeader in the RequestAuthentication jwtRules to forward the decoded JWT payload as an HTTP header. Set forwardOriginalToken: true to pass the original token. The backend receives verified claims in headers without needing to re-validate the JWT signature — Envoy already did the cryptographic verification
B) JWT claims cannot be forwarded — the backend must independently verify the JWT
C) Claims are automatically forwarded as environment variables — no configuration needed
D) Use a separate sidecar for claim forwarding — Envoy cannot modify response headers
✅ Correct Answer: A

Envoy validates the JWT cryptographically, then forwards the decoded claims as headers. Your Django app reads X-User-Id: alice from the header — no JWT library needed, no signature verification, no key management. The mesh handles authentication; the app just reads pre-verified user data.

📝 Detailed Explanation
JWT Claim Forwarding — Pre-Verified Headers to Backend
  RequestAuthentication:
  jwtRules:
  - issuer: auth.jobpost.com
    jwksUri: .../.well-known/jwks.json
    forwardOriginalToken: true
    outputPayloadToHeader: X-User-Payload
    fromHeaders: [{name: Authorization}]
  
  FLOW:
  Client → Authorization: Bearer 
  → Envoy validates: signature ✅ issuer ✅ expiry ✅
  → Envoy forwards:
      X-User-Payload: {"sub":"alice","group":"employer","email":"alice@jobpost.com"}
      Authorization: Bearer   (if forwardOriginalToken: true)
  → Django reads request.headers['X-User-Payload'] → knows user is alice

For anihpj/jobpost, configure outputPayloadToHeader to pass pre-verified user claims to Django. The Django app reads request.headers['X-User-Payload'] and knows the user is authenticated without importing any JWT library. The mesh handles all cryptographic verification; the app just reads headers.

Security Q135 of 200
How does AuthorizationPolicy for ingress gateway differ from sidecar-level AuthorizationPolicy?
A) Ingress gateway AuthorizationPolicy is configured in istio-system namespace only — sidecar policies are in app namespace
B) They are identical — the same AuthorizationPolicy works for both gateway and sidecar
C) An ingress gateway AuthorizationPolicy typically matches on external attributes like source IP blocks, JWT claims, and request paths — it's the first line of defense at the mesh boundary. Sidecar AuthorizationPolicies match on internal attributes like SPIFFE principals and namespaces. Gateway policies often ALLOW authenticated users; sidecar policies enforce service-to-service least privilege
D) Ingress policies can only DENY — ALLOW is only for sidecar policies
✅ Correct Answer: C

Gateway policies guard the perimeter (block malicious IPs, require JWT, rate-limit paths). Sidecar policies enforce internal zero-trust (API can talk to DB, web cannot). Same CRD, different attack surface.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Gateway vs Sidecar AuthorizationPolicy — Different Contexts
  GATEWAY POLICY (perimeter):              SIDECAR POLICY (internal):
  ┌────────────────────────────┐          ┌────────────────────────────┐
  │ selector:                   │          │ selector:                   │
  │   istio: ingressgateway    │          │   app: anihpj-db            │
  │ action: ALLOW               │          │ action: ALLOW               │
  │ rules:                      │          │ rules:                      │
  │ - from:                     │          │ - from:                     │
  │   - source:                 │          │   - source:                 │
  │       ipBlocks: [allowed]   │          │       principals: [api-sa]  │
  │       requestPrincipals:[*] │          │ to:                        │
  │ to:                         │          │ - operation: {ports:[5432]}│
  │ - operation:                │          │                              │
  │     paths: [/api/public/*]  │          │ Internal zero-trust: only    │
  └────────────────────────────┘          │ API SA can connect to DB     │
  Perimeter security: IP + JWT            └────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, deploy an AuthorizationPolicy on the ingress gateway to require valid JWT tokens and block known malicious IPs. Deploy separate sidecar AuthorizationPolicies to enforce that only anihpj-api can access PostgreSQL — two layers of defense at two different trust boundaries.

4.2b AuthorizationPolicy Advanced — Testing, Ext AuthZ & Multi-PolicyQ136-Q142

Security Q136 of 200
How do you test an AuthorizationPolicy before deploying it to production?
A) AuthorizationPolicies cannot be tested — you must deploy and observe
B) Apply the policy and check kubectl events for validation errors
C) Use Kiali to simulate requests — it shows which policies would match
D) Use istioctl experimental authz check <pod> -n <ns> to simulate requests against the policy and see ALLOW/DENY results. Deploy to a test namespace first with identical labels. Use istioctl analyze to catch configuration errors. Test with both positive (should-allow) and negative (should-deny) cases before production rollout
✅ Correct Answer: D

istioctl experimental authz check dry-runs your policy against a pod and shows exactly which rules match. Test positive cases (legitimate access should be ALLOWED) and negative cases (unauthorized access should be DENIED) before deploying to production.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Testing AuthorizationPolicy — Dry-Run Validation
  $ istioctl experimental authz check anihpj-db-0 -n anihpj \
      --source-principal cluster.local/ns/anihpj/sa/anihpj-api \
      --destination-port 5432
  
  RESULT: ALLOW ✅ (matches rule: api SA → DB port 5432)
  
  $ istioctl experimental authz check anihpj-db-0 -n anihpj \
      --source-principal cluster.local/ns/anihpj/sa/anihpj-web \
      --destination-port 5432
  
  RESULT: DENY ❌ (no matching ALLOW rule for web SA → DB)
  
  🔑 Test in CI/CD pipeline before merging AuthorizationPolicy changes

For anihpj/jobpost, test every AuthorizationPolicy with istioctl experimental authz check in CI/CD. Verify that anihpj-api → anihpj-db:5432 is ALLOWED, and anihpj-web → anihpj-db:5432 is DENIED. Only merge policies that pass both positive and negative test cases.

Security Q137 of 200
What is CUSTOM action (ext_authz) in AuthorizationPolicy and how does it enable complex authorization logic?
A) CUSTOM action is a deprecated feature — use ALLOW/DENY only
B) CUSTOM action delegates authorization decisions to an external authorization service via HTTP or gRPC. Envoy sends the request context (headers, source, destination, path) to the external service, which returns ALLOW or DENY. This enables: OPA (Open Policy Agent) with Rego policies, custom business logic (e.g., "only allow during business hours"), integration with enterprise IAM systems. The external service is configured in the mesh's extension provider
C) CUSTOM action requires a commercial Istio addon — not available in open-source
D) The external service must be written in Go — other languages are not supported for ext_authz
✅ Correct Answer: B

ext_authz enables arbitrary authorization logic. Write policies in Rego (OPA), Python, or any language. Check business hours, user quotas, fraud scores — anything beyond simple principal/port matching. Envoy calls your service; your service returns ALLOW or DENY.

📝 Detailed Explanation
CUSTOM ext_authz — Delegate Authorization to External Service
  AuthorizationPolicy:
  action: CUSTOM
  provider:
    name: opa-authz          ← References mesh extension provider
  rules:
  - to:
    - operation: {paths: ["/api/admin/*"]}
  
  FLOW:
  Request → Envoy → HTTP/gRPC → OPA Service (ext_authz)
                              │
                              ├─► Check Rego policy
                              ├─► Verify business logic
                              └─► Return: ALLOW or DENY
                              │
  Envoy ◄──────────────────────┘
  → ALLOW: Forward to backend
  → DENY: Return 403 to client

For anihpj/jobpost, use ext_authz with OPA to implement complex policies: "employers can only post 5 jobs per day," "premium users can access salary data," "admin users can access everything during business hours only." These policies are written in Rego and enforced at the mesh layer — Django never sees unauthorized requests.

Security Q138 of 200
How do multiple AuthorizationPolicies combine — what happens when two ALLOW policies exist for the same workload?
A) ALLOW policies are OR'd — if ANY ALLOW policy matches, the request is permitted. DENY policies are evaluated first and take precedence. If multiple ALLOW policies exist, the request is allowed if it matches ANY of them. This means you can compose policies from different teams: the security team's policy + the application team's policy both apply, and matching either one grants access
B) ALLOW policies are AND'd — the request must match ALL policies to be permitted
C) The most recently created policy wins — older policies are automatically deprecated
D) Multiple policies for the same workload cause a conflict error — Istio rejects the configuration
✅ Correct Answer: A

ALLOW policies OR together — any matching ALLOW grants access. This enables policy composition: the platform team can define base security policies, while application teams add service-specific access rules. The DENY layer always overrides everything — use it for security-enforced blocklisting.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Multiple AuthorizationPolicies — OR Logic
  POLICY A (platform team):               POLICY B (app team):
  action: ALLOW                           action: ALLOW
  rules:                                  rules:
  - from:                                 - to:
    - source:                             - operation:
        namespaces: [istio-system]          paths: ["/api/public/*"]
    → Allows monitoring tools
  
  COMBINED RESULT (OR):
  Request from istio-system → ALLOWED (matches Policy A)
  Request to /api/public/*  → ALLOWED (matches Policy B)
  Request from other → /api/private → DENIED (matches neither)
  → DEFAULT DENY applies when no ALLOW policy matches

For anihpj/jobpost, the platform team creates an ALLOW policy for monitoring tools, while the application team creates an ALLOW policy for public API paths. Both policies coexist — a request matching either one is permitted. This enables clean separation of security responsibilities across teams.

Security Q139 of 200
What is the default behavior when no AuthorizationPolicies exist in a namespace — are requests allowed or denied?
A) Default deny — all traffic is blocked until you create an ALLOW policy
B) Default depend on PeerAuthentication mode — PERMISSIVE allows, STRICT denies
C) Default allow: If no AuthorizationPolicies exist in a namespace, ALL requests are permitted. Once you create ANY AuthorizationPolicy (even a single ALLOW rule), the namespace switches to default deny — only requests matching an ALLOW rule are permitted. This prevents accidentally locking yourself out when creating the first policy
D) Default behavior is random — it depends on the order policies were created
✅ Correct Answer: C

No policies = everything allowed (safe default for initial mesh setup). First ALLOW policy = switch to default-deny (only explicit ALLOWs permitted). This design prevents accidentally locking yourself out when you create your first security policy.

📝 Detailed Explanation
AuthorizationPolicy — Default Allow to Default Deny Transition
  NO POLICIES (default allow):
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Any request → anihpj-db:5432 → ALLOWED (no policies = permit)  │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
  
  AFTER FIRST ALLOW POLICY (default deny):
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  AuthorizationPolicy: {action: ALLOW, rules: [{...api-sa...}]}  │
  │  anihpj-api → anihpj-db:5432 → ALLOWED (matches rule)          │
  │  anihpj-web → anihpj-db:5432 → DENIED (no matching rule)       │
  │  any-other → anihpj-db:5432 → DENIED (default deny)            │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
  
  ⚠️  Always create ALLOW rules for your OWN access first!
      (e.g., monitoring tools, health checks)

For anihpj/jobpost, when creating the first AuthorizationPolicy, always include rules for monitoring and health checks FIRST. Then add the restrictive rules. This prevents accidentally blocking Prometheus scraping or kubelet probes when enabling default-deny for the anihpj namespace.

Security Q140 of 200
How do you implement egress access control — restricting which external services mesh pods can reach?
A) Egress access control is not possible — pods can always reach the internet
B) Use Kubernetes NetworkPolicy — Istio does not control egress traffic
C) Create a DENY AuthorizationPolicy targeting all external traffic — this blocks everything
D) Three-layer egress control: 1) Set meshConfig.outboundTrafficPolicy.mode: REGISTRY_ONLY (blocks all non-registered hosts). 2) Create ServiceEntry for each allowed external host. 3) Optionally add AuthorizationPolicy on the egress gateway to control which mesh services can access which external hosts. Together: only registered hosts are reachable, and only authorized mesh services can reach them
✅ Correct Answer: D

Egress control = REGISTRY_ONLY (block all) + ServiceEntry (allow specific) + AuthorizationPolicy (restrict which mesh services). This is zero-trust egress: by default nothing can leave the mesh; you explicitly register and authorize every external dependency.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Zero-Trust Egress — Three-Layer Access Control
  LAYER 1: meshConfig.outboundTrafficPolicy.mode: REGISTRY_ONLY
  → ALL external traffic BLOCKED by default
  
  LAYER 2: ServiceEntry for approved hosts:
  - hosts: [api.stripe.com]
  - hosts: [smtp.mailgun.net]
  → Only these hosts are reachable
  
  LAYER 3: AuthorizationPolicy on egress gateway:
  action: ALLOW
  rules:
  - from: [{source: {principals: ["*/sa/anihpj-api"]}}]
    to: [{operation: {hosts: ["api.stripe.com"]}}]
  → Only anihpj-api can reach Stripe; web frontend cannot
  
  RESULT:
  ✅ anihpj-api → api.stripe.com (ALLOWED: SE + AuthZ match)
  ❌ anihpj-web → api.stripe.com (DENIED: AuthZ blocks)
  ❌ any pod → unknown.com (DENIED: REGISTRY_ONLY blocks)

For anihpj/jobpost, implement zero-trust egress: REGISTRY_ONLY blocks all external traffic, ServiceEntries register Stripe and Mailgun, AuthorizationPolicy ensures only the API SA can call Stripe. Even if the web frontend is compromised, it cannot exfiltrate data to arbitrary external servers.

Security Q141 of 200
What is audit logging for security events in Istio and how do you enable it?
A) Istio does not support audit logging — you must use an external SIEM
B) Enable Envoy access logging via meshConfig.accessLogFile or Telemetry CRD. Access logs capture every request with source/destination, TLS status, response code, and latency. For authorization decisions, Envoy logs allow/deny events. Combined with Prometheus metrics and distributed tracing, this provides complete security observability — who called what, was it encrypted, was it allowed
C) Audit logging is enabled by default — no configuration needed
D) Access logs only capture HTTP traffic — TCP and gRPC are not logged
✅ Correct Answer: B

Envoy access logs are the security audit trail. Every request is logged: source IP, SPIFFE identity, destination, TLS mode, response code. Filter for DENIED authorization decisions to detect attack attempts. Ship to your SIEM for compliance and threat detection.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Envoy Access Log — Security Audit Trail
  MeshConfig:
  accessLogFile: /dev/stdout
  accessLogEncoding: JSON
  accessLogFormat: |
    {
      "source": "%REQ(:AUTHORITY)%",
      "method": "%REQ(:METHOD)%",
      "path": "%REQ(X-ENVOY-ORIGINAL-PATH?:PATH)%",
      "responseCode": "%RESPONSE_CODE%",
      "tlsVersion": "%DOWNSTREAM_TLS_VERSION%",
      "principal": "%DYNAMIC_METADATA(istio-authz:request.auth.principal)%"
    }
  
  LOG ENTRY:
  {"source":"anihpj-web","method":"GET","path":"/api/jobs",
   "responseCode":"200","tlsVersion":"TLSv1.3",
   "principal":"cluster.local/ns/anihpj/sa/anihpj-web"}

For anihpj/jobpost, enable JSON-format access logs with SPIFFE principal and TLS version. Ship logs to your SIEM (Elasticsearch/Splunk). Alert on: DENIED authorization decisions (potential attack), plaintext connections under STRICT mTLS (policy violation), and requests from unknown principals (possible compromise).

Security Q142 of 200
What is the principle of least privilege applied to Istio security, and how do you implement it?
A) Least privilege means every service gets ONLY the access it needs — nothing more. Implementation: 1) Enable mesh-wide STRICT mTLS (encrypt everything). 2) Create ALLOW AuthorizationPolicies that explicitly list which services can access which ports/paths. 3) Use REGISTRY_ONLY for egress (block all external, whitelist needed hosts). 4) Use DENY policies for known threats. 5) Default deny after first ALLOW policy. Result: by default, no service can talk to any other service — you must explicitly grant each communication path
B) Least privilege means using PERMISSIVE mTLS — services auto-negotiate the minimum security
C) Least privilege is enforced by Kubernetes RBAC — Istio does not add anything
D) Least privilege only applies to ingress traffic — internal mesh traffic is implicitly trusted
✅ Correct Answer: A

Zero-trust = deny by default, allow by exception. STRICT mTLS encrypts everything. AuthorizationPolicy allows only what's needed. REGISTRY_ONLY blocks unknown egress. This is the security posture for production Istio: nothing communicates unless you explicitly permit it.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Least Privilege Implementation — Five-Step Lockdown
  STEP 1: STRICT mTLS (mesh-wide)
  → All traffic encrypted + authenticated
  
  STEP 2: AuthorizationPolicy ALLOW rules
  → anihpj-api → anihpj-db:5432 ✅
  → anihpj-web → anihpj-api:8000 ✅
  → Everything else: DENIED ❌
  
  STEP 3: REGISTRY_ONLY egress
  → api.stripe.com (ServiceEntry) ✅
  → Everything else: DENIED ❌
  
  STEP 4: DENY rules for threats
  → Block known malicious IPs
  
  STEP 5: Audit logging
  → Monitor for DENIED requests (attack detection)
  
  RESULT: By default, nothing can communicate.
          Every allowed path is explicit and audited.

For anihpj/jobpost, implement least privilege: STRICT mTLS between all services, ALLOW policies for web→api→db communication paths, REGISTRY_ONLY egress with ServiceEntries for Stripe and Mailgun only, DENY policies for threat response, and JSON access logging for audit. This is the production security baseline for the job board.

4.3 TLS & Certificate Management — Edge TLS, Rotation & Custom CAQ143-Q150

Security Q143 of 200
How do you configure edge TLS on the Istio ingress gateway to terminate HTTPS traffic for the jobpost.com domain?
A) Edge TLS is automatically configured — the ingress gateway uses self-signed certificates by default
B) 1) Create a Kubernetes TLS Secret containing the certificate and private key. 2) Create a Gateway resource with port 443, protocol HTTPS, hosts [jobpost.com], and tls.mode: SIMPLE referencing the Secret. 3) Create a VirtualService bound to the Gateway to route traffic to the backend. The Gateway Envoy terminates TLS; internal mesh traffic uses mTLS separately
C) Edge TLS requires a cloud load balancer — the ingress gateway cannot terminate TLS
D) Use PASSTHROUGH mode — the backend Django application must terminate TLS, not the gateway
✅ Correct Answer: B

Gateway TLS termination is the standard pattern. The Gateway Envoy handles TLS (offloading crypto from the backend), then forwards plain HTTP to services inside the mesh. Inside the mesh, mTLS re-encrypts traffic between sidecars — so data is encrypted end-to-end from client to backend with a brief decrypt/re-encrypt at the gateway.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Edge TLS Termination — HTTPS at the Gateway
  kubectl create secret tls jobpost-cert -n istio-system \
    --cert=/path/to/cert.pem --key=/path/to/key.pem
  
  Gateway:
  servers:
  - port: {number: 443, name: https, protocol: HTTPS}
    hosts: [jobpost.com, www.jobpost.com]
    tls:
      mode: SIMPLE
      credentialName: jobpost-cert   ← References K8s Secret
  
  CLIENT → HTTPS (TLS) → Gateway Envoy → HTTP → anihpj-web Envoy → mTLS → anihpj-api
  └─ TLS terminated here ─┘                    └─ mTLS inside mesh ─┘

For anihpj/jobpost, create a TLS Secret with your production certificate (Let's Encrypt or corporate CA), configure the Gateway with SIMPLE TLS mode, and the job board is available at https://jobpost.com with a valid certificate. Internal traffic between web and API remains encrypted via mTLS.

Security Q144 of 200
When should you use PASSTHROUGH TLS mode vs SIMPLE TLS mode on the ingress gateway?
A) SIMPLE: Gateway terminates TLS, forwards plain HTTP to backend — use when the gateway manages certificates (standard HTTPS). PASSTHROUGH: Gateway forwards TLS untouched to the backend based on SNI hostname — use when the BACKEND must handle its own TLS (e.g., backend does client certificate validation, or you need end-to-end TLS without gateway decryption). PASSTHROUGH is also used for non-HTTP TLS traffic like database connections
B) PASSTHROUGH is only for gRPC — HTTP/HTTPS always uses SIMPLE
C) SIMPLE mode is more secure than PASSTHROUGH — always prefer SIMPLE
D) PASSTHROUGH requires Mutual TLS — both client and server must present certificates
✅ Correct Answer: A

SIMPLE = gateway manages TLS (offload crypto, one cert for the domain). PASSTHROUGH = backend manages TLS (SNI routing, end-to-end encryption without gateway decryption). Use PASSTHROUGH for compliance (no decryption at edge) or when the backend needs to validate client certificates.

📝 Detailed Explanation
SIMPLE vs PASSTHROUGH — Where TLS Terminates
  SIMPLE (gateway terminates):             PASSTHROUGH (backend terminates):
  Client ──TLS──► GW ──HTTP──► Backend     Client ──TLS──► GW ──TLS──► Backend
                  (GW decrypts)                            (GW passes through)
  
  Use SIMPLE for:                          Use PASSTHROUGH for:
  • Standard HTTPS websites                • Backend client-cert validation
  • TLS offloading (reduce backend CPU)    • End-to-end encryption compliance
  • Centralized certificate management     • SNI-based multi-tenant routing
  • WAF/rate-limiting at gateway            • Non-HTTP TLS (databases, etc.)

For anihpj/jobpost, use SIMPLE mode for the main jobpost.com website (Gateway manages Let's Encrypt cert). Use PASSTHROUGH for the admin database connection, where PostgreSQL handles its own TLS with client certificate validation — the gateway just routes by SNI without decrypting.

Security Q145 of 200
How do you rotate ingress gateway TLS certificates without downtime?
A) Delete and recreate the Gateway — downtime is unavoidable during certificate rotation
B) Update the Secret and restart the ingress gateway pod — 5-10 seconds of downtime
C) Update the Kubernetes TLS Secret with the new certificate and key. The Gateway Envoy watches the Secret and automatically reloads the new certificate without restarting — zero downtime. For seamless rotation: create the new Secret with a different name, update the Gateway's credentialName, then delete the old Secret. Envoy transitions smoothly to the new certificate
D) Certificate rotation requires istioctl certs rotate command — Secret updates are not detected
✅ Correct Answer: C

Gateway Envoy watches the Kubernetes Secret and hot-reloads certificates. Update the Secret → Envoy picks up the new cert within seconds → existing connections complete with old cert, new connections use new cert → zero dropped connections, zero downtime. Seamless rotation.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Zero-Downtime Certificate Rotation — Hot Reload
  STEP 1: Create new Secret with new cert
  kubectl create secret tls jobpost-cert-v2 -n istio-system \
    --cert=new-cert.pem --key=new-key.pem
  
  STEP 2: Update Gateway to reference new Secret
  spec:
    servers:
    - tls:
        mode: SIMPLE
        credentialName: jobpost-cert-v2  ← Switch to new Secret
  
  STEP 3: Envoy detects Secret change → loads new cert
  → Existing connections: old cert (valid for ~minutes)
  → New connections: new cert immediately
  → Zero connection drops, zero downtime
  
  STEP 4: Delete old Secret (after verifying)
  kubectl delete secret jobpost-cert -n istio-system

For anihpj/jobpost, rotate TLS certificates monthly: create a new Secret with the renewed certificate, update the Gateway's credentialName, verify with curl, then delete the old Secret. The job board serves HTTPS continuously throughout the rotation — users never experience a certificate error.

Security Q146 of 200
How do you check when certificates expire in your Istio mesh — both ingress gateway certs and workload mTLS certs?
A) Certificate expiry is shown in kubectl describe pod — check each pod individually
B) Istio automatically alerts when certificates are about to expire — no manual checking needed
C) Only ingress certs can be checked — workload mTLS certs are not accessible
D) Gateway certs: kubectl get secret <name> -n istio-system -o jsonpath='{.data.tls\.crt}' | base64 -d | openssl x509 -noout -dates. Workload mTLS certs: istioctl proxy-config secret <pod> -n <ns> -o json | jq -r '..|.inlineBytes?|select(.)' | base64 -d | openssl x509 -noout -dates. Also: istioctl proxy-config secret shows cert expiry for all Envoys
✅ Correct Answer: D

Two separate certificate types: Gateway TLS certs (managed by you, in K8s Secrets) and workload mTLS certs (managed by istiod CA, auto-rotated every 24h). Check both regularly. Gateway certs have longer lifetimes (90 days typical); workload certs auto-rotate but you should verify the rotation is working.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Certificate Expiry Check — Gateway + Workload Certs
  GATEWAY CERTS:
  $ kubectl get secret jobpost-cert -n istio-system \
      -o jsonpath='{.data.tls\.crt}' | base64 -d | \
      openssl x509 -noout -dates -subject
  notBefore=Jun 1 00:00:00 2026 GMT
  notAfter=Aug 30 00:00:00 2026 GMT       ← Expires in ~80 days
  subject=CN=jobpost.com
  
  WORKLOAD mTLS CERTS:
  $ istioctl proxy-config secret anihpj-web-xxx -n anihpj -o json | \
      jq -r '..|.inlineBytes?|select(.)' | base64 -d | \
      openssl x509 -noout -dates -subject
  notBefore=Jun 12 10:00:00 2026 GMT
  notAfter=Jun 13 10:00:00 2026 GMT       ← 24h lifetime, auto-rotates
  subject=spiffe://cluster.local/ns/anihpj/sa/anihpj-web

For anihpj/jobpost, set up automated monitoring: check gateway certs weekly (alert at 14 days before expiry), verify workload cert rotation daily (alert if any Envoy has certs expiring in < 6 hours). The 24h workload cert rotation is automatic, but monitoring confirms it's working.

Security Q147 of 200
What is the MUTUAL TLS mode on the ingress gateway and when should you use it?
A) MUTUAL mode is the same as PASSTHROUGH — they are aliases for the same feature
B) MUTUAL mode requires the CLIENT to present a certificate that the gateway validates. The gateway also presents its own server certificate. This enables client-certificate-based authentication at the mesh edge — only clients with a valid certificate signed by your CA can connect. Use for: B2B APIs, service-to-service communication across organizations, or high-security internal services
C) MUTUAL mode is automatically enabled when PeerAuthentication is STRICT — no Gateway config needed
D) MUTUAL mode only works with SPIFFE certificates — standard X.509 client certs are not supported
✅ Correct Answer: B

Gateway MUTUAL TLS = client certificate authentication at the edge. Both sides prove identity. The gateway presents its server certificate AND requires the client to present a certificate signed by a trusted CA. This is stronger than JWT — the client identity is cryptographically bound to a certificate.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Gateway MUTUAL TLS — Client Certificate Authentication
  Gateway:
  servers:
  - port: {number: 443, protocol: HTTPS}
    hosts: [api.jobpost.com]
    tls:
      mode: MUTUAL
      credentialName: jobpost-server-cert     ← Server cert (presented to client)
      caCertificates: partner-ca-cert          ← CA that signed client certs
      (optionally) subjectAltNames: [partner.com]  ← Restrict client cert SANs
  
  HANDSHAKE:
  ① Client → Gateway: ClientHello
  ② Gateway → Client: ServerHello + ServerCert
  ③ Gateway → Client: CertificateRequest (require client cert)
  ④ Client → Gateway: ClientCert
  ⑤ Gateway validates: ClientCert signed by partner-ca-cert?
  ⑥ ✅ mTLS established → request forwarded inside mesh

For anihpj/jobpost, use MUTUAL TLS on a dedicated API gateway for B2B partners who need programmatic access to the job board. Each partner gets a client certificate signed by your partner CA. The Gateway validates their certificate before any request reaches the API — no API keys, no JWT, just cryptographic identity.

Security Q148 of 200
How does SNI (Server Name Indication) work with Istio gateways for hosting multiple TLS-enabled hosts on the same IP?
A) SNI lets the client specify which hostname it wants during the TLS handshake (before HTTP). The Gateway Envoy reads the SNI field and routes to the correct server configuration — enabling multiple TLS hosts on the same port 443. Each server block in the Gateway can have different TLS certificates. PASSTHROUGH mode relies entirely on SNI to route TLS connections without decrypting them
B) SNI requires a separate IP address per hostname — it doesn't work on a single port
C) SNI is only for HTTP/2 — HTTP/1.1 uses the Host header instead
D) SNI configuration requires EnvoyFilter — Gateway servers cannot use SNI natively
✅ Correct Answer: A

SNI is how one Gateway Envoy serves multiple HTTPS domains on a single IP:port. The client sends the target hostname during TLS setup, and Envoy selects the correct certificate and routing configuration. No separate IPs, no separate load balancers.

📝 Detailed Explanation
SNI — Multiple TLS Hosts, One Gateway, One Port
  Gateway (one Envoy, port 443):
  servers:
  - port: {number: 443, protocol: HTTPS}
    hosts: [jobpost.com]
    tls: {mode: SIMPLE, credentialName: jobpost-cert}
  - port: {number: 443, protocol: HTTPS}
    hosts: [api.jobpost.com]
    tls: {mode: SIMPLE, credentialName: api-cert}
  - port: {number: 443, protocol: TLS}
    hosts: [db.jobpost.com]
    tls: {mode: PASSTHROUGH}          ← SNI routes without decrypting
  
  Client TLS → "I want api.jobpost.com" (SNI)
  → Envoy selects 2nd server block → api-cert → routes to API VS

For anihpj/jobpost, use SNI to host jobpost.com, api.jobpost.com, and db.jobpost.com all on the same ingress gateway IP and port 443. Each domain has its own TLS certificate, and the Gateway routes by SNI to the correct VirtualService — all without multiple load balancers.

Security Q149 of 200
What is the Istio security response process when a CVE (Common Vulnerability and Exposure) is discovered in Istio or Envoy?
A) Istio has no formal CVE process — you must monitor Envoy CVEs independently
B) CVEs are auto-patched by istiod — no manual action is needed
C) The Istio Security Team publishes CVEs with severity ratings and fixed versions. Response: 1) Check istioctl version for your current version. 2) Review the CVE for impact on your configuration. 3) If affected, perform a canary upgrade to the fixed version. 4) For Envoy CVEs, upgrade Istio (Envoy is bundled). 5) Use istioctl analyze to detect deprecated/insecure configurations. Subscribe to istio-security@ for notifications
D) Envoy CVEs require updating Envoy separately from Istio — they use different release cycles
✅ Correct Answer: C

Istio bundles Envoy, so a single Istio upgrade addresses both Istio and Envoy CVEs. Subscribe to security announcements, maintain a canary upgrade pipeline, and practice the upgrade process regularly so you can deploy security fixes quickly when needed.

📝 Detailed Explanation
CVE Response — Upgrade Pipeline
  ① DETECT: Subscribe to istio-security@googlegroups.com
     Monitor: istio.io/latest/news/security/
  
  ② ASSESS: Check if your configuration is affected
     $ istioctl version → You: 1.22.1, Fixed: 1.22.3
     → Upgrade needed (two patch versions behind)
  
  ③ UPGRADE (canary):
     $ istioctl install --set revision=1-22-3
     $ kubectl label ns anihpj istio.io/rev=1-22-3
     $ kubectl rollout restart deploy -n anihpj
  
  ④ VERIFY:
     $ istioctl proxy-status → All SYNCED
     $ istioctl version → 1.22.3

For anihpj/jobpost, maintain a canary upgrade pipeline so you can deploy CVE fixes within hours of announcement. Practice the upgrade process monthly so it's muscle memory when a critical CVE drops. Use the canary revision approach to roll out security patches with zero downtime.

Security Q150 of 200
What is the complete security posture for anihpj/jobpost in production — combining all Istio security features?
A) Just enable STRICT mTLS — that's sufficient for production security
B) Security is handled by the cloud provider — Istio doesn't add meaningful security
C) Istio security features are mutually exclusive — pick one: mTLS, JWT, or AuthorizationPolicy
D) Defense-in-depth: 1) STRICT mTLS mesh-wide (all traffic encrypted + authenticated). 2) AuthorizationPolicy ALLOW rules (least-privilege service-to-service access). 3) RequestAuthentication on ingress (JWT validation for end users). 4) Gateway SIMPLE TLS (HTTPS for external access). 5) REGISTRY_ONLY egress with ServiceEntries (controlled external access). 6) DENY policies for threat response. 7) Access logging for audit. 8) Custom CA for enterprise PKI. Together: zero-trust mesh where every connection is encrypted, authenticated, and authorized
✅ Correct Answer: D

No single feature is sufficient. STRICT mTLS encrypts everything. AuthorizationPolicy enforces who can talk to whom. JWT validates end users. Gateway TLS protects the perimeter. REGISTRY_ONLY controls egress. Together: complete zero-trust mesh security.

📝 Detailed Explanation
anihpj/jobpost — Complete Production Security Posture
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  LAYER 1: PERIMETER                                             │
  │  • Gateway SIMPLE TLS (jobpost.com, api.jobpost.com)            │
  │  • RequestAuthentication: JWT validation for end users          │
  │  • AuthorizationPolicy: Block malicious IPs, require valid JWT  │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  LAYER 2: INTERNAL MESH                                         │
  │  • STRICT mTLS: All pod-to-pod traffic encrypted                │
  │  • AuthorizationPolicy ALLOW:                                     │
  │    ✅ anihpj-web → anihpj-api:8000                              │
  │    ✅ anihpj-api → anihpj-db:5432                               │
  │    ❌ Everything else DENIED                                    │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  LAYER 3: EGRESS                                                │
  │  • REGISTRY_ONLY mode                                           │
  │  • ServiceEntry: api.stripe.com, smtp.mailgun.net               │
  │  • AuthorizationPolicy: Only anihpj-api can reach Stripe        │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  LAYER 4: OBSERVABILITY                                         │
  │  • Envoy JSON access logging (every request audited)            │
  │  • Prometheus: istio_requests_total{connection_security_policy} │
  │  • Alert: DENIED requests, plaintext under STRICT               │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, this defense-in-depth posture ensures: external users connect via HTTPS with valid JWT tokens, internal services communicate over mTLS with least-privilege access control, external dependencies are explicitly whitelisted through the egress gateway, and every request is audited. This is the production security baseline for a zero-trust service mesh.

4.4 Observability — Metrics, Tracing, Logging & VisualizationQ151-Q160

Security Q151 of 200
What are the Golden Signals (RED metrics) that Istio automatically provides for every service in the mesh?
A) CPU, Memory, Disk, and Network — the four infrastructure metrics
B) Pod count, restart count, image version, and node name — deployment-level metrics
C) Only request count is available — error rate and latency require application instrumentation
D) Rate (istio_requests_total — requests per second), Errors (istio_requests_total{response_code=~"5.."} — error rate), Duration (istio_request_duration_milliseconds — latency percentiles p50/p90/p99). These are reported by EVERY Envoy sidecar automatically — zero application code changes. Reported from BOTH source and destination perspectives for every service-to-service call
✅ Correct Answer: D

RED metrics (Rate, Errors, Duration) are the three signals every service needs. Istio provides them automatically via Envoy — no code changes, no SDKs, no libraries. Every Envoy reports these metrics from both client and server perspectives, giving you complete visibility into every service interaction.

📝 Detailed Explanation
RED Metrics — The Golden Signals for Every Service
  RATE (requests per second):
  sum(rate(istio_requests_total{destination_service="anihpj-api"}[5m]))
  → 500 req/s on the job board API
  
  ERRORS (error rate %):
  sum(rate(istio_requests_total{destination_service="anihpj-api",
        response_code=~"5.."}[5m])) /
  sum(rate(istio_requests_total{destination_service="anihpj-api"}[5m]))
  → 0.1% error rate (1 in 1000 requests fails)
  
  DURATION (latency):
  histogram_quantile(0.99,
    sum(rate(istio_request_duration_milliseconds_bucket{
      destination_service="anihpj-api"}[5m])) by (le))
  → p99 = 200ms (99% of requests complete within 200ms)
  
  🔑 Reported by Envoy automatically — from BOTH source and destination

For anihpj/jobpost, RED metrics are the heartbeat of the job board. Monitor them in Grafana: if Rate spikes (traffic surge), if Errors climb above 0.5% (degradation), or if p99 Duration exceeds 500ms (slowdown). These three signals tell you everything about service health without looking at infrastructure metrics.

  POD ANNOTATIONS (auto-added by Istio):
  prometheus.io/scrape: "true"
  prometheus.io/port: "15020"
  prometheus.io/path: "/stats/prometheus"
  
  PROMETHEUS → scrapes every Envoy every 15s → stores metrics
  
  KEY METRICS AVAILABLE:

For anihpj/jobpost, deploy the Prometheus Operator with a ServiceMonitor targeting the istio-proxy containers. All anihpj-web, anihpj-api, and anihpj-db Envoys are automatically scraped — RED metrics appear in Grafana dashboards within minutes of deployment.

Security Q153 of 200
What are Envoy access logs and how do you enable them for security auditing and debugging?
A) Envoy access logs record EVERY request with: source/destination, HTTP method, path, response code, latency, TLS version, and SPIFFE principal. Enable via meshConfig.accessLogFile: /dev/stdout (text) or configure JSON format via meshConfig.accessLogEncoding: JSON. Each Envoy writes its own access log — both source and destination Envoys log the same request, giving dual-perspective audit trails
B) Access logs are enabled by default in JSON format — no configuration needed
C) Access logs only capture 5xx errors — successful requests are not logged to reduce volume
D) Access logs require a separate logging sidecar — Envoy cannot write logs directly
✅ Correct Answer: A

Access logs are the definitive audit trail. Every request, both successful and failed, is logged with full metadata. Source and destination Envoys both log — you see the request from both sides. JSON format makes them machine-readable for SIEM ingestion.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Envoy Access Log — Full Request Audit Trail
  MeshConfig:
  accessLogFile: /dev/stdout
  accessLogEncoding: JSON
  
  LOG ENTRY (source Envoy):
  {
    "authority": "anihpj-api.anihpj:8000",
    "method": "POST",
    "path": "/api/jobs",
    "response_code": 201,
    "response_flags": "-",
    "upstream_host": "10.244.2.3:8000",
    "duration_ms": 45,
    "downstream_tls_version": "TLSv1.3",
    "source_principal": "cluster.local/ns/anihpj/sa/anihpj-web",
    "bytes_received": 1024,
    "bytes_sent": 256
  }

For anihpj/jobpost, enable JSON access logs and ship them to Elasticsearch via Fluentd. Query for: all 5xx errors (debug outages), slow requests > 1000ms (performance), unauthorized calls from unknown principals (security threats). The dual-perspective logging from both source and destination Envoys gives you complete forensic audit capability.

Security Q154 of 200
What is distributed tracing and how does Istio enable it without application code changes?
A) Distributed tracing requires adding OpenTelemetry SDKs to every application — Istio cannot generate traces automatically
B) Istio only traces ingress/egress traffic — internal mesh traffic is not traced
C) Istio's Envoy sidecars automatically generate and propagate trace spans for every HTTP/gRPC request. Envoy creates spans at BOTH source and destination, injects trace headers (b3, W3C trace-context), and sends spans to Jaeger or Zipkin. Zero application code changes — the sidecar handles all tracing. This gives you end-to-end visibility: anihpj-web → anihpj-api → anihpj-db as a single trace with timing at each hop
D) Tracing only works with HTTP/1.1 — gRPC and TCP are not supported
✅ Correct Answer: C

Envoy handles all tracing automatically. When anihpj-web calls anihpj-api, Envoy creates a span, injects trace headers, and the API's Envoy creates a child span. Jaeger collects all spans and reconstructs the full trace — showing exactly where time was spent across all service hops.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Distributed Tracing — Automatic Span Generation
  REQUEST: anihpj-web → anihpj-api → anihpj-db
  
  TRACE (Jaeger visualization):
  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  TRACE ID: a1b2c3d4                                       │
  │                                                            │
  │  anihpj-web  │███████████ (120ms)                          │
  │  anihpj-api  │   ████████████████████ (200ms)              │
  │  anihpj-db   │      ██ (10ms)                              │
  │                                                            │
  │  TOTAL: 330ms                                              │
  │  → 120ms in web (processing + network)                     │
  │  → 200ms in API (business logic + DB call)                 │
  │  → 10ms in PostgreSQL (query execution)                    │
  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
  
  🔑 All spans generated by Envoy — Django never knows tracing exists

For anihpj/jobpost, deploy Jaeger alongside Istio. Every job posting search, application submission, and admin action appears as a full trace. Debug slow requests by drilling into the trace: if the API span is 200ms but the DB span is 10ms, the bottleneck is in the Django code, not PostgreSQL.

Security Q157 of 200
How do you use istioctl analyze to validate mesh configuration from the CLI or CI/CD pipeline?
A) istioctl analyze -n <ns> performs static analysis of all Istio CRDs in the namespace. It reports Info (suggestions), Warning (potential issues), and Error (definite problems) with message codes like IST0108 (host not found) or IST0113 (subset has no pods). Run it before kubectl apply in CI/CD to catch configuration errors; run it against a live cluster to audit existing config. Supports --all-namespaces for mesh-wide audit
B) istioctl analyze is a runtime monitoring tool — it watches live traffic for anomalies
C) istioctl analyze only checks syntax — it cannot detect semantic issues like host mismatches
D) istioctl analyze requires a running cluster — it cannot analyze YAML files directly
✅ Correct Answer: A

istioctl analyze is your pre-flight config validator. Run it against YAML files before applying, or against a live cluster for auditing. It catches the mistakes that cause silent failures — wrong hostnames, empty subsets, conflicting rules. Integrate into CI/CD to block broken config from reaching production.

📝 Detailed Explanation
istioctl analyze — Pre-Deployment Configuration Validation
  CI/CD PIPELINE:
  $ istioctl analyze -n anihpj -f istio/
  
  ✅ Info [IST0102] Namespace anihpj is enabled for injection
  ⚠️  Warning [IST0108] VirtualService anihpj-api-vs:
      destination host "anihpj-api-v1" not found
  ❌ Error [IST0113] DestinationRule anihpj-api-dr:
      subset "v3" has no matching pods
  
  → CI/CD BLOCKS deployment if any Error-level issues found
  
  LIVE CLUSTER AUDIT:
  $ istioctl analyze --all-namespaces
  → Scans entire mesh for configuration issues

For anihpj/jobpost, add istioctl analyze -n anihpj to your CI/CD pipeline. Any Error-level issue blocks the PR from merging. This prevents misconfigured VirtualServices and DestinationRules from reaching production — catching issues that kubectl would happily accept but Envoy would silently ignore.

Security Q158 of 200
What is the Telemetry API in Istio and how does it replace the older mixer-based telemetry?
A) The Telemetry API is the same as the old mixer — just renamed for Istio 1.20+
B) The Telemetry API only controls tracing — metrics and logs are configured separately
C) The Telemetry API (telemetry.istio.io/v1alpha1) provides mesh-wide, namespace, or workload-level configuration for metrics, tracing, and access logging. It replaces the deprecated mixer component with Envoy-native telemetry. Key features: configure metrics provider overrides, add custom tags to metrics, set tracing sampling rate and provider, configure access log providers and filters. Much simpler than mixer — no external components needed
D) The Telemetry API requires deploying a separate Telemetry server — it's not built into Envoy
✅ Correct Answer: C

The Telemetry API is the modern way to configure Istio observability. No external mixer component — Envoy handles everything natively. Configure custom metrics tags, tracing sampling rate, and access log format all through a single CRD.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Telemetry API — Native Envoy Observability Configuration
  apiVersion: telemetry.istio.io/v1alpha1
  kind: Telemetry
  metadata: {name: anihpj-telemetry, namespace: anihpj}
  spec:
    metrics:
    - providers: [{name: prometheus}]
      overrides:
      - match: {metric: REQUEST_COUNT}
        tagOverrides:
          destination_port:  # Remove high-cardinality port tag
            operation: REMOVE
    tracing:
    - providers: [{name: jaeger}]
      randomSamplingPercentage: 10.0  ← Sample 10% of requests
    accessLogging:
    - providers: [{name: envoy}]
      filter:
        expression: "response.code >= 500"  ← Only log errors

For anihpj/jobpost, use the Telemetry API to set tracing sampling to 10% (reduce overhead), filter access logs to only 5xx errors (reduce log volume), and remove high-cardinality metric tags like destination port (prevent metric explosion). All configured declaratively — no EnvoyFilter needed.
