Security Q160 of 200
How do you debug a slow request in the anihpj job board using Istio's observability tools?
A) Check kubectl top pods — the highest CPU pod is causing the slowdown
B) Step 1: Check Grafana for RED metrics — is the API latency p99 elevated? Step 2: Open Kiali, click anihpj-api, see if error rate is also elevated. Step 3: Click on a slow edge in Kiali → "View Traces" → opens Jaeger with recent traces for that service. Step 4: In Jaeger, find a trace with high duration and drill into spans — see exactly which service (web, api, db) spent the most time. Step 5: Check Envoy access logs for the slow request to see exact timing and response codes. This systematic approach pinpoints the bottleneck without guessing
C) Restart all pods — if it's still slow, scale up the Deployment
D) Slow requests require application-level profiling — Istio observability tools cannot help
✅ Correct Answer: B

The systematic debugging flow: Grafana (is it slow?) → Kiali (which service?) → Jaeger (which hop?) → Access logs (exact timing). Each tool answers one question, together they pinpoint the bottleneck in minutes instead of hours of guesswork.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Slow Request Debug — Systematic Observability Flow
  USER REPORTS: "Job search is slow, taking 5+ seconds"
  
  ① GRAFANA: Check RED dashboard for anihpj-api
     → p99 latency spiked from 200ms to 3000ms at 10:05 AM ⚠️
  
  ② KIALI: Service graph shows anihpj-api edge is ORANGE (degraded)
     → Click service → Inbound metrics confirm latency spike
  
  ③ KIALI → JAEGER: "View Traces" for anihpj-api
     → Trace: web(50ms) → api(2800ms) → db(10ms)
     → Bottleneck: anihpj-api (2800ms!), not database ✅
  
  ④ ACCESS LOGS: filter for requests with duration > 2000ms
     → All slow requests are POST /api/jobs/search with query=...
     → Complex search query causing slowdown
  
  ⑤ FIX: Optimize search query, add caching, or scale API pods

For anihpj/jobpost, this systematic debugging flow turns "the site is slow" from a vague complaint into a precise diagnosis: Grafana confirms the problem, Kiali localizes it to the API service, Jaeger shows the API is the bottleneck (not the database), and access logs reveal it's the search query. Fix the right thing — no guessing, no restarting random pods.

🔍 Category 5: Troubleshooting — Control Plane & Configuration (10%)

Official CNCF sub-topics: Troubleshooting Configuration, Troubleshooting the Mesh Control Plane. Q161-Q180.

🔍 Category 5: TS — Control Plane & Configuration (10%)Q161-Q180

🟡 Diagnosing istiod, Configuration Issues & Control Plane Recovery

This category covers troubleshooting the brain of the mesh: istiod debugging, configuration analysis, certificate issues, and control plane recovery. When the control plane is unhealthy, new sidecars cannot be injected, configuration changes stop propagating, and the mesh gradually degrades.

5.1 istiod Debugging & Configuration ValidationQ161-Q170

TS-CtrlPlane Q161 of 200
How do you diagnose an unhealthy istiod — what logs, endpoints, and commands reveal control plane issues?
A) Check istiod pod status — if Running, the control plane is healthy
B) Only istiod logs are useful — no other diagnostics are available
C) Multi-layer diagnostics: 1) kubectl logs -n istio-system deploy/istiod --tail=200 for error messages. 2) istioctl proxy-status to verify Envoy sync health. 3) kubectl describe pod -n istio-system istiod-xxx for resource issues. 4) istioctl analyze --all-namespaces for config errors that may be crashing istiod. 5) Check kubectl get cm istio -n istio-system -o yaml for MeshConfig corruption
D) Unhealthy istiod always means a bug — reinstall Istio to fix it
✅ Correct Answer: C

istiod debugging requires looking at multiple signals: logs (what is istiod complaining about?), proxy-status (are Envoys getting config?), analyze (is bad config crashing it?), and MeshConfig (is the ConfigMap corrupted?). No single check is sufficient.

📝 Detailed Explanation
istiod Health Check — Multi-Layer Diagnostic Sequence
  DIAGNOSTIC SEQUENCE:
  ① kubectl get pods -n istio-system → istiod Running? Ready 1/1?
  ② kubectl logs -n istio-system deploy/istiod --tail=200 | grep -E "error|fatal|panic"
  ③ istioctl proxy-status → All SYNCED? Any STALE or NOT SENT?
  ④ istioctl analyze --all-namespaces → Configuration errors?
  ⑤ kubectl describe pod -n istio-system istiod-xxx → OOMKilled? CrashLoop?
  ⑥ kubectl get cm istio -n istio-system -o yaml → Valid YAML?
  
  HEALTHY: ① Running ② No errors ③ All SYNCED ④ No config errors ⑤ No OOM

For anihpj/jobpost, run this diagnostic sequence whenever the mesh behaves unexpectedly. Start at step ① and work down — most issues are caught by step ③ (STALE proxies) or step ④ (bad config). If all checks pass and the mesh is still broken, the issue is likely in the data plane, not the control plane.

TS-CtrlPlane Q162 of 200
What does istioctl proxy-status show and how do you interpret STALE vs NOT SENT sync states?
A) SYNCED: Envoy has the latest config and acknowledged it — healthy. STALE: Envoy hasn't acknowledged recent config updates — possible network issue between istiod and Envoy, or Envoy is overloaded. NOT SENT: istiod hasn't pushed config to this Envoy yet — proxy just started, istiod is overloaded, or there's a connection failure. STALE/NOT SENT for > 30s indicates a control plane communication problem
B) STALE means the Envoy is running an old Istio version — upgrade the sidecar to fix it
C) NOT SENT means the Envoy has been deliberately excluded from the mesh — it's intentional
D) proxy-status only shows ingress gateways — sidecar proxies are not tracked
✅ Correct Answer: A

proxy-status is the mesh health dashboard. SYNCED = everything working. STALE = Envoy not responding to config pushes (network/load issue). NOT SENT = istiod can't reach the Envoy. All should be SYNCED within 30s of pod startup.

📝 Detailed Explanation
proxy-status — Reading the Mesh Health Dashboard
  $ istioctl proxy-status
  NAME                          CDS     LDS     EDS     RDS     ISTIOD
  anihpj-web-xxx.anihpj         SYNCED  SYNCED  SYNCED  SYNCED  istiod-1
  anihpj-api-yyy.anihpj         SYNCED  SYNCED  SYNCED  SYNCED  istiod-1
  anihpj-db-0.anihpj            STALE   STALE   STALE   STALE   istiod-1
  
  STALE on anihpj-db → Network issue between istiod and db sidecar!
  → Check: db pod network connectivity, Envoy resource limits, istiod load
  
  NOT SENT would mean: Envoy never connected → check envoy logs for errors

For anihpj/jobpost, run istioctl proxy-status as a health check. If any anihpj pod shows STALE or NOT SENT for more than 30 seconds, investigate immediately — that pod's Envoy is not receiving configuration updates, meaning routing changes and security policies are not being enforced for that workload.

TS-CtrlPlane Q163 of 200
How do you troubleshoot sidecar injection failures — new pods are not getting the istio-proxy container?
A) Injection failure always means the namespace label is missing — just add istio-injection=enabled
B) Restart istiod — injection failures are always caused by a stale control plane
C) Check the pod events — they explicitly state why injection was skipped
D) Systematic checklist: 1) Namespace labeled istio-injection=enabled? 2) Pod has annotation sidecar.istio.io/inject: "false" (overrides namespace)? 3) kubectl get mutatingwebhookconfiguration istio-sidecar-injector — does the webhook exist? 4) kubectl logs -n istio-system deploy/istiod | grep inject — is istiod receiving injection requests? 5) kubectl get cm istio-sidecar-injector -n istio-system — is the injection template valid? 6) PSA Restricted policy blocking NET_ADMIN? Use istio-cni instead
✅ Correct Answer: D

Injection failures have multiple causes. The namespace label is the most common, but webhook issues, PSA policies, and pod-level annotations can all block injection. Work through the checklist systematically — don't assume it's just the label.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Sidecar Injection Failure — Diagnostic Checklist
  WHY ISN'T MY POD GETTING A SIDECAR?
  
  ① kubectl get ns anihpj --show-labels | grep istio-injection
     → MUST show "istio-injection=enabled"
  
  ② kubectl get pod xxx -n anihpj -o yaml | grep sidecar.istio.io/inject
     → "false" = injection blocked (pod-level override)
  
  ③ kubectl get mutatingwebhookconfiguration istio-sidecar-injector
     → MUST exist (deployed with Istio)
  
  ④ kubectl logs -n istio-system deploy/istiod | grep -i inject
     → "injection request for pod xxx" = istiod received the request
     → "injection skipped: policy" = something blocked it
  
  ⑤ kubectl get cm istio-sidecar-injector -n istio-system -o yaml
     → Valid injection template? Corrupted config?
  
  ⑥ PSA Restricted cluster? → Install istio-cni (no NET_ADMIN needed)

For anihpj/jobpost, if new anihpj-web pods start without sidecars, work through this checklist. The most common issue on a fresh setup is step ① (missing namespace label). On hardened clusters, step ⑥ (PSA Restricted blocking NET_ADMIN) is increasingly common — deploy istio-cni to fix.

TS-CtrlPlane Q164 of 200
What is the xDS protocol and how do you debug Envoy configuration synchronization issues?
A) xDS is a file-based protocol — Envoys read config files from a shared volume
B) xDS (Discovery Service) is the gRPC-based protocol istiod uses to push configuration to Envoys. LDS (Listener), CDS (Cluster), RDS (Route), EDS (Endpoint), SDS (Secret). Debug sync: istioctl proxy-status shows per-xDS sync state. istioctl proxy-config listener|cluster|route|endpoint|secret dumps each config type. If an Envoy shows STALE, check the istiod→Envoy gRPC connection with kubectl logs -n istio-system deploy/istiod | grep "ads"
C) xDS is only used for initial configuration — updates use a different protocol
D) xDS debugging requires a special istioctl plugin — standard commands cannot inspect it
✅ Correct Answer: B

xDS is the nervous system of Istio. istiod pushes config changes to every Envoy via gRPC streams — one stream per Envoy, carrying all five xDS types. When proxy-status shows STALE, the gRPC stream is broken — Envoy isn't receiving updates.

📝 Detailed Explanation
xDS Protocol — How istiod Programs Every Envoy
  istiod ◄════ gRPC bidirectional streams ════► Envoy (every sidecar)
  
  xDS TYPES:
  LDS (Listener DS)  → What ports/protocols does Envoy listen on?
  CDS (Cluster DS)   → What are the upstream clusters (services)?
  RDS (Route DS)     → How are requests routed to clusters?
  EDS (Endpoint DS)  → What are the IP addresses of upstream pods?
  SDS (Secret DS)    → What certificates does Envoy use?
  
  DEBUG:
  istioctl proxy-status → per-type sync state
  istioctl proxy-config cluster  → full cluster config dump
  kubectl logs -n istio-system deploy/istiod | grep "ads" → gRPC events

For anihpj/jobpost, if you apply a VirtualService but routing doesn't change, check istioctl proxy-status — if RDS shows STALE, the routing config isn't reaching the Envoy. Check istiod logs for "ads" errors and verify network connectivity between istiod and the affected pod.

TS-CtrlPlane Q165 of 200
How do you recover from istiod crash — what steps ensure the mesh returns to a healthy state?
A) 1) Verify istiod Deployment is running: kubectl get pods -n istio-system. 2) Check logs for crash cause: kubectl logs -n istio-system deploy/istiod --previous (if CrashLoop). 3) If OOMKilled, increase memory limits in IstioOperator. 4) If config corruption, restore MeshConfig from backup or edit ConfigMap. 5) After istiod recovers, run istioctl proxy-status — Envoys will reconnect automatically within 30s. 6) If Envoys don't reconnect, restart affected pods. 7) Verify new pods get sidecars by deploying a test pod. Existing traffic was unaffected — Envoys use cached config
B) Delete and recreate the entire mesh — istiod crashes are unrecoverable
C) Just restart the istiod pod — no further steps are needed
D) Cordon all nodes to stop traffic, then restart istiod, then uncordon — downtime is unavoidable
✅ Correct Answer: A

istiod crash recovery is straightforward because the data plane is resilient. Envoys continue serving traffic with cached config. Focus on: why did it crash? (fix root cause), do Envoys reconnect? (monitor proxy-status), can new pods get sidecars? (test injection). Existing traffic is fine — don't panic.

📝 Detailed Explanation
istiod Crash Recovery — Step-by-Step
  INCIDENT: istiod CrashLoopBackOff
  
  WHAT'S AFFECTED:                       WHAT'S FINE:
  ❌ New sidecar injection               ✅ Existing traffic flows normally
  ❌ Config changes not propagated        ✅ mTLS continues (certs cached)
  ❌ New certs not issued                 ✅ Existing certs valid until expiry
  
  RECOVERY:
  kubectl logs -n istio-system deploy/istiod --previous → find root cause
  → OOM? Increase memory
  → Config corruption? Restore ConfigMap
  → Cert issue? Check cacerts Secret
  
  AFTER istiod recovers:
  istioctl proxy-status → Envoys reconnect (within 30s)
  kubectl run test --image=nginx -n test → verify injection works

For anihpj/jobpost, if istiod crashes, the job board keeps running — Envoys have cached configuration. Focus on fixing the root cause and verifying recovery. Deploy a test pod to confirm injection works before declaring the incident resolved.

TS-CtrlPlane Q166 of 200
How do you validate MeshConfig — what command checks if the istio ConfigMap is valid and properly formatted?
A) MeshConfig is always valid — istiod validates it at startup and rejects invalid config
B) Check with kubectl describe configmap — it shows validation status
C) istioctl analyze catches MeshConfig issues. Also: kubectl get cm istio -n istio-system -o yaml | istioctl experimental config validate (if available). Check istiod logs for MeshConfig parsing errors: kubectl logs -n istio-system deploy/istiod | grep -i "meshconfig". If istiod won't start, validate the YAML syntax: kubectl get cm istio -n istio-system -o jsonpath='{.data.mesh}' | python3 -c "import sys,yaml; yaml.safe_load(sys.stdin)"
D) MeshConfig validation is only available in the paid Istio support portal
✅ Correct Answer: C

MeshConfig corruption is a common cause of istiod startup failure. Someone edits the ConfigMap, makes a YAML typo, and istiod crashes. Always validate the YAML before applying changes, and keep a backup of the working configuration.

📝 Detailed Explanation
MeshConfig Validation — Prevent ConfigMap Corruption
  CHECK MESHCONFIG VALIDITY:
  ① kubectl get cm istio -n istio-system -o yaml
     → Look at mesh: section for obvious YAML errors
  
  ② istioctl analyze -n istio-system
     → Catches semantic issues (wrong field names, invalid values)
  
  ③ CHECK ISTIOD LOGS FOR PARSING ERRORS:
     kubectl logs -n istio-system deploy/istiod | grep -i "meshconfig\|parse\|yaml"
  
  ④ BEFORE EDITING: BACKUP
     kubectl get cm istio -n istio-system -o yaml > istio-cm-backup.yaml
     → If istiod crashes after your edit, restore from backup

For anihpj/jobpost, always backup the istio ConfigMap before making changes. A single YAML indentation error in MeshConfig can crash istiod. Validate with istioctl analyze after every change, and keep the backup YAML ready for instant restoration.

TS-CtrlPlane Q167 of 200
What causes certificate chain issues in Istio and how do you diagnose them?
A) Certificate issues are always caused by expired certs — just check the expiry date
B) Certificate issues only happen with custom CAs — Istio's self-signed CA never has problems
C) Restart istiod — certificate issues are always transient and resolve on restart
D) Common causes: Expired root cert (check with openssl), missing cacerts Secret (custom CA was deleted), clock skew between nodes (cert appears expired), wrong trust domain (multi-cluster mismatch). Diagnose: istioctl proxy-config secret <pod> | openssl x509 -noout -dates for expiry, kubectl get secret cacerts -n istio-system for custom CA, kubectl logs istiod | grep -i "cert\|tls\|x509" for errors
✅ Correct Answer: D

Certificate issues have multiple root causes. Clock skew between nodes is sneaky — certs appear expired even though they were just issued. Multi-cluster setups with mismatched trust domains cause silent mTLS failures. Always check all four: expiry, CA Secret, clock sync, trust domain.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Certificate Chain Issues — Diagnostic Flow
  SYMPTOM: mTLS handshake failures, "certificate verify failed" in logs
  
  DIAGNOSTIC STEPS:
  ① CHECK EXPIRY:
     istioctl proxy-config secret  -n anihpj -o json | \
       jq -r '..|.inlineBytes?|select(.)' | base64 -d | \
       openssl x509 -noout -dates
     → Expired? Cert rotation broken (istiod issue)
  
  ② CHECK CUSTOM CA:
     kubectl get secret cacerts -n istio-system
     → Missing? istiod reverted to self-signed, old certs invalid
  
  ③ CHECK CLOCK SKEW:
     kubectl exec  -n anihpj -- date
     → Different from node time? NTP sync issue
  
  ④ CHECK TRUST DOMAIN:
     kubectl get cm istio -n istio-system -o yaml | grep trustDomain
     → Mismatched between clusters? Cross-cluster mTLS fails

For anihpj/jobpost, run a daily cron job that checks certificate expiry across all mesh pods. Alert if any cert expires within 12 hours (rotation should happen at 50% lifetime). For multi-cluster setups, ensure all clusters share the same trust domain or have cross-trust configured.

TS-CtrlPlane Q168 of 200
How do you troubleshoot a failed Istio upgrade — what checks confirm the upgrade succeeded or diagnose why it failed?
A) If istiod is Running after upgrade, the upgrade succeeded — no further checks needed
B) Pre-upgrade: istioctl x precheck validates cluster readiness. During upgrade: watch kubectl get pods -n istio-system -w. Post-upgrade: 1) istioctl verify-install checks all components. 2) istioctl proxy-status verifies Envoys sync with new istiod. 3) Deploy test pod to verify injection. 4) Check kubectl logs -n istio-system deploy/istiod for errors. 5) Run istioctl analyze for deprecated API usage. If failed: istioctl istio-operator rollback or revert namespace labels to old revision
C) Upgrade failure means reinstall Istio from scratch — no rollback is possible
D) The upgrade succeeded if kubectl get pods shows all Running — no istioctl verification needed
✅ Correct Answer: B

Upgrade verification is a three-phase process: pre-upgrade validation, during-upgrade monitoring, post-upgrade verification. Never assume an upgrade succeeded just because pods are Running — verify injection, proxy sync, and configuration compatibility.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Istio Upgrade — Verification Checklist
  PRE-UPGRADE:
  $ istioctl x precheck        ← K8s version, CRD compatibility
  $ kubectl get crds | grep istio  ← All CRDs present
  
  DURING UPGRADE:
  $ kubectl get pods -n istio-system -w  ← Watch istiod restart
  
  POST-UPGRADE:
  ☑ istioctl verify-install    ← All components healthy
  ☑ istioctl proxy-status      ← All Envoys SYNCED with new ISTIOD version
  ☑ kubectl run test --image=nginx -n test  ← Injection works
  ☑ kubectl logs -n istio-system deploy/istiod | grep -i error
  ☑ istioctl analyze --all-namespaces  ← No deprecated APIs
  
  ROLLBACK (canary upgrade):
  kubectl label ns anihpj istio.io/rev=stable --overwrite
  kubectl rollout restart deploy -n anihpj

For anihpj/jobpost, always perform canary upgrades: deploy the new revision alongside the old, migrate a test namespace first, verify all post-upgrade checks, then migrate the anihpj namespace. If any check fails, instantly rollback by switching the namespace label back — zero downtime.

TS-CtrlPlane Q169 of 200
How do you detect configuration drift — ensuring the running mesh config matches what's in your Git repository?
A) Export current mesh config: istioctl manifest generate or dump all CRDs with kubectl get vs,dr,gateway,se,pa,ra --all-namespaces -o yaml. Compare against Git: diff <(kubectl get vs,dr -n anihpj -o yaml) <(cat git/istio/). Use istioctl analyze to detect unknown or misconfigured resources. For continuous drift detection, use tools like Argo CD with auto-sync or a cron job that diffs live config against Git
B) Configuration drift is detected automatically by istiod — it reports to Git
C) Use kubectl describe on each resource — it shows when the resource was last modified
D) Drift detection requires a commercial Istio addon — not available in the open-source version
✅ Correct Answer: A

Configuration drift is a real risk — someone runs kubectl edit on a VirtualService, changes a weight, and breaks the canary. GitOps with Argo CD auto-sync prevents drift. For ad-hoc detection, diff live config against your Git repository.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Configuration Drift Detection — Git vs Live Cluster
  DETECT DRIFT:
  kubectl get vs,dr,gateway,se -n anihpj -o yaml > live-config.yaml
  diff live-config.yaml git/istio/production/
  
  → Differences found? Someone changed config outside of Git!
  
  PREVENT DRIFT (GitOps):
  Argo CD Application → watches git/istio/ → auto-syncs to cluster
  → Any manual kubectl edit is reverted within 3 minutes
  
  CI/CD DRIFT CHECK:
  istioctl analyze -n anihpj  ← Catch bad config before it drifts
  diff <(kubectl get vs -n anihpj -o yaml) <(cat istio/virtualservice.yaml)
  → Non-zero exit = drift detected → CI fails

For anihpj/jobpost, manage all Istio configuration in Git and deploy via Argo CD with auto-sync enabled. Run a daily cron job that diffs live config against Git and alerts on drift. This ensures the job board mesh configuration is always version-controlled and auditable.

TS-CtrlPlane Q170 of 200
What is the istiod webhook and how do you troubleshoot issues with sidecar injection, validation, or configuration?
A) The webhook is a Kubernetes feature — Istio doesn't use webhooks
B) Webhook issues always resolve by restarting the API server — no Istio-specific troubleshooting
C) Istio deploys multiple webhooks: istio-sidecar-injector (injects Envoy into pods), istio-validator (validates CRDs), and optionally others. Troubleshoot: kubectl get mutatingwebhookconfiguration,validatingwebhookconfiguration | grep istio — do they exist? Check kubectl logs -n istio-system deploy/istiod | grep webhook for errors. If the API server can't reach istiod (network issue), all webhook calls timeout and pod creation fails with "internal error"
D) Webhooks are optional — Istio works without them
✅ Correct Answer: C

Istio webhooks are critical infrastructure. If the API server can't reach istiod, the sidecar injector webhook can't modify pod specs, and the validator webhook can't validate CRDs. This causes pod creation failures and configuration rejections — the mesh becomes non-functional.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Istio Webhooks — Critical Infrastructure Check
  CHECK WEBHOOKS:
  kubectl get mutatingwebhookconfiguration | grep istio
  → istio-sidecar-injector        ← Pod injection
  kubectl get validatingwebhookconfiguration | grep istio
  → istio-validator               ← CRD validation
  
  WEBHOOK FAILURE SYMPTOMS:
  • kubectl apply -f pod.yaml → timeout / "internal error"
  • kubectl apply -f virtualservice.yaml → rejected even though valid YAML
  • New pods stuck in "ContainerCreating" with no events
  
  ROOT CAUSE: API server cannot reach istiod (network, DNS, firewall)
  FIX: Verify istiod Service exists, network policy allows API server → istiod:15017

For anihpj/jobpost, if pod creation suddenly starts timing out, check the webhook configurations first. The API server must be able to reach istiod on port 15017 for webhook calls. Network policies or firewall rules blocking this path will break all sidecar injection and CRD validation.

5.2 Configuration Troubleshooting & RecoveryQ171-Q180

TS-CtrlPlane Q171 of 200
How do you identify which VirtualService or DestinationRule is causing routing issues using diagnostic tools?
A) Delete all Istio CRDs and recreate them one by one — that's the only way to isolate the problem
B) 1) istioctl analyze -n <ns> flags configuration errors. 2) istioctl proxy-config routes <pod> -n <ns> -o json shows what routes Envoy ACTUALLY has loaded — compare against your YAML. 3) kubectl get vs,dr -n <ns> lists all routing config. 4) Kiali's Config Validation page highlights broken resources. 5) Check istioctl proxy-config cluster <pod> --fqdn <svc> to see if DestinationRule subsets have endpoints
C) Routing issues always show in kubectl describe pod events — no istioctl tools needed
D) Only Kiali can identify routing issues — CLI tools cannot inspect Envoy routes
✅ Correct Answer: B

The golden rule of routing debugging: compare what you WROTE (YAML) against what Envoy HAS (proxy-config dump). If they differ, the config isn't being applied correctly. istioctl proxy-config routes shows the ground truth — what Envoy actually believes.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Routing Issue Diagnosis — YAML vs Envoy Reality
  YOU WROTE (VirtualService):           ENVOY HAS (proxy-config):
  hosts: [anihpj-api]                   $ istioctl proxy-config routes anihpj-web \
  http:                                   -n anihpj -o json | jq '.[].name'
  - match: [{uri: {prefix: /api/v2}}]  
    route: [{subset: v2, weight: 10}]   → Route for /api/v2 found?
  - route: [{subset: v1, weight: 90}]   → Weight 90:10 visible?
                                        → If NOT: config didn't propagate
  
  DEBUG SEQUENCE:
  ① istioctl analyze -n anihpj
  ② istioctl proxy-config routes  -n anihpj
  ③ kubectl get vs,dr -n anihpj
  ④ Kiali → Config → Validation
  ⑤ istioctl proxy-config cluster  --fqdn anihpj-api

For anihpj/jobpost, when a canary isn't routing correctly, compare your VirtualService YAML against istioctl proxy-config routes output. If the route exists in YAML but not in Envoy, the config hasn't propagated — check istioctl proxy-status for RDS sync state.

TS-CtrlPlane Q172 of 200
A VirtualService was applied but traffic routing hasn't changed. istioctl proxy-status shows RDS: STALE for the affected pods. What does this mean and how do you fix it?
A) STALE is normal for the first 5 minutes after applying config — wait and it will sync
B) Delete and recreate the VirtualService — STALE means the CRD is corrupted
C) STALE means the VirtualService YAML has a syntax error — kubectl accepted it but Envoy rejected it
D) RDS: STALE means the Route Discovery Service gRPC stream between istiod and the Envoy sidecar is broken — configuration updates are not reaching that Envoy. Fix: 1) Check network connectivity between istiod and the pod. 2) Check Envoy resource limits — an overloaded Envoy may drop the gRPC stream. 3) Restart the affected pod to re-establish the gRPC connection. 4) Check istiod logs for ADS errors
✅ Correct Answer: D

RDS: STALE is a broken gRPC stream between istiod and Envoy. The VirtualService is valid YAML and kubectl accepted it, but the route update never reached the Envoy. This is a data plane communication issue, not a configuration issue — the control plane has the update, but can't deliver it.

📝 Detailed Explanation
RDS: STALE — Broken Route Sync Stream
  NORMAL:                                  STALE:
  istiod ──RDS update──► Envoy ✅          istiod ──RDS update──► Envoy ❌
  (gRPC stream active)                     (gRPC stream broken)
  
  FIX:
  ① Check Envoy resource usage (OOM? CPU starved?)
  ② kubectl logs  -c istio-proxy | grep "gRPC\|stream\|reset"
  ③ Restart pod to re-establish stream
  ④ If persistent: check network policies, increase Envoy resources

For anihpj/jobpost, if a VirtualService update doesn't take effect and proxy-status shows STALE on RDS, restart the affected pods. The new pods will establish fresh gRPC streams and receive the latest configuration. For persistent STALE issues, increase Envoy proxy resource limits — an overloaded Envoy drops gRPC streams.

TS-CtrlPlane Q173 of 200
How do you troubleshoot when istioctl commands hang or timeout — what could prevent istioctl from connecting to the cluster or istiod?
A) istioctl hanging always means the Kubernetes API server is down
B) Use --timeout flag to fix hanging — it's a client-side configuration issue
C) Common causes: 1) Kubeconfig pointing to wrong cluster or context. 2) Network — istioctl connects to the K8s API server AND does port-forwarding to istiod for some commands. 3) istiod not running — commands like proxy-config require istiod. 4) Firewall/Proxy blocking gRPC connections. 5) istiod overloaded — too many proxies causing slow responses. Fix: verify kubectl cluster-info, check kubectl -n istio-system get pods, test with --context flag
D) Restart your terminal — istioctl hanging is always a client-side shell issue
✅ Correct Answer: C

istioctl connects to both the K8s API server (for CRD operations) and directly to istiod (for proxy-config, proxy-status). If either is unreachable, commands hang. Always verify kubectl works first — if kubectl is fine but istioctl hangs, the issue is likely istiod connectivity.

📝 Detailed Explanation
istioctl Connectivity — Two Endpoints
  istioctl ──► Kubernetes API Server  ← kubectl commands (CRDs, pods)
  istioctl ──► istiod (port-forward) ← proxy-config, proxy-status
  
  DEBUG:
  kubectl cluster-info          ← API server reachable?
  kubectl -n istio-system port-forward svc/istiod 15012:15012
                                ← istiod reachable?
  ksw → check context           ← Right cluster?

For anihpj/jobpost, if istioctl hangs, first verify kubectl get pods -n istio-system works. If kubectl works but istioctl doesn't, the issue is istiod connectivity — check that istiod pods are Running and the istiod Service exists in istio-system.

TS-CtrlPlane Q174 of 200
What does istioctl verify-install do and when should you run it?
A) istioctl verify-install compares the running cluster state against the Istio installation manifest. It checks: istiod Deployment (replicas, image version), Services, ConfigMaps, CRDs, MutatingWebhookConfiguration, and RBAC resources. Run it after installation, after upgrades, and as a health check to verify all Istio components are present and correctly configured. Non-zero exit means components are missing or misconfigured
B) verify-install is the same as kubectl get pods -n istio-system — it just checks pod status
C) verify-install only works immediately after installation — it cannot verify a running mesh
D) verify-install is deprecated in favor of istioctl analyze — they serve the same purpose
✅ Correct Answer: A

istioctl verify-install is the comprehensive "is Istio installed correctly?" check. It compares desired state (manifest) against actual state (cluster) and reports any discrepancies. Run after every install and upgrade as a smoke test.

📝 Detailed Explanation
verify-install — Installation Health Check
  $ istioctl verify-install
  
  ✅ Istiod Deployment: 2/2 replicas available (v1.22.1)
  ✅ Istiod Service: ClusterIP 10.96.0.10:15012
  ✅ Ingress Gateway Deployment: 1/1 replicas
  ✅ CRDs: 54 custom resource definitions installed
  ✅ MutatingWebhookConfiguration: istio-sidecar-injector
  ✅ ValidatingWebhookConfiguration: istio-validator
  ✅ ConfigMaps: istio, istio-sidecar-injector
  ✅ RBAC: ClusterRole, ClusterRoleBinding for istiod
  
  All components verified successfully.

For anihpj/jobpost, run istioctl verify-install after installing or upgrading Istio on any of your 6 clusters. It catches missing components, wrong versions, and incomplete installations before they cause mesh issues.

TS-CtrlPlane Q175 of 200
How do you fix a broken PeerAuthentication that is preventing all traffic in a namespace after switching to STRICT mTLS?
A) Delete the namespace — PeerAuthentication policies cannot be reverted once applied
B) 1) Immediately switch back to PERMISSIVE: kubectl apply -f peerauthentication-permissive.yaml. 2) Identify which pods lack sidecars: kubectl get pods -n <ns> -o jsonpath='{range .items[*]}{.metadata.name}{"\t"}{.spec.containers[*].name}{"\n"}{end}' | grep -v istio-proxy. 3) Inject sidecars into those pods or add a workload-level DISABLE policy for pods that cannot support mTLS. 4) Use portLevelMtls for granular control if only specific ports need plaintext. 5) Once all pods have sidecars, re-apply STRICT
C) Restart all pods — they will automatically pick up the correct mTLS configuration
D) Switch to DISABLE — STRICT mTLS is not intended for production use
✅ Correct Answer: B

STRICT mTLS breaking traffic is an emergency. Rollback to PERMISSIVE immediately — apply the old policy, and traffic resumes within seconds. Then audit which pods lack sidecars, fix the gaps, and re-attempt STRICT migration when ready.

📝 Detailed Explanation
STRICT mTLS Emergency Rollback
  EMERGENCY: Traffic stopped after switching to STRICT
  
  ① ROLLBACK (immediate — STOPS the bleeding):
     kubectl apply -f - <

For anihpj/jobpost, always keep the PERMISSIVE PeerAuthentication YAML ready as an emergency rollback. Switching from STRICT to PERMISSIVE takes effect immediately — no pod restarts needed. Practice this rollback in staging so it's muscle memory for production incidents.

TS-CtrlPlane Q176 of 200
What is istioctl bug-report and what does it capture for control plane troubleshooting?
A) bug-report only captures pod logs — it's the same as kubectl logs --all-namespaces
B) bug-report automatically fixes common bugs — it's a repair tool, not a diagnostic tool
C) bug-report is only for submitting GitHub issues — it's not useful for self-diagnosis
D) istioctl bug-report captures a comprehensive diagnostic snapshot: istiod logs (all replicas), proxy-status output, Envoy config dumps from selected pods, all Istio CRDs, MeshConfig, cluster info (nodes, services, endpoints), and webhook configurations. The output is a .tar.gz archive that can be shared with support or analyzed locally. It's the single most complete diagnostic tool for mesh issues
✅ Correct Answer: D

When you can't figure out why the mesh is broken, run istioctl bug-report. It captures everything in one archive. Share it with your team, attach it to support tickets, or analyze it yourself offline. The single most useful command for complex mesh issues.

📝 Detailed Explanation
istioctl bug-report — Complete Diagnostic Snapshot
  $ istioctl bug-report
  
  OUTPUT: bug-report.tar.gz containing:
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  istiod-logs/           ← All istiod replicas (current + prev)  │
  │  proxy-status.txt       ← Sync state of all Envoys              │
  │  envoy-configs/         ← Config dumps from selected proxies    │
  │  crds/                  ← All Istio CRDs in the cluster         │
  │  mesh-config.yaml       ← The istio ConfigMap                   │
  │  cluster-info/          ← Nodes, pods, services, endpoints      │
  │  webhooks/              ← Mutating/Validating webhook configs   │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
  
  USE FOR:
  • Complex issues you can't diagnose
  • Sharing with Istio community/support
  • Capturing state before destructive changes

For anihpj/jobpost, run istioctl bug-report before making any major mesh configuration changes. It captures a baseline snapshot that you can compare against if something goes wrong. Also run it when escalating issues — the support team needs this data to help.

TS-CtrlPlane Q177 of 200
How do you troubleshoot when new CRDs cannot be created — kubectl apply fails with "resource mapping not found"?
A) The Kubernetes cluster is broken — reinstall Kubernetes
B) The error means your YAML has a syntax error — validate the YAML formatting
C) "Resource mapping not found" means the CRD for that Istio resource is not installed. Check: kubectl get crds | grep istio — are all Istio CRDs present? If missing, reinstall Istio CRDs: istioctl install --set profile=default --skip-confirmation or apply the CRD YAML directly. This commonly happens after istioctl uninstall removed CRDs but you're trying to apply mesh config
D) This error means kubectl is outdated — upgrade to the latest kubectl version
✅ Correct Answer: C

Missing CRDs = Istio isn't fully installed. The control plane might be running, but if the CRDs were removed (by uninstall or accident), kubectl can't recognize VirtualService, DestinationRule, etc. Reinstall the CRDs from the Istio release or run istioctl install again.

📝 Detailed Explanation
CRD Check — Are Istio Resource Types Installed?
  $ kubectl get crds | grep istio

  virtualservices.networking.istio.io
  destinationrules.networking.istio.io
