  envoyfilters.networking.istio.io
  ... (and many more)
  
  MISSING? → Reinstall:
  istioctl install --set profile=default

For anihpj/jobpost, if you ever run istioctl uninstall --purge, it removes ALL CRDs. After reinstalling Istio, verify CRDs are back with kubectl get crds | grep istio before applying any mesh configuration.

TS-CtrlPlane Q178 of 200
After an Istio upgrade, some VirtualServices fail validation with "apiVersion not served." What is the likely cause and fix?
A) The upgrade corrupted the VirtualService — delete and recreate all of them
B) The VirtualServices use a deprecated API version (e.g., networking.istio.io/v1alpha3) that was removed in the new Istio version. Fix: update all VirtualServices and other CRDs to the current API version (networking.istio.io/v1beta1 or v1). Check istioctl analyze --all-namespaces which detects deprecated API usage. Also: kubectl get vs,dr,gateway --all-namespaces -o yaml | grep "apiVersion:" | sort | uniq -c to find deprecated versions
C) This error means the Kubernetes cluster version is too old for the new Istio
D) apiVersion errors are cosmetic — the VirtualService still works despite the warning
✅ Correct Answer: B

Deprecated API versions are removed in new Istio releases. v1alpha3 was removed in Istio 1.19+ in favor of v1beta1. Always update CRD apiVersions before upgrading. istioctl analyze catches deprecated usage before the upgrade breaks things.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Deprecated API Version Fix — Pre-Upgrade Audit
  FIND DEPRECATED APIS:
  for crd in vs dr gateway se pa ra ap; do
    kubectl get $crd --all-namespaces -o yaml | grep "apiVersion:" | sort | uniq -c
  done
  
  FIX (update to current version):
  sed -i 's|networking.istio.io/v1alpha3|networking.istio.io/v1beta1|g' *.yaml
  sed -i 's|security.istio.io/v1beta1|security.istio.io/v1|g' *.yaml
  
  PRE-UPGRADE CHECK:
  istioctl analyze --all-namespaces
  → Flags deprecated API usage BEFORE the upgrade

For anihpj/jobpost, audit all Istio CRDs with istioctl analyze --all-namespaces before every Istio upgrade. Update deprecated apiVersions to the current version. This prevents "apiVersion not served" errors that would block mesh configuration after the upgrade.

TS-CtrlPlane Q179 of 200
How do you monitor istiod health using Prometheus metrics to detect issues before they impact the mesh?
A) istiod exposes Prometheus metrics on port 15014. Key metrics: pilot_conflict_inbound_listener (listener conflicts), pilot_xds_push_time (config push latency), pilot_xds_pushes (push rate), pilot_proxy_convergence_time (time for all proxies to sync), pilot_total_xds_rejects (rejected config pushes). Alert on: high push latency, increasing rejects, long convergence time. These metrics reveal control plane issues before they cause data plane problems
B) istiod metrics are only available in the Enterprise edition
C) Monitor istiod CPU and memory only — Prometheus metrics are not useful for control plane health
D) istiod health must be monitored through external synthetic tests — no internal metrics are available
✅ Correct Answer: A

istiod metrics provide deep insight into control plane health. High push latency means Envoys are receiving config slowly. High rejection rate means bad config is being applied. Long convergence time means the mesh is taking too long to reach a consistent state. Monitor these proactively.

📝 Detailed Explanation
istiod Health Metrics — Proactive Monitoring
  KEY ISTIOD METRICS (Prometheus):
  
  pilot_xds_push_time          ← P99 push latency > 5s? istiod overloaded
  pilot_proxy_convergence_time ← Time for all Envoys to sync > 30s? issue
  pilot_total_xds_rejects      ← Rejected config pushes > 0? bad config applied
  pilot_conflict_inbound_listener ← Listener conflicts > 0? port collision
  
  ALERT RULES:
  - alert: IstiodSlowPushes
    expr: histogram_quantile(0.99, rate(pilot_xds_push_time_bucket[5m])) > 5
    for: 5m
  - alert: IstiodConfigRejects
    expr: rate(pilot_total_xds_rejects[5m]) > 0

For anihpj/jobpost, add these istiod metrics to your Grafana dashboards and configure alerts. High push latency or config rejects indicate control plane issues that will eventually cause data plane problems — catch them proactively before the job board is affected.

TS-CtrlPlane Q180 of 200
You applied a new VirtualService but Kiali shows no change in traffic flow. What systematic approach diagnoses why the configuration isn't taking effect?
A) Delete the VirtualService and recreate it — sometimes kubectl apply doesn't register the change
B) The issue is always a stale Kiali cache — refresh the Kiali dashboard
C) Wait 5 minutes — VirtualService changes always take that long to propagate
D) Systematic diagnosis: 1) kubectl get vs <name> -n <ns> -o yaml — is the YAML what you intended? 2) istioctl analyze -n <ns> — any config errors? 3) istioctl proxy-status — are Envoys SYNCED on RDS? 4) istioctl proxy-config routes <pod> -n <ns> -o json — does Envoy have the new route? 5) Send test traffic and check Envoy access logs — is traffic hitting the expected route? 6) If config is correct and Envoys are SYNCED but traffic doesn't match, the issue is likely a host name mismatch in the VirtualService
✅ Correct Answer: D

When config doesn't take effect, work top-down: verify the YAML is correct, verify istioctl sees no errors, verify Envoys received it, verify test traffic matches. The most common cause is a host name mismatch — the VirtualService references "anihpj-api-v1" but the K8s Service is "anihpj-api".

📝 Detailed Explanation
Config Not Taking Effect — Systematic Diagnosis
  YOU: kubectl apply -f virtualservice-canary.yaml
  KIALI: No change in traffic flow 🤔
  
  ① kubectl get vs anihpj-api-vs -n anihpj -o yaml
     → Is the YAML what you intended? Check hosts, weights, subsets
  
  ② istioctl analyze -n anihpj
     → ❌ "host anihpj-api-v1 not found" → FIX THE HOST NAME!
  
  ③ istioctl proxy-status
     → RDS: SYNCED? If STALE, config didn't reach Envoy
  
  ④ istioctl proxy-config routes anihpj-web-xxx -n anihpj -o json
     → Does the route exist in Envoy's config? Compare against your YAML
  
  ⑤ Test traffic: curl -H "Host: anihpj-api" http://web:8000/api/test
     → Check access logs: did it route to v2?

For anihpj/jobpost, this systematic approach catches the #1 config issue: host name mismatch. Your VirtualService says hosts: [anihpj-api-v1] but the K8s Service is anihpj-api. kubectl accepted the YAML, istiod pushed it to Envoys, but Envoy has no routes for the wrong host name — traffic goes to default routing instead.

📡 Category 6: Troubleshooting — Data Plane & Runtime (10%)

Official CNCF sub-topics: Troubleshooting the Mesh Data Plane. Q181-Q200.

📡 Category 6: TS — Data Plane & Runtime (10%)Q181-Q200

🟣 Debugging Envoy Sidecars, Connectivity & Data Plane Issues

This category covers troubleshooting the muscle of the mesh: Envoy sidecar debugging, traffic capture issues, connectivity problems, and runtime performance. When the data plane breaks, services can't communicate — even if the control plane is perfectly healthy.

6.1 Envoy Sidecar Debugging & ConnectivityQ181-Q190

TS-DataPlane Q181 of 200
How do you use envoy config_dump to debug why traffic isn't routing as expected?
A) config_dump shows Envoy version only — it doesn't contain routing information
B) Use kubectl describe on the pod — config_dump is a legacy command no longer supported
C) istioctl proxy-config all <pod> -n <ns> -o json dumps the ENTIRE Envoy configuration: listeners (what ports), clusters (what backends with what endpoints), routes (what matching rules), endpoints (what IPs), and secrets (what certificates). Compare this against your VirtualService/DestinationRule YAML to see EXACTLY what Envoy believes. This is the ultimate debugging tool — if config_dump shows the wrong route, your YAML or propagation is broken
D) config_dump only works on ingress gateways — sidecar proxies don't support it
✅ Correct Answer: C

config_dump is the truth. Whatever your YAML says, whatever Kiali shows — config_dump reveals what Envoy ACTUALLY has loaded. If the route is missing from config_dump, it doesn't matter what your VirtualService says — Envoy isn't using it.

📝 Detailed Explanation
envoy config_dump — The Ultimate Debugging Tool
  DUMP ENTIRE ENVOY CONFIG:
  $ istioctl proxy-config all anihpj-web-xxx -n anihpj -o json > envoy-dump.json
  
  INSPECT SPECIFIC SECTIONS:
  $ istioctl proxy-config routes anihpj-web-xxx -n anihpj --fqdn anihpj-api
  → Shows ALL routes for anihpj-api that Envoy knows about
  
  $ istioctl proxy-config cluster anihpj-web-xxx -n anihpj --fqdn anihpj-api -o json | jq '.[].endpoints[].address'
  → Shows actual pod IPs for each subset
  
  $ istioctl proxy-config listeners anihpj-web-xxx -n anihpj
  → Shows all ports Envoy is listening on
  
  THE GOLDEN RULE: Config_dump = ground truth.
  If it differs from your YAML, the config didn't propagate.

For anihpj/jobpost, when debugging routing issues, ALWAYS run istioctl proxy-config routes against the source pod's Envoy. If the canary route (90% v1, 10% v2) isn't in config_dump, the VirtualService didn't reach the Envoy — check proxy-status for RDS sync issues.

TS-DataPlane Q182 of 200
How do you troubleshoot when Envoy is consuming excessive memory — what commands reveal the cause?
A) High memory usage is normal for Envoy — increase resource limits and ignore it
B) 1) Check istioctl proxy-config clusters <pod> | wc -l — too many clusters? Use Sidecar resource to scope. 2) Check istioctl proxy-config endpoints <pod> | wc -l — too many endpoints? Mesh too large without scoping. 3) Check istioctl proxy-config all <pod> -o json | jq '.configs[].dynamic_active_clusters | length'. 4) Check Envoy stats: istioctl proxy-config stats <pod> | grep "server.memory". 5) Check if mTLS STRICT with large mesh causes certificate overhead. Fix: deploy Sidecar resource to limit Envoy scope, adjust proxy resources, or move to ambient mesh
C) Memory leaks are always a bug — reinstall Istio to get the latest Envoy version
D) Excessive memory means the application is sending too many requests — rate limit the application
✅ Correct Answer: B

Envoy memory is proportional to mesh size. Without Sidecar scoping, an Envoy in a 500-service mesh holds 500 clusters, 5000 endpoints, and 500 listeners — easily 500MB+. The Sidecar resource is the fix: scope to only the services this workload actually talks to.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Envoy Memory — Diagnosis and Fix
  DIAGNOSE:
  $ istioctl proxy-config clusters  -n anihpj | grep "outbound" | wc -l
  → 483 clusters (way too many!)
  
  $ istioctl proxy-config endpoints  -n anihpj | wc -l
  → 2347 endpoints (massive)
  
  FIX — Sidecar Resource (scope Envoy):
  apiVersion: networking.istio.io/v1beta1
  kind: Sidecar
  metadata: {name: anihpj-web-scope, namespace: anihpj}
  spec:
    workloadSelector: {labels: {app: anihpj-web}}
    egress:
    - hosts: [anihpj/*, istio-system/*]
  
  AFTER SCOPING:
  → 12 clusters, 35 endpoints → ~50MB RAM ✅

For anihpj/jobpost, deploy Sidecar resources to scope each workload's Envoy to only the services it needs. The anihpj-web sidecar only needs anihpj-api and istiod — not all 50+ services in the cluster. This reduces memory from ~200MB to ~30MB per pod.

TS-DataPlane Q183 of 200
How do you debug iptables traffic capture — verifying that Envoy is actually intercepting traffic?
A) Exec into the pod and check iptables rules: istioctl proxy-config listeners <pod> shows the virtual listeners that catch redirected traffic. Check actual iptables: kubectl exec <pod> -c istio-proxy -- iptables -t nat -L -v shows redirect rules. Verify traffic capture: check Envoy stats istioctl proxy-config stats <pod> | grep "listener.*downstream_cx_total" — zero connections means traffic isn't reaching Envoy (iptables broken). If stats show connections, Envoy is intercepting
B) iptables rules are managed by kube-proxy — you cannot inspect them
C) If the pod shows 2/2 Ready, iptables capture is guaranteed to be working
D) Check the application logs — if it receives requests, Envoy is intercepting
✅ Correct Answer: A

iptables is the silent mechanism that captures traffic. If the rules are missing (istio-init failed, or someone flushed iptables), traffic bypasses Envoy entirely — no mTLS, no metrics, no policies. Check the rules and verify Envoy sees connections to confirm capture is working.

📝 Detailed Explanation
iptables Traffic Capture — Verify It's Working
  CHECK IPTABLES RULES (inside pod):
  $ kubectl exec anihpj-web-xxx -n anihpj -c istio-proxy -- \
      iptables -t nat -L -v
  
  Chain ISTIO_OUTPUT:
    RETURN  tcp -- 127.0.0.1          ← Skip localhost
    RETURN  tcp -- uid-owner 1337     ← Skip Envoy's own traffic
    ISTIO_REDIRECT  all -- anywhere    ← Redirect everything else
  
  Chain ISTIO_REDIRECT:
    REDIRECT  tcp --  anywhere  redir ports 15001
  
  VERIFY TRAFFIC IS FLOWING:
  $ istioctl proxy-config stats  | grep "listener.0.0.0.0_15001"
  → listener.0.0.0.0_15001.downstream_cx_total: 15423
  → 15423 connections captured! iptables is working ✅

For anihpj/jobpost, if you suspect traffic is bypassing Envoy (no metrics showing up, mTLS not working despite STRICT policy), check iptables rules and Envoy connection stats. Zero connections on the virtual listener means iptables capture is broken — likely the istio-init container failed silently.

TS-DataPlane Q184 of 200
How do you troubleshoot 503 errors (no healthy upstream) returned by Envoy?
A) 503 errors always mean the backend pod crashed — restart the Deployment
B) 503 errors are normal under load — increase the replica count
C) 503 means the VirtualService has a syntax error — fix the YAML
D) 503 NC (no healthy upstream) means Envoy cannot find a healthy endpoint to route to. Diagnose: 1) istioctl proxy-config cluster <pod> --fqdn <svc> -o json — does the cluster have endpoints? 2) Check if all endpoints are ejected: istioctl proxy-config stats <pod> | grep outlier_detection.ejections. 3) Check if the DestinationRule subset has labels matching any pods. 4) Check kubectl get endpoints <svc> — does the K8s Service have endpoints? 5) Check if mTLS STRICT is blocking plaintext health checks
✅ Correct Answer: D

503 NC = Envoy has a cluster configured but zero healthy endpoints. The cluster exists (config is loaded), but the pods are either missing, ejected, or unreachable. Check the cluster's endpoint list first — it tells you exactly what Envoy sees.

📝 Detailed Explanation
503 NC — No Healthy Upstream Diagnosis
  503 NC DIAGNOSIS (anihpj-web → anihpj-api):
  
  ① CHECK CLUSTER ENDPOINTS:
     $ istioctl proxy-config cluster anihpj-web-xxx -n anihpj \
         --fqdn anihpj-api -o json | jq '.[].endpoints'
     → [] (EMPTY!) ← No healthy endpoints = 503
  
  ② WHY NO ENDPOINTS?
     • Subset label mismatch? v2 subset expects version=v2, pods have app-version=v2
     • All pods ejected? outlier_detection.ejections > 0
     • K8s Service has no endpoints? kubectl get endpoints anihpj-api
     • mTLS STRICT but pod has no sidecar? Plaintext rejected

For anihpj/jobpost, when the job board returns 503 errors, check the cluster endpoint list. The most common cause is a DestinationRule subset with labels that don't match any pods — the v2 canary subset has 0 endpoints. Fix the labels and 503s disappear immediately.

TS-DataPlane Q185 of 200
How do you debug Envoy connection timeouts between services?
A) Connection timeouts are always network issues — check the CNI plugin
B) Increase the timeout in VirtualService — that fixes all timeout problems
C) Check Envoy stats: istioctl proxy-config stats <pod> | grep "upstream_cx_connect_timeout\|upstream_rq_timeout". If connect_timeout is high, Envoy can't establish TCP connections to the backend — check network policies, pod readiness, or DNS. If rq_timeout is high, the backend is responding too slowly — check application performance, increase VirtualService timeout. Also check istioctl proxy-config cluster <pod> --fqdn <svc> -o json for connectTimeout setting
D) Timeout debugging requires application-level profiling — Envoy stats are not helpful
✅ Correct Answer: C

Envoy tracks two timeout types: connect_timeout (TCP handshake failed — network/dns/pod issue) and rq_timeout (HTTP response too slow — application issue). Check which one is elevated to determine whether the problem is network or application.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Envoy Timeout Diagnosis — Connect vs Request
  CHECK ENVOY TIMEOUT STATS:
  $ istioctl proxy-config stats anihpj-web-xxx -n anihpj | \
      grep -E "upstream_cx_connect_timeout|upstream_rq_timeout"
  
  upstream_cx_connect_timeout: 42    ← TCP connect failures (network/DNS)
  upstream_rq_timeout: 3             ← HTTP response too slow (app)
  
  CONNECT TIMEOUT (TCP):
  • Backend pod not ready
  • Network policy blocking
  • DNS resolution failure
  • Backend crashed / no endpoints
  
  REQUEST TIMEOUT (HTTP):
  • Backend slow (DB query taking too long)
  • VirtualService timeout too short
  • Backend overloaded

For anihpj/jobpost, if connect_timeout is high between web and API, check that the API Service has endpoints and network policies allow traffic. If rq_timeout is high, check the API's database query performance — the timeout is happening AFTER the TCP connection was established.

6.2 Data Plane Performance & RecoveryQ186-Q200

TS-DataPlane Q186 of 200
How do you identify which Envoy sidecar is causing latency in a multi-hop request?
A) Check kubectl top pods — the pod with highest CPU is causing the latency
B) Use Jaeger distributed tracing: open a trace for the slow request and examine each span. Each span shows the time spent in that service's Envoy + application. Identify which hop (web→api, api→db) has the highest duration. Then drill into that Envoy's stats: istioctl proxy-config stats <pod> | grep upstream_rq_time to see request time percentiles. The hop with the largest span duration is the bottleneck
C) Check all pod logs for "slow" — the Envoy that logs it is the culprit
D) Latency is always in the application, never in Envoy — check application code
✅ Correct Answer: B

Jaeger traces decompose multi-hop latency into per-hop spans. Each span tells you exactly how long the request spent at each service. If the web→api span is 200ms but the api→db span is 10ms, the bottleneck is in the API, not the database. No guessing needed.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Multi-Hop Latency — Trace-Based Bottleneck Identification
  SLOW REQUEST: anihpj-web → anihpj-api → anihpj-db
  
  JAEGER TRACE:
  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  anihpj-web  │████ (50ms)                                  │
  │  anihpj-api  │    ████████████████████████ (250ms) ⚠️      │
  │  anihpj-db   │       ██ (10ms)                             │
  │                                                             │
  │  TOTAL: 310ms                                               │
  │  BOTTLENECK: anihpj-api (250ms of 310ms = 80%)             │
  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
  
  DRILL INTO anihpj-api ENVOY:
  $ istioctl proxy-config stats anihpj-api-xxx | grep upstream_rq_time
  → upstream_rq_time_p99: 245ms (API → DB is fast, 10ms)
  → The 250ms is spent IN the API application, not waiting for DB

For anihpj/jobpost, when users report slow job searches, open Jaeger to find the bottleneck. The trace shows web(50ms), api(250ms), db(10ms) — the API is the bottleneck. Drilling into the API's Envoy shows the DB call is only 10ms, so the remaining 240ms is in the Django code — time to optimize the search query logic.

TS-DataPlane Q187 of 200
How do you debug when Envoy is not proxying traffic — the sidecar is running but requests fail?
A) 1) Check Envoy health: kubectl exec <pod> -c istio-proxy -- pilot-agent request GET /healthz/ready — returns 200 if Envoy is ready. 2) Check listeners: istioctl proxy-config listeners <pod> — are inbound (15006) and outbound (15001) listeners present? 3) Check iptables: rules must redirect to these ports. 4) Check Envoy logs: kubectl logs <pod> -c istio-proxy for "warning" or "error". 5) Check SDS: istioctl proxy-config secret <pod> — missing certs block mTLS connections under STRICT mode
B) If the sidecar is Running, it's definitely proxying — no further checks needed
C) Delete the pod — a new pod will have a fresh sidecar that works
D) Envoy not proxying always means istiod is down — check the control plane
✅ Correct Answer: A

Running ≠ working. Envoy can be Running but not proxying if: listeners are missing, iptables rules are broken, certificates are expired, or the health check is failing. Check all five signals to confirm the sidecar is actually functional.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Envoy Sidecar — Running vs Actually Working
  CHECKLIST (all must pass):
  
  ☐ HEALTH: pilot-agent request GET /healthz/ready → 200
  ☐ LISTENERS: inbound 15006 + outbound 15001 present
  ☐ IPTABLES: REDIRECT rules point to 15001/15006
  ☐ CERTS: proxy-config secret shows valid certs (not expired)
  ☐ LOGS: no "warning" or "error" in envoy logs
  
  COMMON FAILURES:
  • SDS not responding → no certs → mTLS STRICT blocks all connections
  • iptables flushed → traffic bypasses Envoy → no metrics, no policies
  • Envoy OOM → health check fails → Kubernetes restarts the pod

For anihpj/jobpost, if a pod shows 2/2 Ready but requests fail, run through this checklist. The most common silent failure is expired certificates under STRICT mTLS — Envoy is running and listening, but every connection is rejected because the cert is invalid.

TS-DataPlane Q188 of 200
How do you use Envoy admin interface for advanced debugging — what endpoints are available?
A) The Envoy admin interface is disabled in Istio for security — it's not accessible
B) Only the /stats endpoint is available — other admin endpoints are blocked
C) Use kubectl port-forward to access the admin interface — but only on port 15000
D) Port-forward to the Envoy admin interface (port 15000): kubectl port-forward <pod> 15000:15000. Key endpoints: /stats (all metrics), /config_dump (full config), /clusters (upstream clusters + health), /listeners (active listeners), /certs (TLS certificates), /server_info (version/uptime), /runtime (runtime flags), /logging (change log levels at runtime). This is the deepest level of Envoy introspection — more detail than istioctl proxy-config
✅ Correct Answer: D

The Envoy admin interface is accessible via port-forward. It provides raw access to every aspect of Envoy's internal state — more detailed than istioctl commands. Use it when istioctl proxy-config doesn't give you enough information.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Envoy Admin Interface — Deepest Debugging Level
  ACCESS:
  $ kubectl port-forward anihpj-web-xxx -n anihpj 15000:15000
  $ curl http://localhost:15000/help
  
  KEY ENDPOINTS:
  /stats          → Every Envoy counter, gauge, histogram (thousands)
  /config_dump    → Full Envoy configuration in one JSON dump
  /clusters       → All upstream clusters with health status
  /listeners      → All active listeners with connection counts
  /certs          → All loaded TLS certificates with expiry
  /server_info    → Envoy version, uptime, hot restart info
  /logging        → Dynamically change log levels (POST)
  /runtime        → Runtime feature flags
  /ready          → Health check (returns "LIVE")
  
  POWER USER: Accessing this gives you complete visibility into Envoy.
  Use when istioctl proxy-config doesn't provide enough detail.

For anihpj/jobpost, use the Envoy admin interface for advanced debugging: check /certs to verify all TLS certificates are loaded and not expired, check /clusters to see upstream health in real-time, use POST /logging to increase log verbosity temporarily for debugging a specific issue.

TS-DataPlane Q189 of 200
How do you debug DNS resolution issues inside the mesh — a service cannot resolve another service's hostname?
A) DNS is handled by kube-dns/CoreDNS — Istio has nothing to do with DNS resolution
B) Istio's Envoy intercepts DNS traffic by default (via iptables). Check: 1) istioctl proxy-config listeners <pod> | grep DNS — is the DNS listener on port 15053? 2) kubectl exec <pod> -c istio-proxy -- curl localhost:15053 — is Envoy's DNS proxy responding? 3) Check meshConfig.defaultConfig.proxyMetadata.ISTIO_META_DNS_CAPTURE — is DNS capture enabled? 4) If Envoy's DNS proxy is broken, either fix it or bypass: --set meshConfig.defaultConfig.proxyMetadata.ISTIO_META_DNS_CAPTURE=false
C) DNS resolution issues mean CoreDNS is broken — restart CoreDNS pods
D) Use IP addresses instead of hostnames — Istio doesn't support DNS-based service discovery
✅ Correct Answer: B

Istio's Envoy captures DNS traffic (port 53) and proxies it through a built-in DNS proxy on port 15053. This enables ServiceEntry DNS resolution and mesh-wide DNS policies. If DNS resolution is broken, check that the DNS listener is active and the proxy is responding.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Envoy DNS Proxy — How DNS Works in Istio
  WITHOUT ISTIO:                           WITH ISTIO:
  App → CoreDNS:53 (kube-dns)             App → iptables REDIRECT → Envoy:15053
                                                                │
                                                    ┌───────────┴──────────┐
                                                    │ Envoy DNS Proxy       │
                                                    │ • Mesh services: local│
                                                    │ • ServiceEntry: cached│
                                                    │ • External: forward  │
                                                    │   to CoreDNS         │
                                                    └──────────────────────┘
  
  CHECK DNS CAPTURE:
  $ istioctl proxy-config listeners  | grep 15053
  → 0.0.0.0:15053 (DNS proxy) ← MUST be present
  
  DISABLE DNS CAPTURE (if broken):
  meshConfig.defaultConfig.proxyMetadata.ISTIO_META_DNS_CAPTURE: "false"

For anihpj/jobpost, if the Django application cannot resolve anihpj-api, check that Envoy's DNS proxy listener on 15053 is active. If DNS capture is causing issues, you can disable it and fall back to direct CoreDNS resolution.

TS-DataPlane Q190 of 200
What is the systematic data plane debugging flow for any mesh connectivity issue?
A) Restart all pods — if the issue persists, reinstall Istio from scratch
B) Check Kiali first — if the graph looks correct, there's no connectivity issue
C) Top-to-bottom data plane debug: 1) Verify pod-to-pod connectivity bypassing Envoy (test with iptables bypass). 2) Check Envoy health and listeners (is the sidecar functional?). 3) Check Envoy config (config_dump — does it have the right routes/clusters/endpoints?). 4) Check Envoy stats (connections, errors, timeouts). 5) Check mTLS (certs valid? mode matches?). 6) Check iptables (traffic reaching Envoy?). 7) Check Envoy logs for specific errors. This systematic approach isolates whether the issue is: app → iptables → Envoy listener → Envoy routing → upstream
D) Connectivity issues are always control plane problems — check istiod first
✅ Correct Answer: C

Systematic data plane debugging follows the traffic path: app → iptables → Envoy listener → Envoy routing → upstream. Each hop is a potential failure point. Test each link in the chain to isolate where traffic stops flowing.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Data Plane Debugging Flow — Follow the Traffic
  TRAFFIC PATH (each arrow = potential failure):
  App ──► iptables ──► Envoy:15001 ──► Envoy routing ──► upstream pod
  ①         ②             ③               ④                ⑤
  
  ① App: Is the application sending requests? (check app logs)
  ② iptables: Are REDIRECT rules present? (iptables -t nat -L)
  ③ Envoy listener: Is 15001 active? (proxy-config listeners)
  ④ Envoy routing: Correct routes/clusters/endpoints? (config_dump)
  ⑤ Upstream: Is the destination pod healthy? (kubectl get endpoints)
  
  SYSTEMATIC = check each hop, don't skip, don't guess.

For anihpj/jobpost, when web→api communication breaks, follow this flow: check that Django is sending requests (①), verify iptables redirects to Envoy (②), confirm Envoy is listening on 15001 (③), check that Envoy has the correct route to anihpj-api (④), verify the API pod is healthy and in the endpoint list (⑤). This pinpoints the exact failure point in under 2 minutes.

TS-DataPlane Q191 of 200
How do you troubleshoot Envoy OOM (Out of Memory) issues — the istio-proxy container keeps getting killed?
A) OOM is normal for Envoy — just increase the memory limit until it stops
B) Envoy OOM always means a memory leak in the Envoy binary — upgrade to the latest Istio version
C) OOM means the application is sending too much traffic — reduce the application's request rate
D) Diagnose: 1) Check kubectl describe pod <pod> | grep OOMKilled to confirm. 2) Check cluster count: istioctl proxy-config clusters <pod> | wc -l — too many clusters = too many services. 3) Deploy Sidecar resource to scope Envoy config. 4) Check endpoint count: large meshes without scoping cause memory explosion. 5) Increase memory limit as temporary fix, deploy Sidecar resource as permanent fix. 6) Check for config loops: misconfigured VirtualServices can cause Envoy to load excessive routes
✅ Correct Answer: D

Envoy OOM is almost always caused by too many clusters/endpoints — the Envoy is tracking every service in a large mesh. The Sidecar resource is the fix: limit what Envoy knows about. Increasing memory limits is a band-aid; scoping is the cure.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Envoy OOM — Root Cause and Fix
  DIAGNOSE:
  $ kubectl describe pod anihpj-web-xxx -n anihpj | grep OOMKilled
  → OOMKilled (exit code 137) — Envoy ran out of memory
  
  $ istioctl proxy-config clusters anihpj-web-xxx -n anihpj | wc -l
  → 487 clusters (tracking EVERY service in a 500-service mesh!)
  
  FIX — Sidecar Resource:
  apiVersion: networking.istio.io/v1beta1
  kind: Sidecar
  metadata: {name: web-scope, namespace: anihpj}
  spec:
    egress:
    - hosts: [anihpj/*, istio-system/*]
    workloadSelector: {labels: {app: anihpj-web}}
  
  AFTER: 12 clusters → memory drops from 500MB to ~40MB ✅

For anihpj/jobpost, deploy Sidecar resources for every workload. The anihpj-web Envoy only needs to know about anihpj-api and istiod — not all the monitoring, logging, and CI/CD services in the cluster. This prevents OOM kills and keeps the job board stable.

TS-DataPlane Q192 of 200
How does Envoy hot restart work and how does it prevent dropped connections during sidecar updates?
A) Hot restart means Kubernetes replaces the pod — connections drop but reconnect automatically
B) Envoy's hot restart feature allows a NEW Envoy process to take over from an OLD one without dropping existing connections. The old Envoy passes listening sockets and connection state to the new Envoy via a shared Unix domain socket. During sidecar updates (e.g., rolling restart), existing TCP connections are drained gracefully while new connections go to the new Envoy — zero-downtime proxy updates
C) Hot restart requires a separate sidecar container — it doesn't work with a single Envoy process
D) Hot restart is only for ingress gateways — sidecar proxies cannot hot restart
✅ Correct Answer: B

Envoy's hot restart is what enables zero-downtime sidecar updates. The old Envoy drains existing connections while the new one takes over — no connection drops, no 503 errors. This is transparent to the application and users.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Envoy Hot Restart — Zero-Downtime Proxy Updates
  DURING SIDECAR RESTART (rolling update):
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  OLD Envoy (draining)        NEW Envoy (active)                 │
  │  ┌──────────────────┐       ┌──────────────────┐               │
  │  │ Existing conns:   │       │ New conns:        │               │
  │  │ ████████ (drain)  │       │ ████████████████  │               │
  │  │ Passes sockets →  │──────►│ Takes over        │               │
  │  │ Passes stats →    │       │                   │               │
  │  └──────────────────┘       └──────────────────┘               │
  │                                                                  │
  │  RESULT: Zero connection drops during Envoy restart             │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, Envoy hot restart means rolling restart of the API pods (for Istio upgrades or config changes) does not drop active job application submissions. Users submitting forms during the restart don't get 503 errors — Envoy seamlessly hands off connections.

TS-DataPlane Q193 of 200
How do you troubleshoot TCP connection issues — anihpj-api cannot connect to anihpj-db (PostgreSQL) through the mesh?
A) TCP diagnostics: 1) kubectl exec anihpj-api-xxx -n anihpj -c istio-proxy -- nc -zv anihpj-db 5432 — can Envoy reach the DB? 2) istioctl proxy-config listeners anihpj-api-xxx | grep 5432 — is TCP proxy listening? 3) istioctl proxy-config cluster anihpj-api-xxx --fqdn anihpj-db -o json — does the TCP cluster exist and have endpoints? 4) Check K8s Service port naming: MUST start with "tcp-" prefix for TCP protocol detection. 5) Check PeerAuthentication: STRICT mTLS requires both sides to have valid certs. 6) Check AuthorizationPolicy: is the API SA allowed to access port 5432?
B) TCP issues are always network problems — check the CNI plugin and node networking
C) PostgreSQL connections bypass Envoy by default — TCP is not proxied through the mesh
D) Database connections require a separate TCP gateway — sidecar proxies only handle HTTP
✅ Correct Answer: A

TCP troubleshooting requires checking: Envoy TCP listener (is port 5432 being proxied?), TCP cluster (does it have endpoints?), port naming (must be tcp-postgres, not postgres), and authorization (is the API allowed?). The K8s Service port name is critical — wrong prefix = Envoy treats it as plain TCP without mTLS.

📝 Detailed Explanation
TCP Connectivity — PostgreSQL Through the Mesh
  CHECKLIST:
  ☐ K8s Service port name: MUST be "tcp-postgres" (NOT "postgres" or "db")
  ☐ Envoy listener: port 5432 active? (proxy-config listeners)
  ☐ TCP cluster: has endpoints? (proxy-config cluster --fqdn anihpj-db)
  ☐ PeerAuthentication: mode matches (PERMISSIVE or STRICT with both sidecars)
  ☐ AuthorizationPolicy: API SA allowed? principals: [.../sa/anihpj-api]
  ☐ Network connectivity: nc -zv anihpj-db 5432 from API pod
  
  #1 ISSUE: Service port named "postgres" instead of "tcp-postgres"
  → Envoy treats it as plain TCP, doesn't apply mTLS → connection fails under STRICT

For anihpj/jobpost, the #1 PostgreSQL connectivity issue is the K8s Service port name. Always name it tcp-postgres (protocol prefix required). Without this, Envoy doesn't know to apply mTLS and the connection fails under STRICT mode.

TS-DataPlane Q194 of 200
How do you debug WebSocket connections through Istio — real-time connections dropping or not establishing?
A) WebSocket connections are automatically upgraded by Envoy — no special configuration needed
B) WebSockets require EnvoyFilter — VirtualService cannot handle WebSocket routing
C) Envoy supports WebSocket transparently — the HTTP connection is upgraded to WebSocket automatically. Troubleshoot: 1) Check if the VirtualService has a timeout (timeout: 0 is needed for long-lived WebSocket connections — never timeout). 2) Check istioctl proxy-config stats <pod> | grep "upstream_cx" for connection drops. 3) Check if idle timeout is killing connections (meshConfig.defaultConfig.proxyMetadata.IDLE_TIMEOUT). 4) Verify the VirtualService route is correct — WebSocket requests have Upgrade and Connection headers
D) WebSocket debugging requires application-level tools — Envoy cannot help with WebSocket issues
✅ Correct Answer: C

The #1 WebSocket issue in Istio is VirtualService timeout. If you set timeout: 30s, all WebSocket connections are killed after 30 seconds. Set timeout: 0s (infinite) for WebSocket routes. Also check idle timeout settings in MeshConfig.

📝 Detailed Explanation
WebSocket Through Envoy — Timeout Is the Enemy
  VirtualService for WebSocket:
  http:
  - match:
    - uri: {prefix: /ws}
    route:
    - destination: {host: anihpj-api}
    timeout: 0s          ← CRITICAL: 0s = no timeout (WebSocket is long-lived)
  
  IDLE TIMEOUT (MeshConfig):
  meshConfig:
    defaultConfig:
      proxyMetadata:
        IDLE_TIMEOUT: 3600s   ← Default 1h, increase for long-lived WS
  
  ⚠️  timeout: 30s kills WebSocket every 30 seconds (default = 0s = no timeout)

For anihpj/jobpost, if real-time job notifications use WebSockets, ensure the VirtualService has timeout: 0s and the MeshConfig idle timeout is set high enough (at least 1 hour). A non-zero timeout on the WebSocket route causes connections to drop at exactly that interval — very confusing to debug.

TS-DataPlane Q195 of 200
How do you use Envoy access logs for data plane debugging — tracing a specific failed request through the mesh?
A) Access logs only show successful requests — failed requests are not logged
B) Use kubectl logs on the application container — Envoy access logs are too verbose to be useful
C) Access logs require a separate Elasticsearch cluster — you cannot view them directly
D) Enable JSON access logs with meshConfig.accessLogEncoding: JSON. Each Envoy (source AND destination) logs every request with: method, path, response code, duration, upstream host, TLS info, SPIFFE principal, and response flags. For a failed request: check BOTH the source and destination Envoy logs. Response flags like "UC" (upstream connection failure) or "UT" (upstream timeout) tell you exactly why the request failed
✅ Correct Answer: D

JSON access logs from BOTH Envoys give you the complete picture of any failed request. Response flags are the key — they tell you EXACTLY why Envoy rejected or failed the request: UC (upstream connection failure), UT (timeout), UO (upstream overflow), URX (retry limit exceeded).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Access Log Response Flags — Decode the Failure
  KEY RESPONSE FLAGS:
  UC = Upstream Connection failure (backend unreachable)
  UT = Upstream Timeout (backend too slow)
  UO = Upstream Overflow (circuit breaker open)
  URX = Upstream Retry limit eXceeded (all retries failed)
  DC = Downstream Connection termination (client disconnected)
  
  DEBUGGING A FAILED REQUEST:
  $ kubectl logs anihpj-web-xxx -c istio-proxy | grep "POST /api/jobs"
  → {"method":"POST","path":"/api/jobs","response_code":503,"response_flags":"UO",...}
  
  UO = Upstream Overflow → Circuit breaker ejected all API pods!
  → Check outlier detection and API pod health.

For anihpj/jobpost, enable JSON access logs and learn the response flags. When a user reports a failed job application submission, grep the access logs for their request and check the response flags. "UO" means circuit breakers fired — API pods were ejected. "UT" means the API timed out. The flags tell you what to fix.

TS-DataPlane Q196 of 200
How do you troubleshoot circuit breaker ejection from the data plane — verify that Envoy is actually ejecting pods and why?
A) Circuit breaker status is only visible in Kiali — Envoy stats cannot show ejection details
B) Check Envoy stats: istioctl proxy-config stats <pod> | grep outlier_detection shows ejection counts, ejection duration, and success rate. ejections_consecutive_5xx = pods ejected for consecutive 5xx errors. ejections_active = current number of ejected pods. ejections_overflow = pods not ejected because maxEjectionPercent would be exceeded. Also check istioctl proxy-config cluster <pod> --fqdn <svc> -o json for per-endpoint health status
C) Circuit breaker ejection is a control plane feature — check istiod logs
D) Ejection details require enabling debug logging on Envoy — stats don't show ejection status
✅ Correct Answer: B

Envoy stats reveal everything about circuit breaker state. ejection_consecutive_5xx tells you pods were ejected for errors. ejections_active tells you how many are currently out. ejections_overflow tells you if maxEjectionPercent prevented more ejections. These stats are per-Envoy — each sidecar maintains its own ejection list.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Circuit Breaker Ejection — Envoy Stats Deep Dive
  $ istioctl proxy-config stats anihpj-web-xxx -n anihpj | \
      grep outlier_detection
  
  outlier_detection.ejections_consecutive_5xx: 12    ← 12 ejections total
  outlier_detection.ejections_active: 2              ← 2 pods currently ejected
  outlier_detection.ejections_overflow: 3            ← 3 pods NOT ejected (max % hit)
  outlier_detection.ejections_enforced_total: 15     ← Total enforcement actions
  
  PER-ENDPOINT HEALTH:
  $ istioctl proxy-config cluster  --fqdn anihpj-api -o json | \
      jq '.[].endpoints[] | {address: .address, healthy: .healthStatus}'
  → Shows which specific pods are ejected and why

For anihpj/jobpost, when the job board shows degraded performance, check outlier_detection stats on the web and API Envoys. High ejections_active means pods are being ejected — find out why (5xx errors? timeouts?) and fix the root cause, not just the circuit breaker symptoms.

TS-DataPlane Q197 of 200
How do you troubleshoot when Envoy doesn't start — the istio-proxy container shows CrashLoopBackOff or Init:Error?
A) 1) Check kubectl logs <pod> -c istio-proxy for startup errors. 2) Check kubectl logs <pod> -c istio-init — did the init container complete? 3) Check kubectl describe pod <pod> for events (OOM, image pull failure). 4) Check SDS connectivity: Envoy can't start without certificates from istiod. 5) Check istiod health: if istiod is down, new Envoys can't get bootstrap config. Common causes: missing NET_ADMIN capability (use istio-cni), corrupted bootstrap config, certificate issues, resource limits too low
B) Envoy not starting always means a bad container image — repull the image and retry
C) Delete the pod — Kubernetes will recreate it with a working sidecar eventually
D) Envoy startup issues are always caused by istiod being down — check the control plane
✅ Correct Answer: A

Envoy startup failures have multiple causes. Check the init container first (iptables setup), then Envoy logs (bootstrap errors), then istiod connectivity (can Envoy reach istiod for initial config?). The #1 cause in hardened clusters is missing NET_ADMIN — the init container fails silently.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Envoy Startup Failure — Diagnostic Checklist
  CHECK SEQUENCE:
  ① kubectl logs  -c istio-init → Did iptables setup succeed?
     → "iptables: Permission denied" = missing NET_ADMIN → install istio-cni
  
  ② kubectl logs  -c istio-proxy → Bootstrap errors?
     → "Failed to fetch certificate" = can't reach istiod
     → "Invalid configuration" = bad proxy config
  
  ③ kubectl describe pod  → OOMKilled? (increase memory)
     → ImagePullBackOff? (wrong image registry)
  
  ④ Check istiod: kubectl get pods -n istio-system | grep istiod
     → Down? Envoy can't bootstrap without control plane

For anihpj/jobpost on kubeadm, if new pods show Init:Error, check the istio-init container logs. On PSA Restricted clusters, the NET_ADMIN capability is blocked — install istio-cni to handle iptables at the node level instead of per-pod.

TS-DataPlane Q198 of 200
How do you debug gRPC streaming issues through the Istio mesh — streams dropping or not connecting?
A) gRPC streams are not supported through Envoy — use REST instead
B) gRPC debugging requires application-level tools — Envoy is transparent to gRPC
C) Envoy supports HTTP/2 (gRPC's transport) natively. Debug: 1) Check istioctl proxy-config stats <pod> | grep http2 for stream counts and errors. 2) Check DestinationRule connection pool: http2MaxRequests limits concurrent streams — if exceeded, new streams are rejected. 3) Check VirtualService timeout: streaming RPCs are long-lived — use timeout: 0s. 4) Check istioctl proxy-config listeners <pod> for HTTP connection manager with http2_options. 5) gRPC retry policies differ from HTTP — use gRPC-specific retryOn conditions
D) gRPC streaming issues are always network problems — check node-to-node connectivity
✅ Correct Answer: C

gRPC uses HTTP/2, which Envoy handles natively. The #1 gRPC issue is http2MaxRequests limiting concurrent streams — when the limit is hit, new streams are rejected. Also, VirtualService timeout kills long-lived streaming RPCs. Set timeout: 0s and increase http2MaxRequests for streaming workloads.

📝 Detailed Explanation
gRPC Streaming — HTTP/2 Configuration
  DestinationRule for gRPC:
  trafficPolicy:
    connectionPool:
      http:
        http2MaxRequests: 1000     ← Concurrent stream limit
        maxRequestsPerConnection: 0  ← No limit per connection
  
  VirtualService for gRPC:
  http:
  - timeout: 0s                    ← CRITICAL: no timeout for streams
    retries:
      retryOn: "cancelled,deadline-exceeded,unavailable,internal"
  
  DEBUG:
  $ istioctl proxy-config stats  | grep http2
  → http2.tx_reset: 42  ← Stream resets — check limit or timeout

For anihpj/jobpost, if gRPC streaming for real-time job updates drops connections, check http2MaxRequests and VirtualService timeout. Increase the stream limit and set timeout to 0s for streaming endpoints. Also use gRPC-specific retryOn conditions (unavailable, not 5xx).

TS-DataPlane Q199 of 200
How do you troubleshoot when Envoy proxy-config commands return empty — istioctl can't retrieve config from a sidecar?
A) Empty output means Envoy has no configuration — reinstall Istio on that pod
B) 1) Check istioctl proxy-status — is the proxy listed? If not, Envoy never connected to istiod. 2) Check Envoy admin interface: kubectl exec <pod> -c istio-proxy -- pilot-agent request GET /config_dump — does Envoy itself have config? 3) If Envoy has config but istioctl can't retrieve it, the issue is istioctl→istiod connectivity (istioctl uses port-forward to istiod, which queries Envoy). 4) Check if istiod is running and the proxy's xDS stream is healthy. 5) If Envoy has NO config, it couldn't bootstrap from istiod — check startup logs
C) Empty proxy-config output is normal for pods without sidecars — check if injection worked
D) Use kubectl exec to access the Envoy admin interface directly — istioctl is unnecessary
✅ Correct Answer: B

istioctl proxy-config works by port-forwarding to istiod, which then queries the Envoy via its xDS stream. If the proxy-status doesn't list the Envoy, it never connected to istiod. If it IS listed but config is empty, the xDS stream is broken or Envoy didn't bootstrap properly.

📝 Detailed Explanation
proxy-config Empty — Diagnostic Flow
  istioctl proxy-config routes  -n anihpj → (empty)
  
  ① istioctl proxy-status → Is the pod listed?
     → NOT LISTED: Envoy never connected to istiod (check startup logs)
     → LISTED but STALE: xDS stream broken (restart pod)
     → LISTED and SYNCED: Envoy has config but istioctl can't retrieve it
  
  ② Check Envoy directly:
     kubectl exec  -c istio-proxy -- pilot-agent request GET /config_dump
     → Has config? Issue is istioctl/istiod connectivity
     → No config? Envoy bootstrap failed
  
  ③ Check istiod:
     kubectl logs -n istio-system deploy/istiod | grep "ads\|xds"

For anihpj/jobpost, if proxy-config returns empty, first check proxy-status. If the pod isn't listed, the Envoy never connected to istiod — check Envoy startup logs for bootstrap errors. If it IS listed, query the Envoy admin interface directly to bypass istioctl and confirm the Envoy itself has configuration.

TS-DataPlane Q200 of 200
You have completed all 200 questions. What is the complete troubleshooting mindset that distinguishes an expert Istio engineer when debugging ANY mesh issue?
A) Always restart the affected pods first — most issues are transient and resolve on restart
B) Check Kiali first, then Grafana, then Prometheus — observability tools always reveal the root cause
C) Reinstall Istio if the issue persists for more than 10 minutes — a clean mesh is a healthy mesh
D) Systematic, layered diagnosis: 1) Isolate the failure domain (control plane? data plane? configuration? network?). 2) Follow the traffic path (source app → iptables → source Envoy → network → destination Envoy → destination app). 3) Verify each layer independently (config correct? Envoy has config? certs valid? endpoints exist? policies allow?). 4) Use the right tool for each layer: proxy-status for sync, config_dump for routing, stats for counters, traces for latency, access logs for per-request detail. 5) Never guess — test each hypothesis. 6) Document findings and fix the root cause, not the symptom. This methodology solves ANY mesh issue in minutes, not hours
✅ Correct Answer: D

The expert mindset is systematic, not reactive. Don't restart pods hoping it fixes things. Don't reinstall Istio. Follow the traffic, check each layer, use the right diagnostic tool for each layer, and fix the root cause. This methodology works for every mesh issue, from 503s to mTLS failures to performance degradation.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Expert Troubleshooting Mindset — Systematic, Layered Diagnosis
  THE EXPERT METHOD:
  
  ① ISOLATE: Which domain is affected?
     Control Plane → check istiod, proxy-status
     Data Plane → check Envoy, config_dump, iptables
     Configuration → check istioctl analyze, VS/DR YAML
     Network → check endpoints, DNS, connectivity
  
  ② FOLLOW THE TRAFFIC (each hop):
     App → iptables → Envoy:15001 → Envoy routing → Upstream pod
     Check each hop — where does the failure occur?
  
  ③ RIGHT TOOL FOR EACH LAYER:
     proxy-status = sync health
     config_dump = what Envoy ACTUALLY has
     proxy-config stats = counters and errors
     Jaeger traces = per-hop latency
     Access logs = per-request forensic detail
  
  ④ NEVER GUESS — test each hypothesis systematically
  ⑤ FIX ROOT CAUSE — don't just restart and hope
  
  This methodology solves any Istio issue in minutes.

For anihpj/jobpost, this is the methodology that takes you from novice to expert. When the job board goes down at 3 AM, you don't panic and restart pods. You isolate the domain, follow the traffic, use the right diagnostic tool, identify the root cause, and fix it — all in under 10 minutes. This is what 200 MCQs, 100 Issues, and 100 Scenarios have trained you to do.

🔧 Part 2: 100 Security & Troubleshooting Issues

Real-world Istio service mesh failure and security scenarios — each with symptom, 15 categorized causes (5 most likely + 5 less likely + 5 new cluster), diagnostic commands, solutions, and personal mesh advice. Patterned after the 5+5+5 cause-card format.

6Categories

📦 Category 1: Install, Upgrade & Configuration Troubleshooting (20 Issues)IC1-IC20

🟢 Installation & Configuration Issues — Sidecar Injection, Profiles, Upgrades

Installation and configuration issues are the FIRST problems you'll encounter when setting up Istio. The mesh depends on correct sidecar injection, proper namespace labeling, and valid configuration. When any link in this chain fails, your pods either don't join the mesh or behave unexpectedly.

📌 IC1–IC5: Sidecar Injection Failures

IC1
📦 CATEGORY 1: INSTALL/UPGRADE/CONFIG — Issue IC1
Sidecar Not Injected — Pod Shows 1/1 Instead of 2/2

🔍 Symptom: You deployed the anihpj/jobpost application in a namespace that should have Istio sidecar injection, but kubectl get pods -n anihpj shows 1/1 READY instead of the expected 2/2. The Envoy sidecar (istio-proxy container) was never added to the pod. mTLS is not working, metrics are not appearing in Prometheus, and Kiali shows the service outside the mesh.

  kubectl get pods -n anihpj
  NAME                           READY   STATUS    RESTARTS   AGE
  anihpj-web-7d8f9-x             1/1     Running   0          5m
  anihpj-api-5c9b7-y             1/1     Running   0          5m
  anihpj-db-0                    1/1     Running   0          5m

  kubectl get ns anihpj --show-labels
  NAME     STATUS   AGE   LABELS
  anihpj   Active   10m   kubernetes.io/metadata.name=anihpj
                           ← MISSING: istio-injection=enabled!

  kubectl describe pod anihpj-web-7d8f9-x -n anihpj | grep -A2 Containers
  Containers:
    anihpj-web:   (only 1 container — no istio-proxy!)
🔴5 Most Likely Causes
  1. Namespace not labeled: The istio-injection=enabled label is missing from the namespace. Run kubectl get ns <ns> --show-labels. Fix: kubectl label ns <ns> istio-injection=enabled. Existing pods must be restarted — the label only affects NEW pods.
  2. Pod annotation override: The pod has sidecar.istio.io/inject: "false" in its annotations, which overrides the namespace label. Check: kubectl get pod <pod> -o yaml | grep sidecar.istio.io/inject.
  3. istiod not running: The injection webhook (istio-sidecar-injector) depends on istiod being healthy. Check: kubectl get pods -n istio-system | grep istiod. If istiod is CrashLoopBackOff, the webhook can't process injection requests.
  4. MutatingWebhookConfiguration missing: The istio-sidecar-injector webhook was deleted or never installed. Check: kubectl get mutatingwebhookconfiguration | grep istio. Reinstall: istioctl install --set profile=default.
  5. Existing pods not restarted after labeling: The namespace label was added AFTER pods were created. Only pods created/restarted AFTER the label is applied get sidecars. Fix: kubectl rollout restart deploy -n <ns>.
🟡5 Less Likely Causes
  1. istio-system namespace not found — Istio was never installed or was uninstalled
  2. NetworkPolicy blocking API server → istiod communication (webhook traffic on port 15017)
  3. istiod has insufficient resources and webhook calls timeout — increase istiod CPU/memory limits
  4. Injection template in istio-sidecar-injector ConfigMap is corrupted — someone edited it incorrectly
  5. Custom admission controller ordering — another MutatingWebhook runs first and conflicts with Istio's injection
🟣5 New Cluster Causes
  1. PSA Restricted profile enforced: The namespace or cluster enforces Pod Security Admission Restricted, which blocks the NET_ADMIN capability needed by istio-init. Fix: install istio-cni plugin instead (istioctl install --set components.cni.enabled=true).
  2. Istio not fully installed: istioctl install was interrupted or failed mid-way. Run istioctl verify-install to check all components.
  3. Revision mismatch: Namespace is labeled with istio.io/rev=<rev> but istiod for that revision is not deployed (e.g., after a failed canary upgrade).
  4. kubeadm cluster without default PSP: Older kubeadm setups without PodSecurityPolicy may block the istio-init container's privileged operations.
  5. istiod Service not reachable from nodes: The istiod.istio-system.svc DNS must resolve on every node for the webhook to call back to istiod.
🔍 What to Look At:
kubectl get ns <ns> --show-labels | grep istio-injection The single most common cause: missing namespace label. Always check this FIRST.
kubectl get mutatingwebhookconfiguration istio-sidecar-injector -o yaml | grep -A5 namespaceSelector Verify the webhook's namespace selector matches your namespace labels.
kubectl logs -n istio-system deploy/istiod --tail=50 | grep -i "inject\|webhook" istiod logs reveal why injection was skipped for specific pods.
🎯 #1 Root Cause: Namespace is not labeled istio-injection=enabled, or existing pods were created before the label was applied and haven't been restarted. The MutatingWebhook only fires at POD CREATION time — existing pods must be recreated to trigger injection.
🔧 How to Solve:
1. Label the namespace for injection kubectl label namespace anihpj istio-injection=enabled This enables injection for all FUTURE pods in the namespace.
2. Restart existing Deployments/StatefulSets kubectl rollout restart deploy -n anihpj Existing pods won't get sidecars until recreated. Rolling restart creates new pods that trigger the injection webhook.
3. Verify sidecar injection worked kubectl get pods -n anihpj All pods should now show 2/2 READY. The second container is istio-proxy (Envoy).
4. Verify Envoys connected to istiod istioctl proxy-status All anihpj pods should show SYNCED across CDS, LDS, EDS, RDS.
5. For PSA Restricted clusters, use istio-cni istioctl install --set components.cni.enabled=true --set profile=default This handles traffic capture at the node level (DaemonSet) without requiring NET_ADMIN on pods.
💡 Personal Mesh Advice:
Label early, restart always. Label the namespace BEFORE deploying workloads. If you label after, always run kubectl rollout restart deploy -n <ns>. The injection webhook ONLY fires at pod creation time — never on running pods.
Check READY count as your first diagnostic. kubectl get pods -n <ns> 2/2 = sidecar injected and healthy. 1/2 = sidecar injected but not ready (check istio-proxy logs). 1/1 = NO sidecar injected at all (label issue). This single number tells you 90% of the injection story.
istio-cni is becoming the default for production. Newer Istio versions default to istio-cni for good reason: it eliminates the NET_ADMIN capability requirement and works with PSA Restricted. If you're starting fresh, install with --set components.cni.enabled=true.
Verify the webhook exists before troubleshooting injection. kubectl get mutatingwebhookconfiguration istio-sidecar-injector If this is missing, no pods in any namespace will get sidecars — regardless of labels. This happens after istioctl uninstall or partial installations.
